                Dedication

To the pioneers who gave birth to a dream, who had the foresight and
fortitude to pursue that dream and make it a reality, and to those who
followed, persevered, and rejoiced, through the trials, hardships and
successes of their time, to preserve that dream -- we humbly dedicate this
book in their honor.

A Word From The Mayor

The City of Roscoe is celebrating its 100th birthday during 1983.  A
heartwarming "Thank You" goes out to those who settled the untamed land,
and thought enough of this community to keep it going when times were hard.

We can look back over the last 100 years as a time of achievement and
betterment, and look forward to the next 100 years as a time of growth and
development.

On behalf of the community, I would like to thank everyone who has helped
with this celebration.  To those who have worked on the various centennial
committees for many hours, thank you for your time and trouble.  The
celebration would not have been the same without your efforts.  And most of
all, to the visitors and former residents of Roscoe.  "Welcome Home!" May
your visit be pleasant and your remembrances be bright!

Leland N. Treichel Mayor of Roscoe

HAPPY BIRTHDAY, ROSCOE!!

        Advertisement taken from the December 6, 1883 issue of The Dakota
        Pioneer, an Aberdeen publication.  At this time it is believed the
        Cottonade Hotel was a wooden structure and believed to be located in
        the area where the present Henry Trefz home is located.

Town of Roscoe, S. D., in July of 1883

        The Cottonade Hotel, a large tent "hotel", put up by Charles P. Morgan,
        the founder of Roscoe, on his arrival here in April 1883. This was the
        half-way place between Aberdeen and the Missouri river. it provided
        food and shelter for both man and beast until the fall when better
        accommodations were built.  A lantern was placed on a high pole at
        night to guide many weary travelers to this haven of rest on the Dakota
        prairie. The women in the picture are Mr. Morgan's mother and niece who
        were pre-emptors and had come to get their mail which was carried by
        horse stage from Aberdeen and LeBeau.

Foreword

        One hundred years of existence ! ! What an awesome number of years to
        try to remember ---- and to reveal the treasures of our triumphs and our
        failures.  We, the Roscoe Centennial Book Committee, have attempted to
        undertake what proved to be a mammoth task.  We have tried to uncover
        the long forgotten facts and tales surrounding the "life" of Roscoe.
        We have probed the minds of those among us who might recall things as
        they were "in the good old days", or may remember the tales of Roscoe
        in its' younger years, as told by their parents and grandparents. We
        have searched through endless issues of newspapers from the past.  We
        have pored over all the official records we could lay our hands on.  We
        have called on you, the people of the Roscoe Community, to supply us
        with your histories and pictures of the past and present.  Your
        response has been overwhelming!

        Now, as a result of your efforts and ours, you will see the "fruits of
        our labors" between these covers.  We sincerely hope that it pleases
        you.  We realize that in spite of our combined efforts, there will be
        errors and omissions, but none that are intentional.

        We must give Thanks, to everyone on the committee who worked so hard
        and gave so generously of their time, to you the people, who supplied
        so much information and allowed us the use of your pictures, to our
        advertisers and sponsors, and to the Book Committee of the Diamond
        Jubilee in 1958, who supplied the ground work for us.

        We humbly offer to you---the people---

ROSCOE -- THE FIRST 100 YEARS

May our next 100 years be as productive!!

        Roscoe, Dakota Territory, 1885. Looking West from site of present
        overpass on Highway # 12.

VIRGINIA GOETZ CREATES WINNING CENTENNIAL EMBLEM
The Roscoe Centennial Committee selected the emblem for the Centennial by
running a contest for all Roscoe public students.  The contest dates were from
March 17, 1981 to April 20, 1981.  The emblem was to depict a simple
representation of Roscoe's heritage which could be used on buttons, banners,
cops, etc. There were four prizes awarded: $25, $15, $10, and $5. Virginia
Goetz drew the winning emblem.  The second place winner was Bo Beck, third
place, Michelle Goetz, and fourth place, Eugene Thomas.

CENTENNIAL QUILT
Viola and Otto Mohr presented a Centennial Quilt that they had made, to the
Roscoe Commercial Club at their August 1982 meeting.  The purpose of the
presentation was for chances to be sold on the Quilt and the proceeds to go
into the Centennial Fund.

The two large pictures in the center of the quilt show the designs from the
Centennial Emblem and the Centennial Jacket.  The small blocks are each pointed
with a picture depicting some scene from the "good old days".

This was certainly a very generous gesture and very much appreciated by the
entire community.  Thank you, Otto and Viola!

        Virginia Goetz

        Viola and Otto Mohr, Dale Hettick, Commercial Club President and Jerome
        Fischer, Centennial General Chairman.

                ROSCOE 1883 - 1983

        Roscoe about 1912

The county of Edmunds was named for Newton Edmunds who was appointed as the
second Governor of Dakota Territory by Abraham Lincoln.  Edmunds replaced
Governor William Jayne and served as Governor from 1863 to 1866.

Edmunds County was created in 1873 and organized in 1883.  The first settlement
in Edmunds County, (on October 16, 1882) was made by Ezra Drew, Ira Dibble, and
their families.  Mr. Drew homesteaded on the southeast quarter of section 3 and
the northwest quarter of section 11, township 122, range 68.

Ezra Drew and Ira Dibble settled in Edmunds County while the boundaries were
set at range 67 to 73.  In 1885, the Territorial Legislature changed the
boundaries of the county.  Range 66 was annexed, making the county 48 miles
long by 24 miles wide.  The first homestead entry into range 66 was made by
Milton Bliss on October 2, 1882.  The first Pre-emption filing was made by John
Ackley on September 20, 1882.

The Homestead Act was passed by Congress in May of 1862.  This act provided
that any person over 21, who was the head of a family, and either a citizen or
an alien who intended to become a citizen, could obtain the title to 160 acres
of public land if he lived on the land for five years and improved it.  Or, the
settler could pay $1.25 an acre in place of the residence requirement.  This
act attracted thousands of settlers to the west.  From 1862 to 1900, it
provided farms and new homes for between 400,000 and 600,000 families.

        Main Street of Roscoe in 1908

        East side Main Street of Roscoe in 1910

The first homestead was granted to Daniel Freeman January 1, 1863 near
Beatrice, Nebraska.  People then began to move into the west including the
Dakota Territory.  Edmunds County and Roscoe grew as a result of the Homestead
Act.  Though the first settlement of the present day, Edmunds County did not
take place until 20 years after the act was passed, people moved in rapidly
once the first settlement was made.

Sam Basford was the first to locate in the area which soon become Roscoe.
Basford settled on the southeast quarter, Section 24, Township 123, Range 71,
where he built a small shanty.  This took place on or about the first day of
April, 1883.

        Main street 1909

Basford returned to Aberdeen in mid-April and met Charles Purchase Morgan who
was on his way west from Aberdeen with a large tent.  This tent was place on.
the present site of Roscoe April 15 to be used as a hotel.  The actual location
of this tent is not certain.  It has been indicated that the location of the
tent hotel was on the site of the St. Paul's Lutheran Church or on Block 15 of
Morgan's Addition which is the second block east of the school.  It was named
the Cottonade Hotel and served as a shelter for people traveling from Aberdeen
to LeBeau.

Joseph W. Parmley arrived at the site of the tent the day after Morgan arrived.
Parmley and Morgan become fast friends and the two were responsible for giving
Roscoe its name.

Morgan's father, Allen Denison Morgan, served in the New York legislature from
1860-61.  The elder Morgan and Roscoe Conkling become good friends and their
close relationship led Charles Morgan to name the town Roscoe after his
fathers' friend.

The story of Roscoe, however, has its beginnings in the year 1821.  Mexico
gained its independence from Spain by the end of that year.  Mexico, at that
time, included the present day state of California.  Land grants were issued in
the California area by the Mexican government. Juan Miranda received one of
these grants of land and named it Rancho Arroyo De San Antonio. According to
the Historical Atlas Map of Sonoma County, California dated 1877, "Juan Miranda
first settled there about the year 1838, with his family, horses and cattle and
built a small house, about two miles distant from the present city of Petaluma."

In 1844 he applied for a grant of this land.  Jacob P. Leese, then alcalde or
justice of the district of Sonoma, certified that he was the only occupant, and
an order was made October 8, 1844, by Governor Micheltorena, that the usual
title be issued to him.  A formal grant of the land to Miranda was drawn up
pursuant to this order, and was subsequently found in the archives, but was
never executed by the governor in consequence of the political disturbances
which ended in Micheltorena's overthrow.

        Early view of Northeast Roscoe

Miranda was the father of many children, and one of his daughters, Francisca,
married a Mexican named Antonio Ortega, who had no settled habitation, but
lived sometimes with his wife's family, at this rancho, sometimes with the
priests at the different missions and for several years in Oregon.  On the
ground of his occasional visits to his father-in-law, he set up a claim to
being the real occupant of the rancho, and succeeded in obtaining from Governor
Alvarado a decree for the land, purporting to have been made August 10, 1840.

        Peter Biernbaum, second from left, building the present day Eddie's
        Place.

        DeVries and Scholfield Livery and Farm Implements, 1909. Right to left:
        Bene Scholfield, Johnny Scholfield, Konklin saloon keeper, Babe
        Phillips, Sheriff, Lem DeVries, and Tom Malone.  Cream station, Finder
        building and First State Bank.

Thus there were two conflicting claims to the some tract of land.  After the
death of Miranda, at San Rafael, in 1850, his title was sold by order of the
probate court of Marin County, and was purchased by Thomas B. Valentine of San
Francisco.  Whether the proceedings at this sale were regular, so as to vest in
the purchaser a perfect title, is at least doubtful.  The title of Ortega was
conveyed to Charles White of San Jose.

After the establishment of the land commission, both of these claims were
presented to that tribunal for adjudication.  Valentine put in some testimony
which was thought to be rather damaging to the success of the Ortega claim,
whereupon an agreement was made between the holders of these rival titles,
providing that the testimony should be suppressed, the Miranda claim withdrawn,
the Ortega claim pressed for confirmation and the proceeds of the sales of the
lands covered by it divided between the contracting parties.

The Miranda title was thus summarily disposed of by the act of its holder.  To
clear away the Ortega title by the slow machinery of the law, took several
years.  It was confirmed by the land commission, was twice before the United
States District Court-first confirmed, and afterwards rejected in 1863.

The land embraced within the limits of the rancho thus become public domain of
the United States, and the government surveys were extended over it.  That
portion within the boundaries of the incorporated city of Petaluma was ceded to
that city by Act of Congress on March 1, 1867, and the occupants of all the
remainder obtained patents under the pre-emption law.

Valentine besieged the doors of Congress for many years to get an act passed
allowing him to present his title to the court for confirmation.  Such a measure
would have been a great injustice to the occupants of the land, for although
the original title was undoubtedly genuine, and would have been confirmed, he
prevented a confirmation by his voluntary withdrawal of it."

Valentine, on April 5, 1872, was finally successful in obtaining permission to
present his case to the 9th Circuit Court of the United States in California.
This permission was obtained through the passage of a special act of the 42nd
Congress of the United States entitled "An act for the relief of Thomas B.
Valentine."

This act also provided that should Valentine's claim prove to be valid, he
would be issued script in lieu of his claim.  Valentine's claim in fact proved
valid and script was issued in 1874.  This "Valentine" script allowed him to
select an equal quantity of unoccupied and unappropriated public lands of the
United States.

This script was transferrable and E. No. 47 for 40 acres of it came into the
possession of Eugene Stanhope Elliott, a judge and prominent citizen of
Milwaukee, Wis.  Elliott filed this script with the Receiver of the U. S. Land
Office, Aberdeen, Dakota Territory April 19, 1883 and became the first owner of
the 40 acres which he had platted for the purpose of founding a town to be
known by the name of "Roscoe".  Hugh R. Blanding surveyed the original town
site April 25, 1883.

Morgan's addition was the first attachment to the original townsite.  Charles
P. Morgan purchased the 40 acres north of the original plot from the government
May 14, 1884.  He then sold it to his sister, Ida J. Trunkey, August 15, 1884,
for the sum of $1,400.  She then had it platted August 18, 1884. (it should be
noted that all additions to the town of Roscoe are annexed when they are
platted.)

        Main Street 1926

The next addition was Engle's.  Sam Basford gained possession of this land
February 5, 1884 from the U. S. Government.  He then sold the land to David G.
Preston in May of that year who had it platted October 1, 1884 and named it for
George S. Engle, his agent.  The greater part of this addition has since been
vacated.

Lennox addition was platted November 27, 1884. Samuel Basford was the original
owner of the land which become the Lennox addition.  Samuel obtained this land
from the government February 5, 1884 and sold it to David G. Preston May 10,
1884.  Mr. Preston then sold this land to the Dakota Land and Investment Co. of
Milwaukee, Wis.  September 3, 1884 for $50.  The company then had the tract of
land platted and named the addition after their secretary, Benjamin G. Lennox.

Arnold's addition was also platted by the Dakota Land and Investment Co.
November 27, 1884.  Joseph W. Parmley first obtained the land from the
government June 30, 1884.  The land was then sold to John R. McDonald July 9,
1884.  One month later Dakota Land and Investment purchased this tract of land
and had it platted and named it after Sidney Arnold, its vice president.

        View along Highway 12

The land that become Basford's addition was purchased from the government by
Sebastian B. Basford September 28, 1886.  His family platted the addition May
29, 1911. Part of this land was sold to Samuel Dalton March 21, 1918, who in
turn sold it to W. J. Snyder February 19, 1920.  Snyder then platted Lot No. 5
out of the original Basford's addition November 25, 1924.  Snyder then sold a
different portion of the original Basford addition to Herman Tschirley who had
Lots No. 6, 7 and 8, platted May 22, 1930.  Wendelin Mardian had Block No. 1
platted out of Lot No. 8 on August 10, 1936.  Later, Mardian's first addition
was platted out of Lots 4 and 5 of Block No. 1 outlot of Lot No. 8. Peter Goetz
replotted Lot No. 7 October 11, 1948 and named it Goetz's addition.

Lots A1 to A12 were platted by the owner, Rhea Christen.  This took place
September 28, 1938. Schurr's additions involved the replotting of a portion of
Engle's addition.  The first was platted June 27, 1946, the second November 10,
1947 and the third May 27, 1949.

The last addition to date was filed October 7, 1982 and named the first
Railroad Addition.  It consists of a plat of four lots.

With the coming of the Chicago, Milwaukee & St. Paul Railroad in 1886 and the
extension of the north and south lines, Roscoe become a transportation center,
attracting more settlers to our area and creating a business boom for our town.

November 11, 1886 a few pioneers got together and formed a permanent
organization for the village.  To begin with, there were only three members.
They established offices for five trustees, one clerk, one treasurer, one
assessor, one justice of the peace, and one marshal.  They also selected
November 23, 1886 to be the first election day for Roscoe.  The resolutions
were approved and the following men signed the document as inspectors: Edgar
Sharp, George B. Roushow, and Oscar Sutley.  At their first meeting, November
29, 1886, a permanent organization was set up with John L. Smith moving that
Zack F. Sutley be named the first president of the board of Roscoe for a term
of one year and that Edgar Sharp and John Harrington be the first attorney and
clerk, respectively.

The trustees divided the town into five wards.  The number of wards varied from
time to time until 1938. At this time the population reached 500 and Roscoe
became a city.  A Mayor-Council form of government was established when Roscoe
gained city status.  The six councilmen were, from that time to the present,
chosen from three wards.

Town government started in 1886 to handle the affairs of the village.  Money to
carry out needed improvements, however, was limited.  Tax revenue was small,
levies were 5 mills on the dollar, valuation was low.  Town revenue reached
$443.63 in 1892.

        Roscoe 1914

Crosswalks were deemed a necessary item by the board in 1887.  Three of these
crosswalks were constructed in April.  Fifty cents was needed to make repairs
on these walks in the month of May.  The year 1908 saw the introduction of
cement street crossings. Seven street and alley crossings were constructed by
the Bowdle Artificial Stone Co. The cost of these crossings was $515.00.

Specific lots and areas were ordered to receive concrete sidewalks within 60
days.  If owners did not comply with said order, the city would build the
sidewalks and bill the owners.  This order was issued in 1915.

A town well was first sunk in the summer of 1887.  The well was dug by the
Phelps Windmill Co. The town was bonded for $2,530.00 to complete this well.  A
new windmill was purchased for the well in 1893 at a cost of $150.00. A new
well was constructed in 1907.  Licht and Huck dug this well at a cost of
$500.00. This 1907 well was 400 feet deep.

In 1947, the people of Roscoe felt the need for a more modern way of living and
demanded a water and sewer system.  A bond issue to sponsor this project was
approved and the water and sewer system was installed in 1948-1949 at an
approximate cost of $162,000.00.

A disposal plant was installed for $30,000.00 in 1952. Bids were received July,
1968 for a lagoon system.  This was constructed by Hall Construction Co. of
Westport, South Dakota and Hammond and Rohrbach Bros. of Roscoe.  The cost was
$13,537.78. This lagoon project was completed in March 1969.

During the summer of 1981, Roscoe's west well quit pumping and could not be
repaired.  Then on December 31, 1981, the east well quit pumping and left the
city completely without water.

        Main Street during 1916 Edmunds County Fair

Huron Drilling was hired to drill a new emergency well in January, 1982.
During the time the well was being dug, water was hauled into Roscoe from
surrounding towns and distributed to the residents of Roscoe through the water
mains.

A $150,000.00 General Obligation Bond issue was approved in April, 1982 to
drill a new well, transmission line and 14 blocks of water main replacement. An
$80,000.00 Community Development Block Grant was received to help fund the
project.

Four street lamps were purchased in 1910 at a cost of $65.00 a lamp.  Later
orders for globes and mantles would indicate that these were gas lamps.

The town purchased it's first electric light and power plant in 1916.  The cost
of the plant was $6,500.00. The bond issue for this purchase was unanimously
approved by 21 voters.  In 1918, a second unit was added to fulfill
requirements of this growing community. Electrical service was on from 6 a.m.
until 12 p.m. with certain days for washing and ironing.  The plant served from
1916 to 1925 when it was sold to the Northern States Power and Light Company of
Mobridge.  Soon after that, 24-hour service was established.

The war years of 1917-18 were years of great sorrow and feelings.  Then in 1918
thousands of people died from flu.  Twenty-five people died from the Roscoe
community, three and four from the same family.  In 1919 came the after effects
of the war with a business recession that lasted until 1922.

A town square or park was originally platted when the town was laid out, but
was rented out for gardens until 1920.  The town planted trees and opened it as
a public park in this year.  The trees thrived until 1981, when Dutch Elm
disease started taking it's toll of these landmarks.  A total of 25 trees were
removed by the fall of 1982.

In 1976, Wiedrich Construction was awarded a contract to build a comfort
station in the park.  A B.O.R. grant was received to help fund the building and
some new playground equipment.  Camping facilities have been added as a
convenience for tourists.  Due to the loss of so many trees, picnic shelters
were built in the fall of 1982.

Unemployment was a problem during the depression years.  The town tried to
provide work by digging water mains by hand.  The project failed when buyers
could not be found to purchase the $6,500.00 in bonds. However, work did come
to Roscoe in 1934 when federal aid was provided to build a city auditorium.  The
town also had other projects during the depression years, such as graveling and
regrading the city streets.

Roscoe entered into a bitter fight in 1933 over where the court house was to be
rebuilt.  Roscoe lost in this attempt to gain the county court house.

As early as 1886, ordinances were being passed concerning the sale of
intoxicating liquors.  Discussions were held on such topics as closing saloons
on Sundays and not selling liquor to certain individuals.  By 1947, the people
voted to establish a municipal liquor store. The sale of liquor remained in the
hands of the city until 1979 when Donald Roesch built a new cafe and lounge and
leased the liquor license from the city.

Since the nations bicentennial year, Roscoe has experienced a building boom
both in business structures and homes.  There have been at least 18 new homes
and businesses built.

Though Roscoe's population has declined since the town's diamond jubilee in
1958, there seems to be a reversal of this downward trend.  People are moving
into Roscoe again.  Roscoe is again making progress as we celebrate the
completion of our first 100 years.

        City Hall built in 1934.  Henry Schroh and Theophil Sahli. Emanuel
        Hofer (painting) children, unknown.

DID YOU KNOW THAT

Mary Little (Mrs. Henry Huck), was a direct descendant of Little John whose
claim to fame was his association with Robin Hood of Sherwood Forest in England.

Lynn Light started the Movie Theatre in the City Hall in 1934.  Ray Decker
operated the movie machines.  Before that movies were shown on a screen
outside.  Movies had been shown in the Methodist Church building in about 1920.
The building had been used as a dance hall before that.  It burned down in
about 1923.  The Jamieson Hotel and the Brenton Brothers Garage burned in about
that some time area.

Wm. Swan bought the movie equipment from Lynn Light in 1935.

Henry Huck organized the first Sunday School and the first Choir in Roscoe.

Daily train service to Bowdle was established in August 1891.

J. W. Jamieson at one time owned the Edmunds House.

All violators of Diamond Jubilee laws, who were brought into "Kangaroo Court"
in 1958, were found guilty and sentenced to punishments befitting their crimes.

The records at the Roscoe High School show that 938 students have graduated
from that institution in the years 1922-1983 inclusive.

The first all-class Roscoe High School reunion was held in 1958 in conjunction
with the City Diamond Jubilee.

        Ira Brenton, customer unidentified, and Sam Brenton in Brenton Brothers
        Garage, 1922.

INTERESTING ITEMS FROM THE ROSCOE CITY COUNCIL MINUTES

Dec. 8, 1886--(First official meeting of the town board) Zack Sutley was
appointed the first president of the board.  Motion of Trustee Bernard, the
clerk was instructed to procure plans for a jail of the following dimensions,
12 x 22 ft. 8 ft. high.  Cell with two bunks. Building to be papered and sided
and roof shingled.

Dec. 21, 1886--Moved by Trustee Bernard that the marshall be instructed to
procure a pound for the detention of all animals running at large.

Dec. 28, 1886--Jan. 25, 1887.-The ordinance to regulate the sale of
intoxicating liquors was read and on motion of Trustee Bernard approved.  The
marshall being present, the question of a more strict observance in regard to
closing saloons on Sunday was discussed and the marshall was instructed to
enforce the low.

April 19, 1887--it was moved that all persons are requested to comply with the
ordinance as to removing manure and debris from around their premises.

July 18, 1888--An ordinance restraining cattle from running at large was passed
and approved by Board. On motion, E. Newels stable was designated as the town
pound.

April 18, 1888--The Roscoe House was designated as the official polling place.

April 3, 1905--Moved and supported that Fred Emmings confer with R. R. Co. to
plant flowers and trees around their depot in Roscoe.  Carried.  Same motion
for school board to plant trees around their school.

July 3, 1905--Motion that the clerk order the Marshall to look after the pool
tables and see that they are not being used for playing for money or other
valuables.

Jan. 26, 1906--On motion, Dr. Rott was appointed a committee of one to procure
a suitable house out of the town limits for a sanatarium for smallpox patients
and to take all the necessary precautions to keep the small pox from spreading
and to remove the smallpox patients from the railroad depot.

March 29, 1906--Moved and supported that President appoint committee to look up
dumping grounds for the town.  Carried.  The following were appointed- Wm.
Finder, M. Scholfield and Geo. Chappell.

May 7, 1906--The committee on dumping grounds reported they had secured same
from Robert Baldwin on the north side of his pasture and notice is hereby given
to all persons that all garbage, manure, etc. must be dumped there.

Nov. 15, 1907--Moved by Finder and seconded by Lettis, that the clerk get a
star for the Marshall and a star for the deputy Marshall.

July 6, 1908--First mention was made of the use of a telephone.

Nov. 1, 1909--inspection of the new jail was made to see if it was satisfactory.

Dec. 6, 1909--It was decided to accept a contract with DeBolt Safe and Lock Co.
for two cells for the town jail at $350.

May 16, 1910--The telephone bill for this month was 50 cents.

May 22, 1911--The first brick building, the Stephanson building was being built
at this time.  There was considerable disagreement with the city about
permitting a sidewalk entrance to the basement.  A petition signed by
townspeople was presented at this meeting which helped Stephanson to get the
permission he sought.

June 3, 1912 --Moved and supported that the Marshall order all hitching posts
to be removed from Mitchell St. Moved and supported that a one and one-fourth
inch iron pipe be put through 30 posts.

Aug. 5, 1913--Moved and supported that LeFebvre and Tschirley act as a
committee to have signs painted and erected giving notice that the speed limit
for automobiles, motorcycles and other conveyances is places at 10 miles per
hour.

August 1915--The first Edmunds County Fair was held in Roscoe.

Adam Brothers Hardware, Martin and August Adam and four customers, 1923

August 4, 1916--Material is to be denoted to put lights up in the Fairgrounds.
The Power Co. was asked to "hustle" things up so as to have the lights by fair
time

Aug. 14, 15 and 16.

Oct. 6, 1916--Wm.  Finder was instructed to put up the Fire Bell.

May 7, 1918--The chairman instructed the Fire Chief to buy two dozen water
pails at 40 cents each and donate them to the Fire Dept.

Oct. 4, 1921--Moved and seconded that the Town of Roscoe charge the Princess
Theatre $1.00 per night for electric lights used in operating movie picture
machine.

July 3, 1923--Moved and seconded that Committee see Paul Elford in regard to
decorating inside of light plant.

Sept. 2, 1924--Voted to pay the Roscoe band $100 for service rendered during
the year.

Winter of 1936-37--Many hours of snow shoveling done manually at about 25 cents
per hour were paid for. Forty six men were listed as having shoveled in March.

March 1, 1938--Agreement was entered into with Wm. H. Swan to hold movies in
the City Auditorium Saturday and Sunday evenings.

Aug. 5, 1946--A committee of citizens met with council about the Polio epidemic.

April 7, 1947--Clemens Geier bought the Band Stand for $58.60.

March 7, 1955--A curfew was set for children below the age of 15 to be off the
street by 10 p.m.

Dec. 2, 1969--The Roscoe Commercial Club requested a building permit to build a
30' x 48' cafe on lots 25-26, Block 16, Lennox addition at a cost of $8,000.

April 1976--All City ordinances were revised.

1976--The old Legion Hall located along Highway 12, was remodeled and converted
into a museum.

Wm. Lammle became the City Auditor in 1938 and held that office until 1970.

Opal Himmerich become City Auditor in 1970, holding that office at the present
time.

Geo.  Wilson became the City Treasurer in 1945 and held that office until 1961.

Kate Meier become the City Treasurer in 1961, holding that office until 1980.
