ROSCOE CONKLING

The John Conkling family migrated from England to Boston and settled in Salem,
Massachusetts in 1635. Several generations later, Roscoe Conkling was born in
Albany, New York October 30, 1829.  He was born to Alfred and Eliza (Cockburn)
Conkling.  Roscoe's parents were prominent people as his father served as a
Congressman and also as a United States district judge, a position which he
held for 27 years.

Young Conkling left home in 1842 to enter Mount Collegiate Institute in New
York City.  He became a lawyer in Utica, New York in 1846.

Zachary Taylor ran for President of the United States in 1848.  This
presidential race was important for Roscoe Conkling.  It was in this election
that he began to gain national fame, because of the fact that he spoke publicly
for Taylor.  The public support which he gave to Taylor showed that Conkling
was a tremendous speaker.

Roscoe Conkling, from the time of the election of 1848, continued to climb the
ladder of success. in addition to being a shrewd and brilliant criminal lawyer,
he became the Mayor of Utica in 1858.  He was a member of the House of
Representatives from 1859 to 1863.  Conkling was defeated for the Congress in
the election of 1862, but was returned to the House in the election of 1864.
He became a United States Senator in 1867 and held that position until 1881,
when he resigned from the Senate.

Conkling gave strong support to Abraham Lincoln in the presidential election of
1860.  He was also instrumental in the election of President Grant.  During the
administration of Grant, Conkling had a great deal of influence on decisions
made at the presidential level.

Grant was opposed by Horatio Seymour in the election of 1868.  Conkling
campaigned for Grant even though, Seymour was Conkling's brother-in-law.

It should be noted that Roscoe Conkling was a great influence in the Senate
during the Civil War.  He also voted to impeach President Andrew Johnson.
Conkling resigned from the Senate after a disagreement with President Garfield
concerning the removal of Mr. Merritt from the position of collector of the
port of New York.

Roscoe Conkling, after his resignation from the Senate, practiced law in New
York City.  He continued this practice until his death April 18, 1888.

The death of Conkling was on indirect result of the great blizzard of March 12,
1888.  Mr. Conkling walked from his office on Wall Street to the New York Club
on 25th Street, in the blizzard.  He suffered from exposure and exhaustion,
from which he never fully recovered.

Conkling was a handsome man.  He was 6'3" tall, straight, broad shouldered,
dark yellow wavy hair with glints of red, and a reddish exquisitely curled
beard.  He was touchy and temperamental.  Conkling was, above all, extremely
honest.

Roscoe, South Dakota was named after this man. Although Conkling never visited
this town, records show that he traveled to Yellowstone National Park by train
in the summer of 1883.  This trip probably brought him as close to Roscoe,
South Dakota as he ever come.

Founders of Roscoe

        CHARLES P. MORGAN

"Another young but well-known citizen of Chicago has been taken away by
death--Mr.  Charles Purchase Morgan, ex-Vice President of the American Ticket
Brokers' Association.  Mr. Morgan came to Chicago in 1873, and although only 28
years of age at the time of his death, he had been in business for himself
nearly all the time. He spent the year 1883 in   Dakota, where he founded the
now flourishing town of Roscoe, the county seat of Edmunds County.  He named
the town in honor of the great New York statesman, Roscoe Conkling, between
whom and his father, the late Hon.  Allen D. Morgan, of Auburn, N.Y., there was
a long and warm friendship.  Mr. Morgan was a Mason, a Knight of Pythias, an
Elk, a member of the Royal Arcanum and of the Marquette Club.  He was
unmarried, but leaves a mother, Mrs. Mary J. Morgan, a brother, Wm.  Seward
Morgan, and three sisters, Miss Anna Morgan, Mrs. A. J. Trunkey and Mrs. Marion
Reynolds, oil of this city, to mourn his early death."

This obituary was taken from the March 19, 1890 issue of the Chicago
Inter-Ocean, a daily newspaper of that period.

Charles Purchase Morgan was born February 14, 1862, at Ledyard, Cayuga County,
New York, near Auburn.  He was the son of Allen D. Morgan, a gentleman former,
who served briefly in the New York legislature in 1860-61.  His mother was Mary
Jane Thornton Morgan, he was one of five children.  His parents and
grandparents were natives of the Auburn region.  The Morgans were of Welsch
descent.  After the death of his father, his mother moved the family to
Chicago, where Charles lived the rest of his short life, except for the time
spent in Dakota Territory.

His oldest sister, Anna, was a noted elocutionist, a teacher of speech and
dramatics.  She traveled extensively throughout the United States and Europe,
giving her dramatic readings, appearing in Aberdeen in 1883, at which time she
came to Roscoe to visit her brother in his newly established town on the Dakota
prairie.

Charles P. Morgan was a Railroad Ticket Broker in Chicago and we can only
speculate as to the reasons that prompted him to venture out into the unsettled
prairies of Dakota in April 1883.  Perhaps his association with the railroad
gave him some insight into the probability of the railroads extension into this
remote western region, and his sense of adventure made him strive to be a part
of the opening of the new horizons.  It is evident that his family shared his
interest in the new territory, as his mother, Mary J. Morgan, and his sister,
Ida Trunkey, followed him out here and become pre-emptors of land in the area.
According to the information available to us, Morgan brought with him a large
tent which served as a hotel, providing food and shelter to many pioneers in
those next months, and later replaced it with a wooden structure. It is not
known to us exactly how long he remained here in "his town", but all accounts
we have, state that he lived here about one year.

According to his Certificate of Death, obtained from the Cook County Clerk in
Chicago, his occupation at the time of his untimely death, was a railroad
ticket broker. The Chicago City Directory of that time lists him and his
brother, Seward, as "Morgan Bros, Ticket Brokers". Charles P. Morgan died March
17, 1890, at his residence at 294 Erie St. The cause of death is listed as
Cardiac Asthemia from Pleura-Pneumonia, duration of illness was eight days.  He
was buried in Chicago's Graceland Cemetery March 19, 1890.

And so, with deepest gratitude, we remember Charles Purchase Morgan, who at the
age of 21, was the "father" of Roscoe --- our town.

EUGENE S. ELLIOTT

Eugene Stanhope Elliott was born in Vermilion county, Illinois August 13, 1842.
His parents, Rev. George W. Elliott and Susan (Bates) Elliott, were both of
illustrious ancestry.  His father's ancestors settled in the colonies in 1632
and his grandfather, Ezekiel, fought in the Revolutionary War.

Elliott's family moved to Milwaukee, Wis. in 1852 where Elliott received his
early education.  He entered Dartmouth College in 1860 but left to enter the
service of his country to fight in the Civil War.  During his two years in the
service, he participated in the raid at Harper's Ferry and the battle of
Antietam.  When he was discharged, he returned to Milwaukee and began the study
of law.  Prior to his admission to the bar in 1876, he published a weekly
newspaper for six years.

        EUGENE S. ELLIOTT, Judge of Second Judicial Circuit. Died Jan. 2, 1902.
        Milwaukee.

Elliott's career began as a public official in 1886 when he was city attorney
for two terms.  He was chairman of the Republican State Convention in 1894 and a
delegate in 1896 to the national convention.  He began his brief career as a
judge in 1900 and presided at many notable trials.

Mr. Elliott was married in 1865 to Catherine E. Dousman, daughter of George D.
Dousman, one of Milwaukee's earliest settlers.  Her family was largely involved
with real estate and one source said her uncle was the first millionaire in
Wisconsin.  They had three children, one of which married and had a daughter,
Helen McElroy, who died in 1969.

The Milwaukee Whist Club (formerly Chess Club) was founded by Elliott who also
started the American Whist league.  Whist and chess were Elliott's main pastimes
and he was a noted player of each.  It was during a whist game that Mr. Elliott
died January 2, 1902.

JOSEPH WILLIAM PARMLEY

Mr. Parmley was a native of Mifflin, Iowa County, Wisconsin, born January 12,
1861, and was a son of Joseph and Jane (Ashton) Parmley.  After completing a
common-school course he attended the State Normal School of Platteville and the
Lawrence University at Appleton, Wis.

His residence in Dakota territory dates from April of 1883.  After looking over
the northwest he concluded that it would eventually be a great agricultural
empire. The railroads had reached Aberdeen and already extensions were being
considered.  Mr. Parmley studied the map and said that some day the Chicago,
Milwaukee & St. Paul Railroad Company would build west to the Pacific and felt
that there was no more feasible route than from Aberdeen straight to the coast.
He started out on foot and when forty miles west stopped on the present town
site of Roscoe and looked around him for miles, it was then that he saw the tent
hotel set up by Charles Morgan.  He secured a part of the present site of
Roscoe as his pre-emption, then returned to Aberdeen with some of the other
early settlers of Roscoe.  Parmley purchased lumber for a shanty and drove back
to his claim when his luggage arrived in Aberdeen.

        J. W. PARMLEY

Henry Huck, one of the other settlers, had brought a printing press and with
Parmley, set up the Roscoe Herald which was first published in September of
1883. Parmley became the sole owner of the paper and continued to publish it
until 1910, when he purchased the South Dakota Tribune and merged the two
journals under the name of the Ipswich Tribune.  All this time he was working
earnestly for the development of the district in which he had located and his
efforts extended beneficially along many lines.

When Edmunds County was organized in August, 1883, Mr. Parmley was appointed
superintendent of schools and was elected to that office in the fall of 1884.
When he left the position of county superintendent, he was elected register of
deeds and county clerk.  In 1887 he studied law and was admitted to the bar but
never engaged in active practice.  He served as county judge for a number of
terms, both by election and by appointment of the governor and also served two
terms as a member of the state legislature.

Mr. Parmley was intensely interested in better farming methods and was a
pioneer in introducing Durum wheat, better varieties of corn, alfalfa and
drought resistant forage crops.  He has also introduced and bred herds of
registered cattle and had the largest herd of Shetland ponies in the northwest.
He was the owner of the Edmunds County Abstract Company and was half owner of
the McPherson County Abstract Company at Leola.

Mr. Parmley was known as the father of the good roads movement in the state.
He was the originator of a plan to build an improved public highway from
Aberdeen to Mobridge, which was named the Parmley Highway.  Later he led the
movement for the extension of the road to the east as far as the Twin Cities
and to the west to the Yellowstone National Park.  He become president of an
organization known as the Yellowstone Trail Association and the road has since
extended from Detroit to Seattle and is U.S. Highway 12.  In 1925 he was state
highway commissioner.

He was also intensely interested in the world peace movement and was in demand
as a lecturer on the subject.  He traveled extensively and wrote of his
journeys for publication.

Mr. Parmley was married in 1887 to Lissie E. Baker of Dodgeville, Wis.  They
settled in Ipswich after their marriage and had two children, Loren and Irene,

Although he only resided in Roscoe for a short time, he had an influence on our
development through the school systems, legislature and highway projects.  He
died in Ipswich December 15, 1940.

THE TAPESTRY OF THE HENRY HUCK FAMILY HERITAGE By Mabel L. Huck Galbraith

        Henry and Mary Ann Huck

The town of Roscoe, South Dakota, holds an important place, not only in the
pioneer history of the state, but also in the history of our family, for it was
here that the two strands of the tapestry that is our family's heritage were
joined together.  It was here that our mother Grace Eleanor Kearns, of pioneer
stock which came by prairie schooner from Ohio to Iowa in 1853, met our father,
William Gladstone Huck, who was the first child born to Henry and Mary Ann Huck
after they came by rail from Chicago to homestead in Dakota Territory in 1883,
eleven years after their arrival in New York Harbor.  Mother was of Irish and
Pennsylvania Dutch background, while my father's parents had come to America
directly from England.

        William Gladstone and Grace Eleanor Huck

We have no record of the exact time when the first of my mother's ancestors
came from Ireland to America, but John Kearns, my mother's grandfather, grew up
in Richland County, Ohio, and in 1828 married Margaret Groughbough, daughter of
hard-working, God-fearing Dutch pioneers who had come by wagon train from
Pennsylvania to settle in the rich green hills of Ohio.  To this union, nine
children were born, one of whom, William, died at the age of six years.  The
rest, including my mother's father, Valentine, next to the youngest, were
strong and healthy, good farmers, good neighbors, and active in church, school
and community.

When Valentine was five years old, John and Margaret Kearns sold their property
in Ohio and traveled by prairie schooner to Linn County, Iowa, where, in the
summer of 1853, John Kearns purchased a 105 acre farm from the U. S.
Government, and he and his family became part of Linn Grove Community.  In
1872, John Kearns died at the age of sixty-eight.  His older children had grown
up and established homes of their own by that time, but Valentine, who lived on
the farm and had helped his father run it, continued to cultivate the land for
his mother, who was still strong and well.  He also homesteaded on a
thirty-seven and one-half acre plot adjoining the home place.  Two years later
Val married Eliza Ellen Erion and brought her home to the log cabin he had
built on his homestead claim.  The Erions had come from Ohio to Iowa by covered
wagon when Eliza, or Ellie, as she was called, was twelve years old.  Mr. Erion
was an elder in the Presbyterian Church and Ellie was a leader among the young
people.

During the ensuing sixteen years, from 1876 to 1892, Val and Ellie had eight
children, the first three born in the log cabin to which Val brought Ellie as a
bride, and the other five in a large frame house which he built up the hill
from the cabin to accommodate his growing family.  Grace Eleanor, who was to
become my mother, was the first child born in the new home.  The oldest boy,
Carl, become a Presbyterian minister, while Grace became a school teacher, two
facts which played a crucial role in bringing my father and mother together in
Roscoe early in the twentieth century.

While the Kearns family were playing a part in the growth and development of
the Midwestern United States, my father's people were sharing in the early
temperance movement and other campaigns for social improvement in Westmoreland
County, England, but in 1872, my father's parents, Henry and Mary Ann Huck,
came to America.

Since their first child, little Clement, was still only a baby, Henry, or
Harry, as he was called, came first, leaving his wife and baby with their
family in England until he had a job and place to live.  Since he was an
excellent printer, he had no difficulty getting a job with Horace Greeley on
the "New York Times, found an adequate place to live, and sent for Polly and
the baby. But three days out of Liverpool, little Clement become very ill with
smallpox.  Despite the doctor's skill and all the care that Polly, assisted by
a kind German woman who insisted on sharing their quarantine on the ship and
helping in every way possible, the baby become steadily worse.  When the ship
reached Blackwell's Island, Polly, Clement, and three German mothers whose
babies also were ill with smallpox, were quarantined, so Polly's only contact
with Harry was through his daily letters and his visits each evening, when he
would row over from the mainland and they could see each other and talk across
the intervening water.  On August 14, little Clement died and was buried with
three German babies who had suffered the same fate.  Then, after thorough
fumigation of all belongings and a doctor's examination resulting in a clean
bill of health for Polly, she was at last able to join Harry on land.

Harry loved his job with Horace Greeley, but he had promised to join his
brother John, who had come to Chicago several years before, so Harry and Polly
soon made the three-day trip by rail and were welcomed to Chicago by John and
his six children.  The mother had died a few weeks after the trauma of the
great Chicago fire, from which she was unable to recover.  The oldest daughter,
Belle, who was fourteen, had become housekeeper and cook for her father and
younger siblings.

For eleven years, Harry and Polly lived a busy and productive life in Chicago.
During that time, Harry built up a very successful printing business, providing
Sunday School lessons, Bible commentaries, and other material for churches and
other Christian organizations, brochures for new businesses, notices for
evangelistic and temperance meetings, in which work he was always very active,
and, as land was opening for settlement farther west, reports  and brochures
for land promoters, as well as for the Chicago, Milwaukee, and St. Paul
Railroad. An excellent singer, as well as a good organizer, he  led several
singing groups, choirs, etc., helping in evangelistic meetings in the Salvation
Army Hall, Moody Tabernacle, and in churches in towns outside of Chicago, in
association with friends who were good speakers.

Six children were born to him and Polly while they lived in Chicago, but the
first, little Edie, died of a severe fever before her fourth birthday.  Before
Edie's first birthday, in October, 1872, Harry's brother John died, so Harry
and Polly took his six children as their own.  As their family increased in
size, it was necessary to find more adequate living quarters, and they made
their home in seven different places during their years in Chicago.

But as Harry heard more about the opportunities awaiting the venturesome
pioneer in lands west of Chicago, he became very much interested.  Printing
brochures for the railroad and the reports which his friends, Bishop Fallows
and Charles Cook, brought back after their trips to Dakota Territory, increased
his longing to file on a claim.  After much thought and prayer, he and Polly
decided to move to Dakota.

        Henry Huck

On April 15, 1883, Harry Huck arrived by train in Aberdeen, Dakota Territory.
With him were his nephew, Henry Huck, and his two little sons, Walter Evanston,
just past eight years old, and Frederick Bliss, not quite seven, while Charles
Cook, who had already filed on a claim near La Beau, accompanied them.  Harry
also brought machinery, lumber, and needed supplies for life on a homestead, in
addition to Old Molly, the cow (which the Hucks had acquired some months
earlier to provide the family with milk), little Molly, her calf, and a few
hens which had provided them with eggs while they were still living in Chicago.
The provisions for the Hucks and Mr. Cook combined filled two freight cars,
but it was cheaper to get the tools and supplies they would need from
Montgomery Ward in Chicago and take them along when they made the trip than it
would be to order them shipped out later, and the needed provisions were not
yet available in territory so recently opened for settlement.  It was an
exciting adventure for all of them, especially for Walter and Freddie.  How
glad they were that their father had decided to take them with him instead of
leaving them in Chicago with Mamma and the babies!

It was a beautiful spring morning when the train drew into the Aberdeen depot,
the breakfast which was served them on the train was delicious, and the few
moments of worship, in which Charles Cook shared as Harry led his boys in
prayer, were filled with heartfelt thanksgiving to God for the safe journey and
for the opportunities opening before them.  Then came the exciting moment in
which Harry and the boys first set foot on Dakota soil.  Since Cook had been
there before, he was able to help Harry in planning for the trip out to file on
the claim.

Leaving the livestock in the fenced yard provided for that purpose at the depot
and storing the freight to await the time when they could move it out to the
claim, while Freddie and Walter were left in the care of the landlady, Mrs.
Hazzard, Harry and Henry went with Cook to the Department of Interior office,
where they looked at the maps to learn the location of available homestead
sites.  Then, riding in the farm wagon they had shipped out from Chicago, and
driving a two-horse team of the four old street car horses Harry had bought in
Aberdeen, they set out to find and file on satisfactory claims.  Out past the
small townsites of Georgetown, Edmunds, and Freeport they drove, then fifteen or
twenty miles farther across the lush, rippling grass.  At last, far ahead, they
came to a valley, a "draw" where water remained all summer long, ending in a big
"slough", rimmed on two sides by large rocks half- hidden in the tall grass and
rushes.

At the very summit of a low hill on the west side of the slough, a perfect
circle several feet across showed clearly in the gross, a circular path where
almost no grass was growing, like the location of a large tent, only more
deeply marked.  Harry stopped the team and walked over to inspect the circle so
deeply marked in the prairie grass.

"I think that must be a buffalo ring", he said to the others as he returned to
the wagon.  "I have heard of them, and I'll ask around and find out.  This is
the land I want for my claim.  We'll have to find out about staking the claims,
and figure out where the boundaries are, but this spot is good enough for me."

Henry was pleased with the location, too, and planned to file on a claim next
to his uncle's.  Since Cook planned to file fifty or sixty miles farther west,
it was decided not to drive that for that day.  A few moments later they came
to a new town site--a large tent, with three or four men lounging about, as
well as a wagon and smaller tent.  A sign above the large tent flop was
inscribed, "Cottonade Hotel", and one of the men, who all greeted the newcomers
cordially, identified himself as Charles Morgan from Chicago, proprietor of the
Cottonade Hotel.

As Harry talked with Morgan, they found that they had known several of the some
people in Chicago, including Bishop Follows and Mr. Rounds.  Charles Morgan had
his mother and sister with him, and had homesteaded on the land on which the
tent was situated.

"This is the townsite of Roscoe," Morgan told Harry. "It is named in honor of
U.S. Senator Roscoe Conkling of New York.  We are glad to welcome you to
Roscoe."

While Harry was setting up his tent, he mentioned to Morgan that he had brought
his printing press with him and was hoping to publish a town and county
newspaper.  Morgan expressed his approval of this possibility, saying that a
newspaper would be a great asset to the town, helping it to become the county
seat and a real business center.  A few moments later, another man joined the
group.  Morgan introduced the newcomer as Joseph W. Parmley of Wisconsin.

Harry and Joe Parmley were drawn to each other at once, and soon found that
they had many similar ideas and ambitions.  It was decided that Joe would go
with Cook and the Hucks the next day when they filed on their claims.  He had
already staked his claim, but had not yet filed, so would go to Aberdeen with
them.  Harry rose at 4:00 the next morning and prepared breakfast for the boys,
and they were ready to leave soon after sun-up to stake their claims, as Henry
had watered and fed the horses while Harry was preparing breakfast. With the
assistance of the map and the instructions Morgan had given, Harry and Henry
staked their claims.  Henry's land joined Parmley's on the south, and Harry's
was next to Henry's.  Harry chose and staked a quarter for Avie west of Henry's
claim.

After staking the claims, they drove to Aberdeen, arriving in good time at the
Land Office, where Harry, Henry, and their new friend Joe filed their claims.
Cook had filed on his when he made his first trip out to Dakota.  Walter and
Freddie, whom Harry had brought with them, were very excited about all the new
experiences, and the prospect of having their own farm.

When their business at the Land Office was finished, they went back to the
railroad yards to choose some of their equipment for taking back to their
claims.  Harry decided that it would be best to leave some of the heavy items
that they would not need yet at the railroad yards to be hauled out later, so
they first built a platform with some of the lumber, set the printing press,
machinery, tools, and stone there, then loaded into the wagon the more
immediate needs, food stuffs, trunks, barrels, small tools, and some lumber for
building a shack.  While Harry, with the help of Parmley and Cook, was loading
the wagon, Henry and the boys took the animals to water, fed and watered the
chickens, and milked old Molly.  They would load the buggy in the morning.
Parmley, Harry, and the boys slept that night at Hazzards, while Henry and Cook
camped with the two loaded wagons.

They were on the road at daylight the next morning, Henry and the little boys
in the buggy pulled by the little Morgan mare, followed by Harry's wagon, drawn
by Jack and Jess, with Red and Pat tied on behind.  The Cook team and wagon
followed, with old Molly and her calf, little Molly, tied behind.  On the
corner of the wagon box hung the indispensable kerosene lantern. Joe Parmley
was staying in Aberdeen to wait for his luggage and would look after the rest
of their belongings.

It was a long drive back to Roscoe with the loaded wagons, and there were many
other wagons on the, road, hauling all manner of belongings to claims scattered
across the prairie, so they were not alone. They reached Roscoe at sundown, in
time to unload needed items and make the livestock comfortable for the night,
and the next morning they began building. Harry discovered that there was a
wonderful camaraderie among the. settlers, and he soon made the acquaintance of
the Schipkes, the Basfords, and others, all willing to exchange work or tools
or whatever was needed for the development of the infant town and community.

Charles Cook offered to stay and help the Hucks build, then they could go to
his place and help him.  They made an early start out to the claim, stopping by
Basfords on the way to get Sam Basford as extra help with the building, since
he had the benefit of experience in building his own house.  Henry and the boys
would come later in the buggy, bringing lunch for all of them.

The building went up fast, and by dark the shack was finished, oil except
hanging the door.  Harry put tools, nails, etc., inside the new shack and
nailed the door up before returning to Roscoe for the night in preparation for
the trip to Aberdeen the next day to bring out the rest of the lumber, the
printing press, etc.  On this trip, they brought home two wagon loads of
equipment, including the printing press, and also picked up mail for most of
the people in the community, including letters from home, telling them that all
was well.  Avie wrote Henry that she was coming to file on her claim April 20,
then would return to Chicago and come out with Polly and the children later.

Joe Parmley returned to Roscoe with them also, as his luggage had come through,
and he was eager to get back to the building projects on the claim and had
ordered lumber so that it could be picked up on the next trip to Aberdeen.

The next day, Harry's friends helped him build a chicken coop, with an
extension stall for old Molly, a short distance from the shack, northwest of
where the house would stand.  Harry planned to build his house on the site of
the buffalo ring, which had even greater meaning to him after Sam Basford, who
"knew the plains like a book" told him the story behind these landmarks.

"Years ago when the buffalo roamed the plains," Sam explained, "the great gray
wolves, the prairie wolves, and even the coyotes, were their enemies.  The young
stock were no match for hungry wolves, and even the mature cows and bulls were
often pulled down if a wolf could get behind its prey.  A wolf could hamstring
the biggest bull and have him helpless in a minute if he could get behind him.
But the buffalo herds learned to outwit the wolves.  Whenever they stopped for
the night or to make a stand against an attacking wolf pack, they chose a high
spot if possible and formed a ring of bulls and mature cows, with their heads
all pointing out in defense against the attackers and the young stock and
calves safely enclosed in the protecting ring.  The wolves couldn't touch the
young stock, and there was no chance for them to hamstring the mature animals,
either."

"What a perfect example for humans", Harry thought. "Satan is like the roaring
lions of Judea, but he is also like the savage wolves and the sly coyotes of
Dakota, watching for a chance to snatch one of our little ones, or hamstring
one of us older ones.  We need to stand shoulder-to-shoulder against him,
watching for him every moment and keeping our young stock out of his reach,
fighting him off at every turn.  I want my home built on the site of that
buffalo ring!"

"I will build my home where they made their brave stand," he said aloud, "and
it will be a permanent landmark."

For the rest of the week, the men were all very busy with the building
projects, but Saturday morning Harry and Charley Cook consulted with Joe
Parmley about having some kind of worship service the next day, and they at
once began to make plans to express their gratitude to the Lord by holding
their first worship service in the new community.  Mrs. Morgan agreed to allow
them to use the dining area of the "Cottonade Hotel" for the meeting, and the
word went out that there was to be a worship service at 10:30 Sunday morning.
Charles Cook, who was a lay preacher and had led many evangelistic services in
Chicago when he and Harry were working together to spread the Gospel of Christ,
would give the message, and Harry would lead the singing, using the song books
he had printed when he was in Chicago.

It was a good meeting, with seats nearly filled.  The Huck-Parmley-Cook
quartette sang the special, Harry led the congregation in two hymns, and Mr.
Cook gave a timely message from 11 Chronicles 7:14, stressing the privilege and
responsibility of owning land and using it in accordance with God's plan.

The message closed with these words: "Here in America we acknowledge God as our
Father. Therefore, as His children, we need to act and live in a way pleasing
to Him, to know Him and see His will.  We are strangers in a new land.  We are
facing a new beginning, and God is a help in time of need.  We shall need Him
often.  Let us seek the Lord while He may be found, for He only can supply all
our needs."

After the meeting there was a time of pleasant fellowship, getting better
acquainted with new neighbors. Harry and Joe went to assist Mrs. Morgan in
preparing the dinner, as many had said they were staying for the noon meal at
the Hotel.  Those with lunches spread it on the table and all ate together,
marking that Sunday, so early in the history of Roscoe, not only with a
community worship service, but also a community dinner.  Although many
homesteaders had come to Dakota without their families, as Harry had, and were
expecting to send for them later, there were a few children present, Julius
Schipke's three, Elmer Lewis' four little ones,  McMullen's two, some from the
Werdens, and others.  Harry led in the giving of thanks for the good food, and
Cook thanked them all for coming and suggested that they meet for worship and
fellowship each Sunday.  All agreed that they would not soon forget the day and
the good fellowship.

In the weeks that followed, the building projects continued.  Parmley's house,
larger than Harry's shack, was finished in three days, and work was begun on
Harry's house, which he wanted to finish soon so that Polly and the children
could join him.  Avie came out to file on her claim and then returned to
Chicago as planned.  With the advice and approval of Cook and Charlie Morgan,
Joe Parmley decided to accept Harry's offer of the job of editor of his paper.
The plans were that they would begin publishing as soon as possible, with Polly
as proof-reader and Henry and Avie as typesetters.  Both Avie and Parmley, were
concerned about the need of schools for the community, and Avie was
commissioned to find some teachers for the west when she returned to Chicago to
help Polly move out. Roscoe had applied for a post office, but had not been
granted one yet.  However, the stage running from Aberdeen to the Missouri
Country delivered mail at every post office on the route, so would deliver to
Roscoe as soon as it was accepted as a post office. Meanwhile, everyone who
went to Aberdeen brought back mail for the entire community.  Every homesteader
was plowing and planting with whatever farm equipment he had available, and
people willingly exchanged tools and machinery as they exchanged work.  He who
must borrow one day had the privilege of helping by loaning the next.  It was
an exciting time of growth, learning, and shoring, with everyone working at
peak capacity and encouraging one another as they worked.

On July 26, 1883, the County of Edmunds was organized and Roscoe was at once in
the race for County Seat, even though most of the town buildings, Grocery,
Hardware, Hotel, Newspaper Office, and even the direction Main Street should
run, were still in the planning stage.  Harry Huck and Joe Parmley went into
business together to publish a newspaper which was to be named "The Edmunds
County News and Roscoe Herald".  Joe would furnish the money and hold the
position of editor, while Harry did the work of publishing the paper.  The
first issue was published on September 25, 1883, just three days after Polly
Huck, the proof-reader, and Miss Averil Bird, who was one of the typesetters,
arrived from Chicago with the three younger Huck children, Clara Louise, just
post five, Harry Milton, not yet four, and Averil Mignonette, who celebrated
her second birthday shortly after her father and older brothers arrived in
Dakota.

It was a joyous family reunion when Harry drove to meet his wife and little
ones, with Avie, also, at the Chicago and Milwaukee depot in Aberdeen on
Saturday, September 22, but the drive home was a difficult and fearful one.
The smell of smoke was in the air, and grew stronger as they traveled westward
toward Roscoe.  The sun was a yellow ball, obscured by the smoke in the air,
and the team were restless and nervous about traveling west.  The first big
prairie fire they had experienced was racing before the wind, passing ahead of
them to the northeast, but since they had no way of knowing where it had
started nor how much damage it had done, the adults were nervous and fearful,
though the children, weary from the long trip, slept the last part of the
journey.

It was nearly dark when they were still about a mile from the claim, but with
eyes burning and throat smarting from the smoke they urged the team on.  Then
the smoke cleared enough so they could see the shack still standing.  Henry,
the boys, and a neighbor greeted them.  Some of the lumber had been burned,
there was a wide swathe of blackened grass where the fire had passed, and the
stock of hay the men had worked so hard to prepare for winter was gone, but the
cow, calf, and chickens were safe, and the family were all safe and well, so
their hearts were filled with praise and thanksgiving to God.  It was a
homecoming to remember always!

In the years that followed, Roscoe and the community grew and flourished.  The
number of homes and cultivated fields and gardens spread across the prairies
increased, children were born and grew, school and churches were established,
and the Roscoe Herald become known for its support of community progress. Harry
Huck, Joe Parmley, and others worked to encourage cultural projects and
legitimate businesses, as well as to establish good schools.

Harry organized the first Sunday School in Roscoe and taught the children to
sing, then assisted in building the churches and keeping the saloon out of
Roscoe.  Joe Parmley was the first Superintendent of Schools, and Harry was for
some time the Clerk of the District School Board.

He would buy books for the schools so that every child would have equal
opportunity to learn, regardless of the economic status of his parents.  He
joined the Farmers' Alliance, voted the prohibition ticket always, was
treasurer of several different organizations, and was Justice of the Peace for
many years.

As town, schools, churches and community were growing, so also was the Huck
family.  The first child born after they moved to Dakota was my father, William
Gladstone, on November 26, 1884.  A month before his second birthday, Joseph
Ernest was born, October 26, 1886, and two years later, on November 10, 1888,
Frances Annette.  Both William and Joe were blue-eyed, like Freddie and their
grandfather Little, but little Frances had snapping brown eyes like Polly and
little Avie.

        Milton Huck, Postmaster with first parcel post received in his post
        office, 1910.

Young people growing up in the Roscoe community worked hard, but there were
also many interesting and educational activities in which they shared.  I have
heard my Dad tell often of the spelling bees, debating clubs, the literary
society, and other activities, in addition to choir and other singing groups,
and occasional plays, programs, and other literary presentations. However,
school attendance was not at all regular, especially for the boys, who had
heavy responsibilities in tilling the soil, harvesting the crops, herding and
coring for the cattle, etc.  Yet in spite of the fact that my father, in common
with other boys of the community, was seldom able to attend school after he was
old enough to herd cattle (which was very young--probably no more than seven or
eight), or work in the fields at ten or twelve years, except during the winter
months when the weather was too bad for the cattle to graze and there was no
field work, he learned a prodigious amount of history, literature, and science,
for he was an avid reader.  The grades in the school were not arranged as they
are now, and a student went at his own speed in various subjects without
reference to his level in other subjects.  For instance, my father read through
all eight readers, and the eighth grade reader required a very high level of
proficiency.  Yet he never progressed beyond probably third grade level in
grammar and spelling.  Both Harry and Polly Huck encouraged their children to
study and learn all that they could, and several of them, including William,
also learned the skills necessary to publish a newspaper.

When Edmunds County was organized in 1883, the people of Roscoe hoped that
their town would be chosen as County seat, but that honor went to Ipswich,
which gave an impetus to the growth of that town.  But Harry continued to
encourage folks to come to Roscoe, both through newspaper articles and by giving
assistance to newcomers whenever possible.  It was through his encouragement
that the W. H. Elford family, whom he had known in Chicago, came to Roscoe, and
also the John Scholfield family, from Yorkshire, England.  John Scholfield was
the friend who had gone to Castleford with Harry many years ago.  He had
married Polly's older sister, Lizzie, and now the two old friends and the two
sisters were once again together.

The Huck family continued to live on the farm for fourteen years.  Harry Huck
had filed not only on a homestead, but also on a preemption and a tree claim,
so had more than just 160 acres, on which they raised cattle and crops.  Both
Walter and Fred had herds of cattle of their own.  In 1897, Al Jacobs resigned
from his position as Roscoe postmaster and Harry was appointed to the position.
So he bought the Hopkins Bank building from Jacobs and moved his family to
town. Walter and Fred sold their cattle and bought out a store in town, where
they opened the Huck Brothers Mercantile Store.  Harry continued to publish the
Roscoe Herald" in the some building in which it had opened, but the post office
was moved into the Huck Brothers Store, where Clara Huck acted as post office
clerk for her father.

Among other changes at this time, the old Methodist Church was repaired and
reorganized.  Several Methodist families who had been attending the
Presbyterian Church, the Hucks, Elfords, Deckers, Scholfields, and others
attended and helped in the services, and Harry became choir leader and Sunday
School teacher.  Through advertising in his newspaper, Harry furnished chairs,
pulpit, argon, and other furniture, and the repairs needed on the building were
accomplished by the work of the people themselves.

In November of 1899, the "Herald", in common with newspapers throughout the
land, was full of political news.  One evening, Mr. Huck and his son Fred went
down to the newspaper office to run off a few papers to get into the early
morning mail.  As they entered the dark office, Harry bumped against the door,
knocking from his hand an open can of kerosene which he was planning to use in
the kerosene lamp.  The kerosene spilled onto his clothes and the floor, and
perhaps on or very close to the hot stove with which the office was heated.
Fred ran back to the store to get more kerosene, but as he started back with
it, he heard screams.  Walter, who was in the store, looked out through the
window and saw a man running from the office building with his clothes in
flames.

Snatching his father's coat, which had been left in the store earlier, Walter
ran out and smothered the fire enveloping the victim.  Only after the fire was
out did he realize that the man he had rescued was his own father.  By that
time, Fred and many others had joined !hem, and compassionate hands did all
they could to help, although everyone was in a state of shock at the suddenness
of the tragedy.  There was not much the doctor or anyone else could do for one
so badly burned, although he lived for nearly three weeks after the accident
and seemed to be gaining for a time.  But the great loss of skin and the severe
shock to his nervous system were too much.  On November 24, 1899, Henry Huck
went to meet the Lord he had loved and served so faithfully.

His oldest son, Walter was chosen by the unanimous petition of the Roscoe
Community to take his father's place as postmaster.  For five years, Fred
continued to publish the "Roscoe Herald" with the help of other family members,
including his younger brother, Will (my father), who made good use of the
experience more than a decade later when he published the "Table Mountain
Beacon" in Harding County.  The family also continued to run the Huck Brothers
Mercantile Store and later I believe they ran a farm implement shop in Roscoe.

The town and community of Roscoe continued to grow, and sometime during the
first decade of the twentieth century, the Presbyterian Church called the
Reverend Carl Kearns of Iowa to the pastorate.  With his wife, Daisy, and small
daughter, Margaret, who had been born in Korea while he was serving there as a
missionary, the Reverend Kearns pastored the Roscoe church for several years.
He soon heard that an elementary school teacher was needed in Roscoe, so he
wrote to his younger sister, Grace Eleanor Kearns, who was a teacher, and
suggested that she might like to continue their pioneer heritage by joining him
in Roscoe.

Miss Kearns at once sent her application to the Roscoe School Board, was
accepted, and soon joined her brother and his family.  Life in Roscoe was quite
different from anything Miss Kearns had experienced in the well-established,
thoroughly civilized community where she had grown up, for Dakota was then in
the pioneer state Iowa had left behind long before she was born.  But although
she was a quiet, shy, reserved young woman, she had plenty of determination and
courage, and soon mode a place for herself in Roscoe.  As she become better
acquainted, some of her new friends began the age-old game of match-making.
After due consideration, they chose Milton, the middle one of the five Huck
brothers, as the best "match" for her, for he was active in the church, a good
worker, and a good singer.  So they waited patiently for the two to meet.

It was not long before Miss Kearns met Milton Huck and his two older brothers,
and her matchmaking friends watched eagerly to see what would happen.  But
there was nothing.  It was a pleasant and friendly encounter, but nothing more,
so the matchmakers were foiled.

However, a few weeks later, all the young people went to a basket social,
including Milton's younger brothers, William (or Will) and Joseph.  Will, whom
the matchmakers had not considered because he was not thought to be quite as
responsible as his older brother and was certainly not as active in the church,
took special note of the quiet, genteel young school teacher from Iowa, and
hoped to be able to buy her basket and eat supper with her.  Since he had no
way of knowing which basket was hers, he failed to bid on the right one, and
wound up eating supper with a girl he had known for a long time.  However, he
was not one to be defeated so easily, so he asked Miss Kearn's permission to
walk her home to the parsonage where she stayed with her brother and his family.

Grace considered his request, noting that he was taller than his older brother,
and quite nice-looking. His eyes were a clear blue, not brown like Milton's.
She usually liked brown eyes better, but somehow, as she hesitated, she
realized that she would like to know Will better, and found herself saying yes.

It was a frosty night, and they had not gone far down the path leading to the
parsonage when suddenly Grace slipped on a patch of ice, her feet flew out from
under her, and she found herself sitting down hard on the ground--in Will's
lap!  For a moment, she was terribly mortified, but Will helped her to her feet
with the most carefully respectful manner possible, as he said with a twinkle
in his eye, "When I realized I couldn't save you from falling, I decided the
best I could do was give you a soft place to land!"

With that, they both burst out laughing.  The remainder of the walk to the
parsonage was very enjoyable, and that was the beginning of a relationship that
ripened into love.  When Western South Dakota was opened for homesteading, Will
and Grace, who by then were "promised", though not officially engaged, decided
that homesteading would be the best possible way to get land of their own on
which to build their home.  Will liked farming much better than running a
store, and Grace was also from a farming background, so the dream of
homesteading on their own land was very important to both.  However, they
decided to wait until a little more information about the country was available
before making any move.  A few months later, probably early in 1910, Will's
youngest sister, Frances, filed on a homestead in Harding County, the first of
the Huck family to do so.

Sometime in late summer or early fall, Frances learned that one of the many
one-room country schools which served the children of homesteaders scattered
across the Harding County plains had no teacher for the coming term.  She wrote
to Grace at once, and when the school term began in 1910, Miss Grace Kearns of
Iowa was teaching in Harding County, South Dakota, while living in a small
tar-paper shack on her claim not far from, the North Dakota line, walking three
miles twice each day to her school.  In 1911, Will followed her out to Harding
County and filed on a homestead as near hers as possible, though not adjoining
hers, as another homesteader had that claim, but Will saw that as no problem.
He wanted plenty of land, and he had no doubt that he would buy the land lying
between their claims when the time was right.  In the meantime, he would build
the required dwelling house and barn, living on and cultivating the land for
the period of time specified by the Homestead Law.

In 1913, when Grace had lived on her claim the required three years, the land
became hers.  Then she and Will could be married and live together on his claim
while he fulfilled his time.  This is exactly what they did. On Will's birthday
(which was also Thanksgiving Day that year), November 26, 1913, William
Gladstone Huck and Grace Eleanor Kearns became man and wife. They went back to
the Kearns home in Iowa for the wedding, the bride's older brother, the
Reverend Carl Kearns, performed the ceremony, and his little daughter,
Margaret, was flower girl.

Back again on the claim, with the help of a team of horses and some neighbors,
Will brought his bride's claim shack to his place and joined it to his own shack
to create for them a two-room house.

Thirteen months later, December 23, 1914, I was born in the bedroom that had
been Mother's claim shack.  I was the oldest of the family of six children born
to this man and woman whose lives first touched each other in Roscoe, South
Dakota.

(Ed.  Note: Henry and Mary Ann Huck are referred to as Harry and Polly by this
writer)

        H. M. Huck Store. Begin fifth from right: Beth and Milton Huck, Mrs.
        Henry Huck, Joe and Inda Huck, Frances Huck (driver in buggy).

        Local roundhouse, 1945.

THE RAILROAD COMES TO ROSCOE

The railroad was instrumental in establishing and building most towns along its
route.

Although Roscoe was founded in 1883, the railroad did not play as big a role in
its establishment as it did for other nearby communities.  However, had the
railroad chosen another route west Roscoe may not have remained at its present
site.  The rail system reached Ipswich in 1883 and it wasn't until 1886 that
the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul extended its system west to Roscoe.

        Train derailment west of Roscoe 1938.

With the railroad in Roscoe, residents were able to ship and receive goods, as
well as travel, without journeying to Ipswich or Aberdeen to make connections.

Passenger trains had their regular routes through Roscoe, enabling the local
townspeople to travel eastward without tremendous hardship.  Special excursion
trains ran on holidays taking folks from area communities to Ipswich and
sometimes Aberdeen and back.  For the July 4, 1900 celebration in Ipswich the
train departed from Roscoe at 8:30 a.m. and left Ipswich for the return trip at
6:45 p.m.

        Morning Passenger, 1908

        South Train, 1908

        Old depot and coal sheds, 1909, depot was located about 2 1/2 blocks
        west of Main Street, coal sheds were south of the main line.

Usage of the railroad declined with the advent of the trucking industry
and the automobile.  The Milwaukee Road and other rail lines began abandoning
routes as other modes of transportation became well-established and offered an
alternate mode of transportation giving more flexibility in rates, routes and
scheduling.

        Depot after it was moved to south side of Main line track (in 1909-10)
        near Main Street (Note: building has been turned around.)Milton Huck
        with suitcase speaking to M. T. Lightner.

        Train derailment west of Roscoe 1938.

South Dakota Main Line, (which passes through Roscoe), from Ortonville,
Minnesota to Miles City, Montana was abandoned by the Milwaukee Road in 1982.
The Main Line was purchased by the State of South Dakota and is now operated by
the Burlington Northern Railroad.

        1942 coal shed fire.

        Engine upset, Nov. 1946, Albert Meier.

        Raising engine with crane 1946.

        Railroad yards, looking east, 1910

        The end of an era, last Milwaukee depot moved out on Oct. 27, 1982.

WATER RELATED ITEMS

        This document was submitted by Paul Elford, testifying to the means
        used to solve the water problems in the winter of 1899-90.  The above
        named subscribers agreed to pay designated amounts into city treasury
        monthly for six months to keep the city well in running order.

        Old water tank with concrete base and town well located just east of
        old fire hall.  Mazzini Scholfield and Lloyd Finder getting water.

In 1887 the first artesian well was sunk at a cost of $2,500.00. A bond issue
was used to pay for this well which proved to be insufficient. A second well was
drilled in 1907, at a cost of $500.00. This well was powered with a windmill
and was 400 feet deep. This provided an ample supply of water for that time.

In 1947 the people of Roscoe decided to improve and upgrade their way of life
by providing a city water and sewer system. The taxpayers went to the polls and
passed a bond issue in the amount of $162,000.00 for this system. About 195
homes and many of  the businesses were hooked up to the system as it was
installed in 1948 and 1949.

In 1959 the city bought a railroad well and a 100,000 gallon elevated storage
tank for $2,200.00 with annual lease payments for the property on which the
well and tank was located.

In late 1961 and early 1962 the main railroad well was totally redone with a
new screen, casing and pump at the cost of $4,235.98.

During the late 1970s two new pumps were purchased and installed to replace the
older style line turbine pumps in both the wells.  The new pumps met state
codes and were more efficient.

In August of 1981 the city met with a well drilling firm in regard to the
oldest of the two wells, the problem being that this well was producing
approximately 26 gallons per minute which was less than one-half its normal
pumping rate.  The council decided to have some work done on this well in an
attempt to rehabilitate it. After nearly a weeks work on the well the council
was informed that the cost of rehabilitating this well exceeded its usefulness,
as it would have a cost of up to $70,000.00. At this point of time the council
began dealing with the Geological Survey, Department of Water and Natural
Resources and several other well drillers and engineers in regard to the future
water development of Roscoe.  It was necessary to abandon the well, as it would
have served no purpose to the community to put any amount of money into it.

December 31, 1981, well No. 1, the city's only source of water quit pumping
unexpectedly.  It caught the town with only a partial tank of water and the
100,000 gallon tank frozen.

Over the next month the residents of Roscoe faced one of the most critical
problems in its history.  Due to the lock of water and pressure in the mains,
when water was put back into the system many mains broke. This was complicated
by high winds, below zero temperatures, and wind chill readings of more than
-100 degrees.

A new well, for emergency use only, was started January 7, 1982.  Water was
hauled in from Bowdle, Faulkton and Ipswich in an effort to keep enough water
in town for domestic use and a small amount of fire protection.  Residents were
asked not to do laundry, use woodburning stoves, and limit the amount of water
used for their daily needs.  The new well was completed January 21, 1982.
School had to be closed for much of this time in an attempt to keep water usage
at a low level.  The cost of drilling the well and hauling water exceeded
$45,000.00.

On February 16, 1982, a bond election was held for $150,000.  The bond was
overwhelmingly defeated, but due to the financial strain put onto the city
council during the water problems, another election was held. After an
informational meeting at the school gym, explaining the need and the council's
plans, the election received a favorable vote, 205 for and 49 against.

        East water tower, 1959

During the summer, the council, their engineer, the Northeast Council of
Governments and the Oahe Board, worked with Farmers Home Administration on a
loan, the Department of Water and Natural Resources on a low interest loan and
a Block Grant.  The city received an $80,000.00 grant from HUD for their
project, and a loan from FMHA for $90,000.00 at eight and three-eighths percent
interest.  The city also received a $9,040.00 grant from the Oahe Conservancy
Sub-District for development of a new well field and transmission line.

On September 20, 1982, bids were opened for the construction of a new well for
the City of Roscoe.  On September 22, 1982, the contract was awarded to Leonard
T. Pulkrabeck of L. T. P. Enterprises Inc. of Fargo, N. D. to construct an
eight inch well and supply other needed equipment at the well location.  Work
started October 12, 1982 and was completed December 3, 1982.  The contract was
in the amount of $35,524.50.

On October 14, 1982, bids were opened for the construction of the transmission
line into town and the replacement of approximately 15 blocks of water mains in
town.  A contract was awarded October 18, 1982 to Stanley Hieb of Hieb
Excavating of Eureka, S.D. Work started on the transmission line October 27,
1982.  The contract called for a two phased project. Phase one had to be
completed by December 15, 1982. This part of the project was the transmission
line to town.  Phase two was to replace the old water mains in town.  This part
of the project must be completed by July 30,  1983.  Hieb Excavating's contract
was for $100,456.32.

On December 3, 1982, at approximately 4:30 p.m., the new well was placed in
service.  The well is pumping 240 gallons of water per minute into the high
tower. According to the water samples taken during actual construction of the
well, the water quality is very good. The well has the capacity of pumping in
excess of 750 gallons per minute.  The new well and related improvements should
serve the future water supply demands and requirements for many decades to come.

        West water tower, 1959.

Roscoe Water Crisis Attracts National Attention!!
MAJOR TELEVISION NETWORKS AND "BIG CITY" NEWSPAPERS
PICK UP OUR WATER EMERGENCY

        Much activity at the west water tower during water "outage".

The City of Roscoe and its' citizens received national recognition when our
last well and only source of water ceased to function as the New Year (I 982)
began.  As the townspeople came to realize that this was not just an ordinary
water break with the prospect of water service being restored in a matter of a
few hours, the News Media also became aware of the existence of a hazardous
situation in Roscoe.  Normal household and business functions were not possible
without the use of water.  The added fear of being caught without adequate fire
protection was possibly the greatest cause for concern among the people.

However, as always in the past, the people rose to the situation and found ways
to improvise and meet the emergency head-on.  The few private well owners made
their wells available to everyone, snow was melted by the tubsful, (we were
"fortunate" to have enough of that! ( Water was hauled in from nearby farms and
whatever other means of obtaining water were thought of, were used.

        Councilman Dean Lehr and Mayor Treichel at site of drilling.

The News Media sent their "reporters" to Roscoe throughout these days to get
the story for their network or publication.  They interviewed our City
officials and many citizens of the Roscoe community, and one of the highlights
of each day became the "evening news" and the newspapers to see whose familiar
faces had been captured today!

Through the untiring efforts of our City officials, the cooperation of the
citizens of Roscoe, and the aid of our good neighbors, Ipswich, Bowdle and
Faulkton, in a few days time, the musical sound of precious water could again
be heard trickling out of our faucets.  The reporters and TV cameras were
withdrawn and went once again in search of new stories to tell.  The citizens
of Roscoe heaved a sigh of relief and settled back into normalcy and to
contemplate the gravity of the difficult situation they had survived, and to
chuckle with warm good humor about the lighthearted atmosphere and the close
kinship we all experienced throughout this shored, trying time.

And so was added, another chapter to the history of Roscoe----

        Tank trucks brought water In from neighboring towns.

        Many Roscoe residents remembered with deep longing, the good old days,
        with its luxury of the little "palace" out-back !

        Drilling in progress at site of new permanent well east of town.

ROSCOE HERALD Jan. 16, 1890
Our attention was recently called to a letter that was received in Roscoe in
something less than three days from the time it was mailed in the post office
at New York City.  In these days of rapid transit, such dispatch rarely excites
notice or comment, yet it strikingly illustrates the progress made by the world
in this twilight of the nineteenth century.

ROSCOE HERALD Nov. 13, 1890
J. A. Procter, trapped a large prairie wolf a few nights ago, a short distance
west of his home.  The scalp brings $3.00 in bounty and the hide is worth
$1.50. Mr. Procter thinks the chances are favorable for securing a number of
these animals this winter and expects to derive quite a revenue from the
business.

ROSCOE VOLUNTEER FIRE DEPARTMENT

        Chemical Fire Engine No. 2 purchased in 1916

The Roscoe Volunteer Fire Department came into existence on the evening of
February 27, 1918.  A meeting was held that date and the department was
organized.  Officers chosen at this first meeting were as follows: Chairman,
William Finder; Secretary, William Christen; Treasurer, Herman Tschirley.  C.
L. Pratt, M. Scholfield, H. Tschirley, and W. Christen were appointed as the
committee to draw up a Constitution for the department.

March 5, 1918 was the date of the second meeting of the department.  The
Constitution was adopted at this meeting and is still the governing document,
with amendments.  J. B. Wold was chosen as the first Fire Chief at this second
meeting.  J. S. Schumacher was chosen as Captain of Engine No. 1 and H. J.
Schipke was chosen as Captain of Engine No. 2.

Members of the department were required to donate $1 to the Fire Department
when they joined.  This practice still exists today.  Paid up members of the
deportment in 1918 were: J. B. Wold, E. Hofer Jr., W. Rivimius, J. Lehman, J.
S. Schumacher, W. Christen, P. Malsam, M. Scholfield, H. Wiedemeier, C.
Schumacher, J. E. Evans, L. Devries, H. J. Schipke, T. Ellwein, O. Wudel, W.
Erlenbusch, W. L. Johnson, W. Finder, J. Scherr, W. L. McCafferty, G. Wynn, C.
Grandahl, G. Price, C. L. Pratt, G. Schmaltz, H. Tschirley, G. Wilson, E.
Licht, J. Schumacher, B. H. Blake, F. B. Huck, A. Bahr, F. A. Price, C. Wynn
and G. Lehman.

William Finder served as chairman from 1918 to 1930.  Charles Christen then
served the longest term as chairman.  His term ran from 1930 to 1947.  Chairmen
from this time on are as follows: Paul Elford, 1947 to 1951; William Lammle,
1951 to 1957; Eugene Job, 1957 to 1958; M. R. Lammle, 1958 to 1959; Milbert
Ellwein, 1959 to 1963; Alvin Schmitt, 1963 to 1967; Harold Sahli, 1967 to 1970;
Orville Schneider, 1970 to 1972; Jerome Prisbe, 1972 to 1973; Harold Sahli,
1973 to 1975; Jerome Fischer, 1975 to 1976 and Ervin Spitzer, 1976 to 1977.

Fire Chiefs over the years were as follows: J. B. Wold, 1918 to 1930; George
Wilson, 1930 to 1938; August Adam, 1938 to 1948; Albert Meier, 1948 to 1949;
Minor Larson, 1949 to 1953; Milbert Ellwein, 1953 to 1958; Robert Auchampach,
1958 to 1965; Harold Margeson, 1965 to 1967; Leo Himmerich, 1967 to 1970; Harold
Peterson, 1970 to 1973; Leo Himmerich, 1973 to 1975; Alvin Schmitt, 1975 to
1976 and Gary Adam, 1976 to the present time.

        1982 Officers: Marvin Uhrich, 2nd Ass't.  Chief, Gary Adam, Fire Chief,
        Dean Lehr, 1st Ass't. Chief, and Harry Himmerich, Sec. and Treas.

The Secretary and Treasurer positions were combined in 1931.  Herman Tschirley
served as Treasurer from 1918 to 1920; Jacob Lehman, 1920 to 1927 and Henry
Schipke, 1927 to 1931.  The Secretary position was held by William Christen
from 1918 to 1931.  Upon combining the two positions, William Christen served
as Secretary and Treasurer until 1939.  Others holding the combined positions
through the years are as follows: Martin Adam, 1939 to 1948; Harvey Haug, 1948
to 1954; Ray Murray, 1954 to 1955; Harold Peterson, 1955 to 1957; Emmett
Johnson, 1957 to 1958; Robert Blumhardt, 1958 to 1960; Edwin Dabbert, 1960 to
1961; Harold Sahli, 1961 to 1963; Edwin Dabbert, 1963 to 1968; John Beyers,
1968 to 1970; Alton Schnabel, 1970 to 1975 and Harry Himmerich, 1975 to the
present time.

        New fire hall

        Firefighting equipment and officers

        Answering a fire call

Present officers of the Fire Department are: Fire Chief, Gary Adam; First
Assistant Fire Chief, Dean Lehr; Second Assistant Fire Chief, Marvin Uhrich;
Secretary and Treasurer, Harry Himmerich.

The first method of fighting fires in Roscoe was the bucket brigade.  Business
places had 50 gallon barrels of water on hand, also buckets to dip the water
from the barrels to use on the fire.  Two chemical fire engines were purchased
to combat fires.  The first engine was obtained in 1908 and the second was
purchased in 1916.  A wooden tank was used to supply water for the two engines.
This tank was on wheels and was purchased in about 1910.

Fire fighting was carried out with the use of the two chemical engines until
1948.  A fully equipped International fire truck was purchased in 1948. This is
the white truck which is still with the fire department today.  Another fire
truck was purchased in 1962.  This was also an International truck.  This is
the red truck, which is the fire department's main fire fighting vehicle today.
The fire department has obtained two vehicles from the Forestry Division,
which are being used to aid in fighting fires.  One of the vehicles received
from the Forestry Division was a Jeep, CM 2 vehicle.  The other vehicle is a CM
2 vehicle used to transport water.  The Jeep was obtained in 1979 and the water
transport in 1980.

The fire department has had two panel trucks in the past.  One of these panels
was purchased in 1950.  The other panel was obtained in about 1970.  These two
vehicles were used to transport hoses and fire fighting equipment.

Funds were obtained from the City of Roscoe and the surrounding townships to
build a new fire hall.  This fire hall has been used to house fire trucks and
equipment since March of 1981.

These are some items of interest that probably should be mentioned.  These
items came from the minutes of the fire department.  A meeting of the fire
department took place February 4, 1920 and was called to order by President
Finder.  Upon motion the meeting was adjourned until a later date because of
the "Flu" epidemic.

President Finder retired from the fire deportment March 5, 1930.  Finder was
given a walking cane for his service as President.

The fire department decided to pass out Christmas bags and give a free show to
children 15 years and under.  This decision was made at the December 5, 1936
meeting.  The show was "Popeye".

The first firemen's feed was held March 2, 1921.  This feed has been an annual
event since that time.

Fire has been responsible for the loss of much property in Roscoe and the
surrounding area.  The Roscoe Volunteer Fire Department has been active and
still is active in keeping the dangers of fire to a minimum.

        Old fire hall

LAW ENFORCEMENT

        Ed Schumacher, Chief of Police for 32 years.

On September 15, 1979, after several years of difficulty in maintaining
adequate law enforcement, the Roscoe community entered into a contract agreement
with the Edmunds County law Enforcement program.

        Phil Toft, Mike Nehls, Randy Majeske, Mike Olson, Jon Olson, Darlene
        Diode and Bet Hoyle.

        Jake Geist, City Marshall in 1935, with daughters Irene and Vivian

This program is operated out of the County Sheriff's Department.  At the
present time Roscoe, Ipswich and Bowdle are contracting for this type of law
enforcement.  Each community contracts for a given amount of patrol time each
week.

The County Sheriff is Dennis (Mike) Olson.  The Deputies are Randy Majeske,
resident in Roscoe, Mike Nehls, resident in Ipswich and Phil Toft, resident in
Bowdle.

ROSCOE HERALD--Henry Huck, Editor
August 21, 1896
The Roscoe and Huntley baseball nines played another game on the ground of the
former, which resulted in a victory for the home club, the score being 28-36.

Dec. 20, 1901
It is reported that C. E. Kittenger has purchased the 100 ft. lot south of the
Post office and will build a large double building thereon, using part for a
store and living room and part for a town hall and bank.

March 15, 1901
Adolph Jeschke has quit the Edmunds to go onto the farm.

        City Tennis Court

        New shelters in City Park 1982

March 29, 1901
Thos.  Lettis is about to commence the erection of a first class restaurant
building with living rooms in connection, on the lot north of the Post office.
Fred Matz purchased a new Crescent bicycle from Huck Bros. last Tuesday.  Quite
a number of Crescents are in use around Roscoe now.  Better get in line and
ride a "Crescent".

April 19, 1901
Mr. Walker, the General excursion agent for the Chicago, Milwaukee and St. Paul
Railroad, informs us that they will run six, ten car passenger trains through
Roscoe to Evarts and points west May 26th.  The fare will be a very low rate,
he says he has no doubt they will be obliged to increase the number of trains to
accommodate the people who will want to take advantage of the cheap rate, to
see our beautiful country and others who will want to come and see friends.

As I Remember
BY PAUL ELFORD

        Paul Elford

Since Time and the Centennial Committee have asked me to write my remembrances
of the first 100 years of Roscoe.  Through my personal knowledge and the help
of history, I will attempt to do the best I can as there is no one I can ask.

Roscoe was founded by J. W. Parmley and Chas P. Morgan.  Mr. Parmley came from
Mifflin, Wisconsin and Mr. Morgan was from Chicago, Illinois.  They met the
first part of April 1883 walking out from Aberdeen, which was the end of the
railroad.

Mr. Morgan filed on the land which is now Morgan's addition to Roscoe.  Parmley
filed on the quarter west of Morgan's which is now part of the original town of
Roscoe.  The name of Roscoe was of Roscoe Conkling who was a Senator from New
York and an uncle of Mr. Morgan.

They set up two tents about the center of Block 15 of Morgan's addition.  One
of the tents was for people and the other was for horses.  The tents were on
the main traveled route from Aberdeen to LeBeau on the Missouri River.  LeBeau
was a river town that had been started earlier as a trading post for trappers
and buffalo hunters.

Edmunds County was surveyed in July 1883, by Mr. Turner and Mr. Parmley.  Mr.
Parmley was appointed the first school Superintendent.  He walked to the
different neighborhoods organizing schools. The town of Roscoe was surveyed and
laid out in lots in 1884.

Mr. Henry Huck, a printer from Chicago, came about the same time as Mr.
Parmley.  Mr. Huck had brought a printing press and some type with him and he
and Mr. Parmley started a newspaper--The Roscoe Herald, which was the first
paper published in Edmunds County, September 25, 1883.

Mr. Huck was a religious man and organized the first Sunday School in Roscoe.

Mr. John Rice, who homesteaded west of Roscoe was appointed the first County
Judge.

Mr. Morgan was the first Post Master. Roscoe Main Street was laid out where
Highway No. 12 is.

Mr. Rice built a store on the corner where Donnie's Restaurant is now.

School was held upstairs in Rice's Store.  Several other buildings were built
on both sides of what was then Main Street.

Edmunds House, a hotel and rooming house was built where later Spitzer's
Implement was.

The Roscoe House, a rooming house, was built where The Farmer's Union is now.
Next was a store run by Zack Sutley.

        The Sutley House, site of the first town election in 1886.

On the south side of the street was the Town Hall (Hooper Hall), the Huck Print
Shop and a Pool Hall.

When the Railroad came through in 1886, business places started to be built on
what is now Main Street.

The first bank building was built by Mr. Hopkins in the building where
Tschirley had a seed store.

The first church service was held September 9, 1883, by Mr. Johnston, a
Missionary Minister from LeBeau.

The first child born in Roscoe was a boy born to the Lewis family and named
Roscoe in November 1884.

The first legal meeting to form a town government was held in the Sutley House
November 11, 1886.  Edgar Sharpe, Geo.  B. Ranshaw and Oscar Sutley were
elected officials.  At their first meeting November 29, 1886, a permanent
organization was set up with John R. Smith moving that Zack F. Sutley be named
the first president of the Board of Roscoe for the term of one year and that
Edgar Sharpe and John Harrington be the first Attorney and Clerk respectively.

Moses Stanley operated the first lumber yard in 1884, hauling lumber from
Ipswich and Aberdeen.  He also organized a library in 1884 in J. N. Heal's
store.  His son, Orin, was the Librarian.

In 1884 0. C. Gibson had his home and a livery barn across south from the
present Roscoe Auto Co. site.

Ed Armstrong had a dray line and a harness shop north of the Edmunds House.

Jack Wake had his first hardware store where the Farmer's Union is now.

The first saloon was next to Wake's Store.

J. N. Heal's dry goods store was west of the saloon.

In 1886, the entire block of buildings except the Edmunds House, was burned.

In 1887,  Jack Wake rebuilt his hardware store on what is now Main Street where
Adams Electric now is.

Madge Trunkey was the first girl born in Roscoe, June 1885.  Her mother worked
in the Post Office for Mr. Morgan.

In 1883 Sam Basford who homesteaded Basfords addition to Roscoe, built the
first rooming house in Roscoe between Aberdeen and LeBeau.

Shafer Bros, had the first Butcher Shop next to Wake's Store.

In 1890, Roscoe celebrated the first Fourth of July in the State of South
Dakota.

        Julia and Kasper Sahli in their cream Station, late 1920.

In 1892, Ed Hamill and Elmer Lewis opened a new dry- goods store where the
Roscoe Hardware is now.

In 1887, Roscoe had one bank, two blacksmith shops, two clothing stores, two
coal and lumber dealers, a drugstore, a hardware store and two saloons.  Six men
practicing low, J. W. Harrington, John Rudd, Edgar Sharp, Hughes, Wantler and
Bradshaw.  Bradshaw also published the Roscoe Magnet.

W. S. Campbell ran the Roscoe Herald after Henry Huck's death.

A. J. Gibson was the only doctor then.  He is buried in the northwest corner of
the cemetery.

In 1889, the hotels were Sutley House, Dakota House and Hotel Ripley.  H. E.
Heald ran the Ripley Hotel.  J. B. Johnson ran the Dakota House and the Sutley
House was run by the Sutley family.

In 1889 Zack Sutley was the Postmaster.

In 1886, the Roscoe Creamery Co. was formed as a Co-op. The meeting was held in
the M. T. Lightner office. The director of the Milbank Creamery was present.
Officers were elected and the equipment ordered from the Jack Wake Hardware.

The Creamery Building was located east of town near where Fred Sauers house
was.  It was later moved east of Beebe, but used only a few years when cream
buyers started buying for larger creameries.

Lightner and Bickelhaupt were grain buyers and had their first house south of
the bank where the loading platform was.

When the railroad was built to Eureka, Lightner and Bickelhoupt started buying
grain in Eureka which later became the greatest primary wheat market in the
world.

In 1883, the first bank was owned by Chas Hopkins and was in the building where
Tschirley's Seed Store was.  It was later sold to Hicks Bros. who moved it into
the Kittenger Building, (The West Hotel).  It was later sold to M. T. Lightner,
Ward McCafferty, J. W. Jamieson and W. F. Pratt.  McCafferty was the cashier,
Lightner was president.  When Theo Meunz bought the building and made it into a
hotel, the bank was moved to it's present location.  In May 1906, the stock was
purchased by R. W. Sherin and Cecil Sherin.  In November of 1906, the bank was
purchased by E. Keller and H. A. Warner and Chas E. Christen.  E. Keller was
elected president, H. A. Warner was vice president and Chas Christen was
cashier.

In July of 1954, the controlling interest of the bank was sold to A. N.
Johnson.  September 15, 1956, Emmett Johnson joined the bank as assistant
cashier and director.

Henry Schipke become associated with the bank in 1912 and was elected cashier
in 1944, a position he held until his death in 1951.

Our Lively ELDERS

        Banking brothers, Charles W. Christen and William Christen, have been
        associated with  the First State Bank of Roscoe, S. D., as long as a
        lot of people can remember.  Charles, now 77, was one of three
        purchasers of the bank in 1903 and was its manager until late summer,
        when he sold controlling interest. William, now 70, joined the bank in
        1909, became cashier in 1927 and worked full time until last summer.
        Both continue as officers and directors and are seen regularly around
        the bank. Taken from Minneapolis Tribune in 1954.

Harold Margeson has been associated with the bank as assistant cashier and
director for about 35 years.

1884--March 3. The first meeting of Glen School District was held.  Officers
elected were Eli Mack, H. H. Aldrich, Joel Proctor and Elmer Lewis.  First
business was the sale of bonds to build a school worth $800.  It was built
where the Lutheran Church now stands.  When the new school was built in 1888,
it was moved three miles west of Roscoe and was known as the Snell School.
Again it was moved back to Roscoe and is still used as a dwelling.

The lumber for the new school building was from G. J. Barker Lumber Yard in
Roscoe and cost $197.20. The carpenter work was $97.  Digging cellar was $8.31.
Mason work, building chimney, plastering, building foundation was $122.60.
Putting in seats and cleaning was $3.  The furniture was purchased from the U.S.
Furniture Co. and cost $136.75. It was completed August 28, 1884.

Mattie Welch was the first teacher.  She was paid $60 for September and October.

The next school house was built in 1888 and was where the shop and bus storage
are.  It was two story and had four rooms.

It was in use for many years until the present school was built in 1918. The
cost of the new school was to be $35,000.  John Bishop was chairman of the
board.

The building was completed in 1920.  The first graduating class was in 1922
with Martin Scherf, Donald Price, John Scofield and Ardan McCafferty.
