In 1920, two country schools were built, Bishop School north of Roscoe, and the
Houck School northwest of Roscoe.  The Dunn School, four miles west of Roscoe
was built in 1934.

This school was built of material from the old Roscoe School.  It was a WPA
project.  These country schools were necessary on account of the bad roads in
bad weather.

In 1953, the grade addition was built at a cost of $60,000.  In 1904 John
Carrol and Robert Baldwin put in a stock-dipping tank east of town where the
Fair grounds were.  It was then necessary to have your cattle and sheep dipped
before they could be sold or shipped on account of Texas ticks supposed to be
among the stock at that time.  That some year, Carrol and Baldwin built a
livery barn on north Main St. where Bill Lammle had his shop.

There were four restaurants in town, three on Main St. and Doctor Phillips
south of the old depot where he fed most of the railroad men.  One day in
September 1907, Phillips Restaurant fed 11 train crews plus regular boarders,
making 110 meals.  September 4, 1908, they served 50 prairie chicken dinners.
One restaurant on Main St. was where the liquor store was.  One was north of
Sims Store.  The other was where Eddie Dabbert's store now is.

There are lots of changes in businesses--too many to write about but noticeable
is the shopping customs. Farmers did come in on Saturday night to trade and the
stores would be open until midnight.  They now close at 5 p.m. on Saturday.
Shopping is Wednesday, Friday and Saturday mornings.

Most of the merchants lived upstairs over their stores.

When Mr. Finder came to town, there was a building boom of better homes.  Mr.
Finder was a stone mason and plasterer from Wisconsin.  He built his home on
Main Street where Sahli's store now is.  One of his daughters was a milliner
and used the front room of their house for her shop.  The family lived in the
back and upstairs.  The front room was later used for a doctor's office and
telephone office.

Dan Lief, a grain buyer, built a house north on Main Street.

Henry and Robert LeFebvre built a brick bank building on Main Street.  Henry
built a home where Dave Jeschke lives.  His brother, Robert, built the house
where Tschirley lived.  Joe and Art Hartelt came from Minnesota and built a
hardware store where Vanghans store had burned.  Joe lived upstairs over the
store and Arthur built the house on North Main where Robert Schurr lives.  W.
H. Elford built the house where Ken Wiedrich lives.  W. C. Elford, my brother,
built the house south of it where Winkler lives and Harland Allen built the
house where Will Christens lived.

        Robert LeFebvre, Mourke LeFebvre, Henry LeFebvre and his wife Rosetta
        LeFebvre.

The house where August Schumachers lived was built for a parsonage for the
Methodist Church.

Most of the carpenter work on these houses was done by Mr. F. A. Price.  There
were no more buildings in that part of town until Mr. Tower built his house
west of Elford's.  I know when these houses were built because Howard Price and
I mixed the cement and plaster and carried the bricks for Mr. Finder.  Jack
Wake's house was built much earlier where Price lived.  It is now a parking lot
for the school.  Across west of the street was the Stevenson house.  When the
new school was built, it was moved by C. P. Decker to a lot on Main St. and
Florence Mahon lived in it until she moved into a retirement home.  The
Stevenson house was then torn down.  Two other houses in that block were torn
down when the school was built--the Matz house and Miss Fewins, a teacher had a
small house there.

East of the school house was a house built by Fred Huck.  There were no other
buildings in that block.  In the next block south was the Methodist Church where
the Library now stands.  East of the church was the Vanghan house.  In the
block south of that was the Thos Lettis and Brandburg houses.  Chas Christen
and Henry Schipke built homes in that block.

In the block west of the Presbyterian Church was the Presbyterian parsonage.
In the block south of the church was the Jake Schumacher house where George
Price lived.  It was moved in from the Julius Schipke farm when they built
their new house.  There was also a blacksmith shop on the southwest corner of
the block. The Finder house in the some block was built by Mrs. Finder after
her husband died.  There were several more houses.  The next time there was any
building was when Martin Scherf built several houses, that are still in use.
In 1981, there were nine new houses built in Roscoe!  There were a number of
small houses where the section men lived but none of them are there now. They
were in the southern port of town near the railroad.

The one I recall the best was Mike Bolan.  He had several sons and they all
worked for the railroad.

The Railroad came to Roscoe in 1886 and went west to Bowdle.  There were a
number of people who came here but did not stay as good wells were hard to get
so many went on to Bowdle or Eureka area where good water was closer to the
surface, as they had no machinery to dig wells but had to dig them by hand.

Most of the good free land was homesteaded soon after that.  My folks filed on
the southeast quarter of Section 18, Bryant Twp.  M. T. Lightner was our closest
neighbor to the south and Sam Dalton to the west where Ed Werre now lives.

I knew most of the people who lived around Roscoe then as people didn't go for
with horse and buggy.  The road to Roscoe from the northeast ran between our
house and barn.  There were no fences so the roads did not follow the section
lines as they do now.

        Mr. and Mrs. W. H. Elford, early pioneers, 1893.

The place where George Roth lives was the Joel Proctor homestead.  He was
supposed to be the grandfather of the Proctor of Proctor and Gamble soap.

North of the cemetery was the Hunneywell place. There were trees there and many
picnics were held there as well as at the Proctor Grove.

There were others that went into the ranching business.  Bundy and Kennedy had
a sheep ranch where Johnny Kranzler lived.  Sherman had a ranch south of Roscoe
where Rohrbach lives.

Hooper and Regan had a big ranch northeast of Roscoe near the county line.

Cushing had a ranch north of Roscoe that had a spring on it.  If us kids worked
hard we could take our lunch and the horse and buggy and go up there to the
spring for a picnic.  It was a sight to see the clear cold water coming out of
the ground by itself.  The spring is in the Donnie Roesch pasture.

The Silver Spring ranch was southwest of Roscoe in Cloyd Valley Township.

There was a claim shanty on almost every quarter section of land.  After they
proved up, many left.  Some sold the land, others rented it.  They went back to
greener fields.  Most of the shanties were small and were burned by prairie
fires.  Others were torn down and the lumber used by neighbors.  Those that
were in fair condition were bought by neighbors who planned to stay there

The railroad was a big thing for Roscoe. it gave work to a number of people
with regular salaries.

Then they built the railroad north and south of Roscoe there were two crews
that were stationed in Roscoe as each branch line had a train daily.  With two
or three men in the cool-shed and two with the water pumping plant and two in
the roundhouse.

There were two section crews, with eight or nine men in each crew.  One was
east and the other was west from the town.  All work was done by hand as there
was no machinery or tools to use.  There was a depot agent and three telegraph
operators, and a yard man who kept the depot and the yards clean.  The next
year the railroad was built through to Evarts on the Missouri River.  Those
were interesting times.  There were days in the spring when cattle were shipped
up from Texas and were being taken to ranches west of the Missouri River. In
the fall they were shipped east to market.  There would be as many as 20 train
loads in a day going through Roscoe.  The trains were about 20 cars long as
they had small engines and light rails.  At Evarts if the water wasn't too high
they swam across.  Later they had pontoon bridges.

Nearly every fall there would be a wreck of one of these trains west of Roscoe
and long horn cattle would be roaming all over.  They would have to be rounded
up and reloaded at Roscoe.

One year when the railroad was being rebuilt they had a work crew at Roscoe.
They built Dago Spur west of the roundhouse to put their bunk cars on.  Most of
them could not speak English so we had fun trying to talk to them.  I don't
know their nationality but we called them Dagos.  One day they shot some owls
and they called them "Big Eyed Chicks".

There are lots of memories of the early days--too many to write about here.  A
few people that I remember the best were Mr. Chappell, who was a house mover
and dray man and Mr. Price, who was a carpenter and did most of the building in
those days. We had no undertaker, so Mr. Price made most of the coffins, laid
the bodies out and conducted the funerals. Mrs. Matz was a midwife and
delivered most of the babies in those days.

In 1937, the Chamber of Commerce was organized.  W. C. Peterson was the
organizing chairman, Geo Wilson was elected president and W. C. Peterson vice
president.  Wm.  Lammle, secretary and treasurer.  The executive committee was
Otto Noehre, Martin Adam, Chris Schumacher and Paul Elford.  It is still active.

In 1937, the American Legion Post was organized with members from Bowdle and
Loyalton.  August 4, 1937, the first meeting was held in the City Hall.
Officers were elected.  They were Paul Elford, commander; Chas Fischer, vice
commander; E. E. Curtis, adjutant; Barney Miller, finance officer; W. C.
Peterson, chaplain; John Roesch, sgt. at arms; Bertsch and W. C. Peterson,
executive committee.

The Post sponsored Junior baseball, had on ice skating rink west of the Legion
Post in the winter, getting water from the railroad well.  They also sponsored
Boys State as they do now, also had scrap and paper drives and sold War Bonds.
We were busy during WW II doing something all the time.

The Legion Auxiliary's officers were Hilda Peterson, president; Clara Elford,
vice president; Viola Tschirley, 2nd vice president; Lillian Nystrom,
secretary; Emma Mayer, treasurer; Clara Reidt, sgt. at arms; Marie Bishop,
chaplain; and Minnie Curtis, historian.

The Post and Auxiliary were named for Andrew Schauer who died in camp October
1918.

The first meeting of both the Legion and Auxiliary was held in the City Hall
January 1938.  We decided then to build our own building.

J. W. Parmely had all the lots in Block 14 and the last half of 13 in Lenox
addition.  He sold them to the Post for $5.00. We then got a bunk-car from the
railroad and put it onto the east lot and held our meetings there.  It was also
used as a warming house for skaters.

October 24, 1939 the Post voted to build a new home just west of Streifels.  We
got a W.P.A. project, the state Highway hauled the sand and stone as the west
part of the lots was to be used as a rest stop for tourists.  We planted some
shade trees and put in some tables.

The building had a basement where the Auxiliary met and the Post met upstairs.
It worked fine until water began coming into the basement, then we all had to
meet upstairs.

Edmunds County Fair was held in Roscoe for a few years before WW II and was
very successful.  In 1918, there were a number of ranchers here from Montana
making hay, so they proceeded to put on a rodeo which was a good one.  Montana
had dried out so there was no hay there.  The rodeo was held on the Fairgrounds
east of Chappell's place.

        Rodeo at Edmunds County Fair, 1919.

In 1950 Roscoe had a population of 800 which is apparently the top.  Roscoe,
not having a doctor or hospital was a drawback for retirees.

Roscoe has always had problems with water since the early days, so now I hope
their new well will provide enough water for a long time.  Maybe for the next
hundred years.

The first chemical fire engine was bought in 1908. Before that, fire was fought
with a bucket brigade, taking water from rain barrels.

As this is being written, I am living in Harlingen, Texas at 1902 Breedlove.
This is where your summer spends the winter.

If anyone wants to question any part of this article, they are welcome to do
so.  I've spent the last year on research and memory.  I am happy now that I
can think of something else.

I've tried to live by this motto.  I shall pass through this world but once.
If therefore there can be any kindness I can show or any good thing I can do,
let me do it now, let me not defer it or neglect it for I shall not pass this
way again.

Tales of Pioneer Pathfinders BY W. P. WILSON, ROSCOE

The first settler of Roscoe was Sebastian B. Basford. He told me that he
dropped a load of lumber at the southeast corner of Section 24, Twp. 123, Range
71, and went back to Aberdeen for more.  There he met Charles Morgan coming
west to locate with a large tent, and he prevailed on him to locate at Roscoe
which he did, and started the first hotel in Roscoe, later building a wooden
hotel about 30 rods west of where Mr. Chappel now lives. (Ed. note: The date of
this writing is not known to us.)

When Mr. Basford finally built, he built on the northeast one-fourth of Section
25, part of the building now occupied by Mr. Wilke.  A man by the name of
McCullen settled the southeast one-fourth; Elias Hinsdale, the southwest
one-fourth; and Elmer Lewis the northwest one-fourth.  Charley Morgan filed on
the southwest one- fourth of 19-123-70; and J. W. Parmley on the northwest
one-fourth of 30-123-70.  A Mr. Stanley started the first lumber yard in
Roscoe, drawing his material from Ipswich, which was the end of the C. M. & St.
Paul Railroad at that time.  He also filed on and proved upon the southwest
one-fourth of 31-123-70; and a Miss Bird (afterwards the wife of Robert Finch)
filed on the northwest of Section 31.  There was no government survey at that
time, except the Meridian line running north and south through Roscoe, and that
was a guide for the new settlers to pick out their claims.

I came to Roscoe the 18th of March 1884 and worked for a man by the name of
Miles W. Glover, who had settled the year before on the northeast one-fourth,
Section 1-122-71, and the township was afterwards named after him.  I filed on
the east one-fourth of Section 12 in the same township where my boy has his
stock farm at the present time.  A man by the name of Elias Turner settled on
the southeast one-fourth, Section 1, between Mr. Glover and me, where Mr.
Williams is now living.

Mr. Glover came to Dakota Territory in the Spring of 1883 and brought some cows
and three horses and that summer when the surveyors, completing the survey,
found out Glovers had cows, they came to buy milk and asked Mrs. Glover if it
was hard work to find water on the prairie.  She said, "Yes, and what we do
find we have to kill with a club before we can drink it." To find water was one
of the drawbacks of the earlier settlers in Dakota.  I drawed water from
Roscoe, three and one- half miles one winter for 90 head of stock, a feat that I
would think impossible now, but it shows what we can do if we have to.

In 1884, I went with four others with five loads of lumber from Ipswich to a
few miles west of where Mound City is now for a man by the name of Shepherd,
who went there with 300 head of cattle to start a cattle ranch.  We were two
days on the road and had to camp out at nights, and find water for ourselves
and the teams, from the sloughs.  After we left Roscoe, we saw only one
building and that was called the "Black Shack" and no one lived in that.  The
first night we traveled until quite late before finding a slough with water, so
we could water the horses.  The next morning we went for water to make some
coffee and found none except between the bogs and that was full of wigglers, so
I strained what I got through my handkerchief, guessing that it was clean and
knowing that the wigglers was not what I liked.

For fuel in the home we burned hay, cow chips, flax straw and sometimes would
slip over to the tracks when the section men were out of sight and cut off two
or three ties on the outside of the rail and have some wood fire for a change.
Many a time I have sat in front of the stove with a pile of hay to bake bread
and sometimes a spark would fly out and set the pile on fire, and then there
would be scrambling to smother it out as we generally had an old piece of
carpet or quilt handy for that purpose.

Many times when I was walking over the prairie and I saw a cow chip well
seasoned on the top side, I would catch my toe under it and tip it to the other
side to the sun to finish it for fuel later on, and now I think of it if I
don't do it (force of habit I suppose).

The Nineteen Twenties in Roscoe
AS REMEMBERED BY MRS. LYNN (EVELYN DECKER) LIGHT

The 1920s brought many changes in Roscoe.  Many of the children of the pioneer
families moved away and many of the pioneers themselves were deceased.  Fred
and Walter Huck were the last of the Huck family to leave.  Fred Huck had built
the house on the south corner east of the schoolhouse.  They moved to Colville,
Washington.  Walter and Edith Huck and their family moved to Ipswich.  Benie
Scholfield was the last of that family to leave--the year after son John was
graduated, they moved to California.  John soon made the Philippine Islands his
home.  Paul and Clara (Witter) Elford and Mrs. Charles (Dorothy Elford) Fischer
stayed in Roscoe after the Elfords moved to the west coast. Clarence Chappell,
who married Emma Landfried and Mrs. John (Elizabeth Chappell) Bishop remained
here. All the Decker girls had married and moved away except Alice, who married
Adney Downer.  She died in 1920.  Charles, the only son, was the postmaster.  By
1930, Ella Zander (Mrs. Lester Oehler) was the only child of August and Emma
Zander left in Roscoe, and Lovel Johnson and his wife Ruth (Witter) were the
last of the Johnson family.  The Borsberrys and the Basfords had moved.

1920-2l, the first year in the news schoolhouse saw the inauguration of a
four-year high school.  Mr. Francis Peterson was the principal and Florence
Gartland was the other high school teacher.  Other teachers that year were Mina
Telecamp, Marion Inlagen and Lydia Daibert.  The next year the first class was
graduated from Roscoe High School.  It consisted of Donald Price, Arden
McCafferty, Martin Scherf and John Scholfield.  In 1923 it was Ledo Cole,
Howard Cooper and Edmond McCafferty and Arthur Scherf.  There were no graduates
in the spring of 1925 or 1926.

In the fall of 1926, three teachers were hired for teaching in the high school.
Two of them were to stay for many years, Supt.  Elmer Rovang of Fergus Falls,
Minn. and Miss Valborg Johnson of Canton, S.D., who become Mrs. Martin Scherf.
The third teacher was David Fiett.  He left after two years to get his medical
degree.  Leslie Peterson took his place.  That year the graduating class was
Martha Zander, Edwin Wilson, Esther Christen and Milton Tschirley.  I believe
Edwin Wilson was the first Roscoe High School graduate to receive a college
degree.  In the Class of 1928, five of the eight graduates went on to receive
their degrees, Lillian Redman, Harold Scherf, Lynn Light, Hayden Williams and
Evelyn Decker.  The other members of the class were Marie Williams who married
Wm.  Fischer, Rosa Ellwein who married Wm.  Wudel and Viola Thielen who married
Milton Tschirley.

January 24, 1928, a special train was chartered to take the Roscoe High School
Basketball team and their supporters to a basketball game in Java.  We had a
very good team that year and had beaten Java on our own court,, but we lost the
game at Java that night by a score of 16-7.

In 1925-26, the first High School Girls Basketball team was organized.  At that
time the centers played full court and the guards and forwards played half
court. Rev. and Mrs. Agnes Bates who had jointly pastored the Presbyterian
Churches in Roscoe, Glenham, and Java, left Roscoe.  He took a semi-retirement
pastorate in eastern South Dakota.  Mrs. Bates shared the pastoral duties by
reading his sermons when his schedule took him to one of the other churches.
Rev. Cecil Allin became the pastor of the Presbyterian Church here.

In the early 1920's, Rev. J. P. Scherf become the pastor of the Lutheran
Church and in 1928, the big, new Lutheran Church was built.  He remained the
pastor for many years.

H. J. Bebermeyer and son M. B. purchased the Ipswich Tribune and also the
Roscoe Independent.  At that time the Roscoe paper was being published by Joe
Hilton of Mobridge.  Lynn Light become the local editor.  That position was
held by a member of the Light family until 1942.

There were two general merchandise stores, The Ben Redman store and the Herman
Broufman store.  The Broufmans built the building on north main where the Hieb
Implement now is.  Mrs. Broufman's brother Abe Hess, clerked in the store for
several years.  There were two banks and several cream stations.  A boy from
Strasburg, N. D. would occasionally play his accordion in one of the cream
stations.

In 1920, Mrs. Art Hartel came home from Minneapolis where they had been
vacationing.  While there she had a "permanent".  She was the first woman in
Roscoe to have one!

The Ward McCafferty family lived in the large house that stood where the Senior
Apartments are now located.  Their children were Arden, Edmond, Phoebe, Vivian
and Jack.  Arden was in the first graduating class.  They moved away about
1924.  The house was used for the high school dormitory in 1934 and 1935. Later
when Dr. Aldrich was in town, it was used for a hospital.

        The McCafferty House -- this house served Roscoe in Several ways during
        its "lifetime".  A comfortable family home, a hospital operated by Dr.
        Aldrich in 1926, a dormitory for about 40 high school girls in 1934-35
        with Evelyn Decker serving as Dean of Girls and finally as a two family
        home. The house was torn down in 1973 to make way for the Roscoe Senior
        Housing buildings.

Mr. and Mrs. Victor Longlois came to Roscoe from Belfield, N. D. They had the
house built that the Arthur Scherfs bought later and is now the home of the
Harold and Berneice, (Schipke) Margeson family.  He was an attorney and shared
the office of Judge Lightner in the First State Bank building.  The Longlois'
came to Roscoe in the early 1920s.

People who moved into the area in these years were: The Albert Williams,
Hayden, Marie, Wilbur, and Ernest; The DeForrest Alcorns, Kenneth, Carol, Rhea
and Patricia-, The Henry Thielens, Albert, Gertrude, Viola and a son-in-law,
Babe Rundle and his family; The Wm. Swans, Lloyd and Ethel; The Heinrichs,
John, Hilda, Barbara, Mary and Laurence; The Otto Wudels, Alvina, Arthur,
William, Martha, Elton, and Emma; The Otto Noehres, Esther, Waiter and Ernest;
The Herman Olsons, Harold, Russell and Beverly; Lewis Larson, The Sam Fountains
and The Felix Poeschls.

Three large buildings burned down, the old Methodist Church, the Jamieson Hotel
and the Brenton Brothers' Garage.

The post office was renovated with all new equipment, and under Charles Decker,
two rural routes were added and the first (a triweekly) become a daily.

The Milwaukee Depot was a busy place.  Wm.  Swan was the agent assisted by
Lewis Larson and Felix Poeschl.  There was 24 hour service for Western Union.
There were three "Flyers" a day both east and west.  If it was an urgent
matter, they would stop for passengers, but as a rule, local passengers used
the local Aberdeen to Mobridge train.  The branch line to Linton had a mail car
as did all four trains going each way.  Mail was sorted on the trains and
dispatched for sorting at Roscoe where pouches of mail were made up for the
branch lines.  There was a branch line going south to Orient also.  The
"Flyers" took on mail from a mail crane on the go" and threw off the pouches of
mail for Roscoe and for the other stations along the way.  Mail service was
excellent.

Roscoe was really a railroad center.  Some of the men who were employed by the
Milwaukee were: Lovel Johnson, Guy Wynn, Tom Gouras, Peter Heinrich, John
Pickett, Joe Gruba, H. J. Vanderhoff, John Aligier, George Price, William Hild,
Frank Meier, Wm. Swan, Felix Poeschl, Lewis Larson and many others.

Memories of Roscoe BY MRS. TILLIE MILLER DAGUE

        Tillie, Ella, Olga and Martha Miller

Many memories are brought back to me as a child raised in Roscoe.  May some of
the old timers bring back some of these times.

My parents, the Gottlieb Millers, moved there in 1913, and lived in the house
known as the Decker house on the northeast corner where the school house is
now.  I started school in the old wooden school house and my first teacher was
Eva Cramer Zander.  I watched the new school being built where I went then.  As
a child, I remember the Livery Barn on the west side of main street.  The
telephone office was north of the post office and the operators were Mayme
Wilson and later Ethel Brenton.  The postmaster at the time was Mr. McCafferty.
Judge Lightners office and the First State Bank were in the same building.
Eugene Light at the time was the elevator manager.  Vanderhoff was the depot
agent.  My father was the coal shed foreman during World War I. He kept the
troop trains supplied with coal day and night.

In later years he was also section boss in his 18 years working for the
railroad company.

The Lowensteins had the saloon in the brick building south of the tracks.
Zanders Meat Market, then next was an ice cream parlor run by Frances Beech and
next to that was the Farmers State Bank run by Mr. Tschirley and Mr. Snyder.
The Wilson Lumber Yard was to the west of that.  The night the Jamieson Hotel
burned down was terrible.  The garage later known as the Spitzers was owned by
Ben Blake.

The West Hotel was owned by a Mr. Muenz and Moore.  The dry goods store owned
by Mr. Henning was on the east side of main street; one of the clerks was
Bertha Ellwein.

The Hartel Hardware store next to the tracks burned down either in 1918 or
1919.  The two story building there used to be a hotel, that was moved there
from south of the roundhouse which is a field now.  North of the tracks, the
building then was a restaurant run by Julia Brower.  The first electricity to
come to Roscoe was managed by Jake Wold.  We then lived one block east of the
town well where we got our water.  My father planted the trees that ore on the
corner south of the Spitzer house in 1916.  A machine shed was on the area
where the city hall is today.  The Huck family had the store which later Ben
Redman took over.  I worked for them many times.  Rev. Bates was the pastor of
the Presbyterian Church about that time.  I was confirmed in the old Lutheran
Church in 1921 by Pastor Manteufel. Then the Scherf family came to Roscoe.

The county fairgrounds were located on the east side of Roscoe where they had
rodeos.  Lawrence Welk played his accordion at the dance during the fair.  Lem
Devries and Scholfield were cattle buyers and had their barn east on the south
side of the highway. These are the memories of a child of ten then--hope some
folks may know where and when these times were.

ROSCOE HERALD March 6, 1890
When the passenger train pulled out for the east Saturday night it collided
with one of Sutley's calves that claimed the right of way at the time.  Like a
good many individuals, the calf found to it's sorrow that it is useless to
oppose a big corporation like the railroad company.

ROSCOE HERALD March 27, 1890
Captain S. V. Arnold takes exception to our statement last week that he has no
special love for the members of the W.C.T.U. and desires us to soy that he
loves all the ladies from Susan B. Anthony down through the entire list.  We
are willing to make the correction, but-- what will Mrs. Arnold say?

George Hammond at Random BY BONITA ROESCH

        George Hammond at his easel

Today, George Hammond is eighty-two years old.  He was born on a farm near
Canton, S.D. April 21, 1901. The year of 1913 was a very dry year and his father
announced he would move his family to an area that received more rainfall.
Elmwood, Wisconsin become their new home in the Spring of 1914.  His parents
raised a family of seven boys and one girl and spent the remainder of their
lives in Wisconsin.

Times were hard and being the third oldest, George left home in his early teens
to seek his own livelihood. A neighbor to his father owned maple trees.  George
secured a permit and a registered trade mark, bought two hundred pails with
holes, made wooden spigots and went into the maple syrup business with the
neighbor. He earned enough money to buy a train ticket and at the age of
seventeen in the Fall of 1918 got off the train in Aberdeen, S.D.

It was the time of WW I and a government agent was at the train depot
recruiting people to work on farms in the area.  That day the government man
hired George, another man and a woman to work for a Jim Anderson who lived on a
farm near Aberdeen.  That fall, George threshed for Anderson and his neighbors.

During the Winter of 1918, George along with a brother and Uncles Clyde and
Alfred Ettles bought a sheep ranch about twelve miles north of Ipswich.  They
took sheep on shares and then bought some and sold them to an out-fit in
Montana.

George wintered horses on this farm in 1921 for John and Pat O'Connell.  That
Spring he went to work for these two brothers from Leola who built roads.
George recalls that two teams of horses were used per dump wagon to haul the
fill and gravel needed to build the roads.  A unit called an elevating grader
was also part of the road building equipment.  This grader was pulled by 16
horses, twelve at the helm and four at the rear.

In 1924 when George crossed the Missouri River on his way to break up land he'd
rented in the Wakpala area he remembered that bridges were nonexistent and so
he loaded his new Case tractor which he'd purchased from Spitzer and Lemmle of
Roscoe, onto a ferry.  However, in 1927 when he recrossed the River with his
Case he did so by way of a bridge but waited until 3:00 in the morning so he
wouldn't have to look at what he was crossing.  And besides, lugs were illegal.

1927 saw George renting and breaking land north of Beebe up near Leola.  He
remembers it was the year of wonderful crops.  That Fall he went to Wisconsin to
spend the winter with his family.  He returned to Roscoe to stay in the Fall of
1928.  He was hired by the Spitzer and Lemmle Company and worked for them for
eight years.  The first task he was given to attend to was to level the site of
the Jamieson Hotel which had been destroyed by a fire.  George had stayed at
that hotel when he visited Roscoe to attend rodeos with his Uncle Alfred
Ettles, in 1919.

        The Jamieson Hotel was a fine eating place and had many residents
        living there.

George purchased his first car in 1925 for $15.00. It was a second-hand Model
T. He needed money to put in a crop so he bought ten bushels of potatoes from
Fred Roesch and five bushels from a Vilhauer for $1.00 a bushel.  He loaded
them into his Model T and took them to Mobridge where he sold them to a
restaurant owner for $4.50 a bushel and returned to Roscoe with enough money to
put in a half-section of wheat.

In the early 1930's this man set to building a snowmobile for the type of
country that needed a machine that could move over the snow.  He used a Model T
frame and placed a motor in backwards so he could run the chain drive from the
back of the motor to the propeller.  He purchased the propeller for ninety-
eight cents from a junk dealer who was dismantling the plane for parts.  The
propeller needed to be sheared down and gears needed to be changed so the fan
wouldn't run faster than the motor.  The machine required three skiis which
were formed or bent from hickory wood, a trade which he had learned as a young
boy in Wisconsin.  George steered this machine from the rear ski.  When he was
asked if the machine ever worked, he said, "I went from Spitzers to the
dumpground without any problems.  Then we had to pull it back.  I never did toy
much with it after that.  It was too heavy and the propeller locked a little
pitch.  But with a few alterations I think it could've -worked."

Martin Scherf sold George his first truck for cost and he was in the trucking
business for the next eleven years.  The truck was a 1936 Ford and George built
himself a twenty two foot trailer.  He hauled horses to Wisconsin that Martin
Scherf had sold to farmers there. Lumber, watermelons, and apples were some of
the loads he brought back to South Dakota.  At one point George was stopped by
a highway official who checked his load and found him to be carrying fresh
fruits and vegetables, commodities which he had no license for at the time.
The official asked him what he planned on doing with the load and George said,
"This load belongs to my family.  I'm taking it home to my wife to can." This
was perfectly legal and so the official only remarked, "You must have many
children." George did not comment.  His wife, Ann, does admit that at times she
felt like she had conned whole semi loads.

In 1946 George sold his truck to Arnold Hettich, purchased heavy-duty
equipment, and went into the road construction business until the time of his
retirement in 1973.  "I figured it out one time and decided I had built 240
miles of road between Mobridge and Aberdeen.  I also put in a lot of dugouts,
dams, and sod-waterways.", George commented.  When he was asked what prompted
him to retire, he turned to his wife and asked, "Did I retire or just get
tired?"

Since his retirement, Mr. Hammond has been busy with the art of painting.  He
finds it fun, relaxing, and entertaining.  He has sketched most of his life and
likes to use photographs, pictures from newspapers and magazines and places he
has visited to guide him in his painting. His work consists of pioneer and
western themes plus animals and wildlife.  He has also painted portraits of
friends.  He uses oil and latex point.

George has exhibits of his paintings in many business places in Roscoe and this
winter will be busy painting pictures to sell during Roscoe's Centennial.

When Roscoe celebrated their 75th Jubilee, George was very much involved as he
was mayor and, on horseback, grandly led the parade beside John Roesch, the
oldest veteran.  When he was asked what it was that he was looking forward to
in regard to the Roscoe Centennial he replied, "Just to be around."

        George's paintings on display.

        Thank you George for the interview.  You are a man of one character
        with many ideas that have led you through many experiences.  After
        becoming aware of  how many years you put in twenty hour days and, at
        times, went to bed only every other night, I would have to say you are
        a relic of a true pioneer and definitely a survivor.  I'm glad my
        children were able to know you well.  I hope some of it rubbed off!

                ROSCOE FAMILY HISTORIES

GOTTFRIED SR. AND EVA ADAM

        Back row: Reinhold, Frieda, Albert, Gust, Tina, and Gottfried,
        Jr.; front row: Clara, Gottfried, Sr., Eva, and Fred Adam

Gottfried Adam was born January 10, 1888 in Neudorf, South Russia.  He was born
to Mr. and Mrs. Friedrich Adam.  The family came to the United States in 1893
and settled on a farm northeast of Roscoe.  Eva nee Dockter Adam was born June
21, 1894 in Neudorf- Odessa, South Russia.  She was born to John J. and
Christina Dockter.  The family came to the United States in 1901 and settled
north of Hosmer.

Gottfried and Eva were joined in marriage January 28, 1913.  They lived on a
farm northeast of Roscoe for 43 years.  In 1956, they moved to Roscoe.
Gottfried passed away February 2, 1960 and was buried in Roscoe.  Eva is
presently in residence at the Senior Citizens' Home in Hosmer, S.D.

Gottfried and Eva had nine children, Fred, married Viola Mehlhoff, living in
Temecula, Calif. Albert, married to Ida Voegele, and is living in Roscoe, S. D.

Gust is married to Vera Aman and they live in McLaughlin, S.D.

Gottfried Jr., married to Luella Aman, and they live in Roscoe, S.D.

Walter is deceased.

Rienhold is married to Fernada Pleinis, and they live in Bellflower, Calif.

Clara is married to Art Hoffer and they live in Los Cruces, N. M.

Tina Stroud lives in Denver, Colo.

Frieda is married to LeRoy Weller and they live in Rapid City, S.D.

ALBERT AND IDA ADAM

Albert G. Adam was born August 16, 1915 on a farm near Hosmer, S.D. His parents
were Gottfried Adam and Eva nee Dockter Adam.  Ida nee Voegele Adam was also
born near Hosmer, S.D. September 1, 1930.  She was born to John B. Voegele and
Pauline nee Pleinis Voegele.

        Michael, Ida, Albert and Laurie Adam

Ida and Albert were joined in marriage October 26, 1947 at St. Paul's Lutheran
Church of Roscoe, S.D. For their first eight years of marriage, they settled in
Roscoe.  During that time, Albert was a carpenter as well as a part-time
farmer.  In 1956, they moved to the farm where Albert's folks had lived.
Albert and Ida remained there for 25 years.  After Albert's retirement in 1981,
they built a new home and once again moved to Roscoe.  They are currently in
residence there.  Albert was an eight-year member of the Leola school district
board.  He also served in the Army for four and one-half years during World War
II.

The Albert Adam family attends St. Paul's Lutheran Church.  Included in that
family is Michael Neal, who was born July 6, 1961.  He graduated from Leola High
School and Lake Area Vocational Technical School in Watertown, S.D. He is
currently employed by Taylor Implement of Ipswich, S.D. Also included is Laurie
Ann, who was born June 15, 1963.  She also graduated from Leola High School and
is currently attending Jamestown College in Jamestown, N.D.

GOTTFRIED AND LUELLA ADAM

        Luella, Gary and Gottfried H. Adam

Gottfried H. Adam, Jr. was born June 16, 1919 near Hosmer, S.D., the son of
Gottfried Sr. and Eva Dockter Adam.  They were both born in Neudorf, White
Russia and immigrated to the United States in the late 1800s.  Gottfried, Sr.
lived with his parents on a farm about 20 miles northeast of Hosmer and Eva
lived on a farm about six miles north of Hosmer after homesteading some land in
the Bison, S.D. area. Gottfried, Sr. and Eva built up a farm 15 miles east of
Hosmer where they raised nine children.  When they retired, they moved to
Roscoe where Mr. Adam died. Eva is a resident at the Hosmer Nursing Home.

Luella Aman Adam was born in Hosmer July 7, 1927 to Philip and Esther Wittmeier
Aman.  They made their home in Roscoe where they still reside.

Gottfried and Luella were married November 24, 1946 in Roscoe where they
established their home. From 1946 to 1963 they did some farming in the Roscoe
area and also were in the electrical business, doing wiring and selling
appliances from their home.  In 1963 they built a workshop and appliance store,
28x60, next to their home.  In 1977 they remodeled what had been Scherf's Super
Valu store and moved their business into it and are still located there.

They have one son, Gary D., married to Kathy Holsing, living in Roscoe where he
is a partner in his father's business.  They have one daughter, Heather.

Gottfried served in the United States Army from February 21, 1941 to August 31,
1945 during WWII.  He served in Africa, Sicily, Italy, France, Germany and
Austria.

Gottfried is on the City Council, having served there for many years.  He is
also a member of the Roscoe American Legion and the Rod and Gun Club.  They are
both members of Commercial Club and St. Paul's Lutheran Church.

GARY AND KATHRYN ADAM

        Kathy, Gary and Heather Adam

Gary Dean Adam was born September 16, 1948 at the Ipswich Community Hospital,
Ipswich, S.D. to Gottfried Herman Adam, Jr. and Luella Aman Adam.  His parents
were born and raised in the Hosmer-Roscoe area.

Kathryn Elaine Holsing Adam was born July 7, 1948 at the Ipswich Community
Hospital, Ipswich, S.D. to Arthur Fredrich Wilhelm Holsing and Blanch Lillian
Simon Holsing.  Her parents were born and raised in the Wecota-Loyalton area.

Gary attended Roscoe Grade School and High School and graduated from Northern
State College, Aberdeen, S.D. with a Bachelor of Science degree.

Kathy attended Ipswich Public Grade and High School. She graduated from
Stewart's School of Hairstyling and worked for one year at Dorothy's Beauty
Salon in Ipswich. In October 1968 she became a teller at the Ipswich State
Bank. In 1979 she became an Assistant Cashier at the Bank.

June 14, 1970 Gary and Kathy were married at the St. Paul's Lutheran Church in
Roscoe, S.D.

Gary began working with his father in June of 1970 in the electric and heating
business and has since become Gottfried's partner at Adam's Electric in Roscoe.

Gary and Kathy are members of the St. Paul's Lutheran Church.  Gary has served
on the finance committee of the church and was Secretary of the Church Council
for three years.

Gary has held offices of secretary, vice president and president of the Roscoe
Commercial Club, of which Gary and Kathy are both members.

Gary has been Chief of the Roscoe Fire Department since 1976 and put in many
hours to help the Roscoe community obtain a new fire hall.

Gary is a member of the Roscoe Rod and Gun Club and is a school board member at
Roscoe Public School.

They have one daughter, Heather Nicholle, born September 21, 1975 at the
Ipswich Community Hospital.  She is a student at the Roscoe Grade School.

MARTIN AND CHRISTINA ADAM

        Back row: Clara, Cecilia, Martha, Ida; Front Row: Martin and Christina
        Adam

Martin Adam was born November 20, 1890 in Neudorf, S. Russia, to Friederick and
Eva Mayer Adam. Both of them were born in Neudorf.  Martin came to South Dakota
with his parents when he was about two years old.  They settled on a farm about
20 miles northeast of Roscoe.  In 1912 he went with a cousin John Adam to take
up a homestead in Idaho.  After a time he came back and married a neighbor
girl, Christina Hieb, July 7, 1912.

Christina Hieb Adam was born January 9, 1894 at Hosmer, S.D., to Michael and
Rosina Borkirchert Hieb. Michael was born in Kassel, Russia and Rosina in
Neudorf, Russia.

The young couple made their home at Yale, Casio County, Idaho.  Their first
daughter, Ida was born July 11, 1913.Life in this new land proved to be
difficult and discouraging, so in a couple of years they returned to South
Dakota.

They settled on a farm about 12 miles northeast of Roscoe, where fortune smiled
on them and with hard work and perseverance they prospered.  Three more
daughters were born to them: Martha, July 30, 1915; Cecilia, January 7, 1917;
and Clara, September 24, 1921.

In 1923, forced by ill health, Martin, along with his brother August decided to
make Roscoe their home. They purchased the Wudel Hardware from Otto Wudel and
were in business as Adam Brothers Hardware until 1946, when they sold out to
Rueben Kirschenman.

Wanting to be useful in retirement, Martin served as Edmunds County Treasurer.
At this time, they moved to Ipswich where they lived until 1959, when they moved
back to Roscoe.

Martin died April 27, 1959 after several years illness. He was buried in
Morningside Cemetery, He had been a member of St. Paul's Lutheran Church where
he served faithfully in various church offices.

Christina continued to live in Roscoe until she suffered a disabling stroke in
1963.  She was able to spend short summer periods in Roscoe until 1969.  She
sold her home and moved to Scotland into an apartment, where she lived four
years.  Her remaining four years were spent in the Good Samaritan Nursing Home
in Scotland. She died September 26, 1977 and was buried in Morningside Cemetery.

The daughters, after High School, pursued their various careers.  Ida went to
N.S.T.C. and taught rural school till she married Ivan Downer, June 29, 1936,
and settled on a farm southwest of Roscoe.  Later they went into the hardware
business, first in Stickney, then in Scotland, where they are now in
retirement.  Two sons were born to them: Glenn, born in Roscoe, is in the Navy
and John, born in Stickney, died in 1977.

Martha went into the nursing field, getting her training in Mankato, Minnesota.
She remained in Minnesota and married Harold Schulte September 16, 1937.  Five
children were born to them: Rodney, Stanley, Delmar, Berniece and Marcele.
They are now living in Butterfield, Minnesota and Phoenix, Arizona.

Cecilia attended Business College in Mitchell, South Dakota.  She was employed
in the Treasurer's office and married Ronald Roberts of Ipswich, November 17,
1940. In 1945, they purchased Stickney Hardware with Ivan Downer.  They are
still in the business.  Four daughters were born to them: Beverly, Phyllis,
Mavis and Nancy.

Clara went to N.S.T.C. After a short period of employment in Ipswich she was
married to Hillard Beller, May 24, 1942.  After a tour in the Navy, they
settled in Washington.  Two children were born to them, Louise and Lyle.  In
1957 Clara suffered a tragic death by drowning.  She was laid to rest in Haven
of Rest Cemetery, Gig Harbor, Washington.

EVERETTE ALLBEE FAMILY

Everette and Kitty Cranage Allbee were married in Wisconsin in 1881 and came to
South Dakota in 1908, settling two miles south of Beebe.  Everette served on
the school board there for many years.  Later they moved to the Loyalton area.
To this family 11 children were born, nine in Wisconsin: Walter, who is deceased
and buried there, Ellis, Everette, Henry, William, Ralph, Florence and Bessie.
Two were born in South Dakota, Ethel and Harry.

        Mrs. Everette (Kitty) Allbee

They came to Richville, Wis. and were farmers. Everette and Kitty are both
deceased and buried in the Ipswich Cemetery.  The surviving members are: Bessie
Penfield Bruns and Mrs. Fred (Ethel) Roberdeau.  Both are living in Northville,
S.D., and Harry, who lives in Wenatchee, Wash.  All are retired.

The parents of Everette originated from Scotland.

WILLIAM AND CHARLOTTE ALLBEE

        Back row: Billie, Dorothy, Tom, Clyde and Mary; Front row: William and
        Charlotte Allbee

William (Bill) Allbee was born in 1891 in Grant County, Wis., the son of
Everette and Kitty Cranage Allbee.  He came to South Dakota in 1908 with his
parents.  On October 6, 1915 he married Charlotte (Lottie) Lieber at the hotel
in Ipswich, S.D. He farmed until moving into the town of Loyalton.  Here he
worked on the building of the Loyalton Dam, and was custodian at the school's
dormitory before moving south of town where he retired.  They moved back into
Loyalton and ran the pool hall until it burned down.

William (Bill) passed away in Loyalton in 1969 and is buried in Ipswich, S.D.
William and Charlotte had five children.  Mary married David Soward in Loyalton
and lived   south    of   there.   Dorothy,    a     graduate of Loyalton High
School married Harold Hall of Mellette, S.D. in Aberdeen.

Harold served in the U.S. Army.  Later they moved to California where they made
their home until Dorothy retired from the Sears Roebuck Store.  They are now
living in Williams, Ariz.  Three children were born to this couple: Sheila, who
passed away at one year and is buried in Ipswich, Sharon and Dick, who are both
married.

Clyde graduated from Loyalton High School in 1940. (See Clyde Allbee).

Thomas married Barbara Ryman in Minnesota and farmed at home for awhile.  They
now live in Timber Lake, S.D. and he is self-employed.  They have four
children: Cheryl who is married and lives in the Faulkton area; Douglas, who is
married and is serving in the Navy; Mike, married and living in Brookings; and
James, who is still at home.

William Allbee, Jr. married Marjorie Brotzel in Barnard, S.D. and farmed in the
Loyalton area before moving to Aberdeen where they still reside.  They have
four children: Debra and William who are both married and living in Aberdeen,
Barry, who is serving in the U.S. Navy and Tracy, still at home.

CLYDE AND MARCELLA ALLBEE

        Back row: Bob, Colleen and Garry; Front row: Clyde and Marcella Allbee

Clyde married Marcella Hammrich of Ipswich in Coeur D'Alene, Ida. after serving
four years in the U.S. Navy. They purchased a quarter of land and built up a
farm west of the home farm where they lived until they sold to their son.  They
moved back to the home farm and are residing there.

Clyde served on the Loyalton School Board until the district joined with
Roscoe.  He also served as township supervisor for many years, still holding
that position.

The Allbees have three children: Robert (Bob), who served six years in the
National Guard and married Diane Malsom.  They were the last couple to marry in
the old Catholic Church in Roscoe.  Diane graduated from Roscoe High School.
They farmed with his parents until they bought the farm.  They have five
children, Scott, Charlotte, Terry Lee, Elaine and Nancy, all at home and
attending school in Roscoe.  They are all active in 4-H.  Garry, a graduate of
the Ipswich High School is married to Mary Bauer and they live in Aberdeen.  He
is self-employed and they have two children, Franklin and Marcy, both at home.
Colleen graduated from Roscoe High School.  She married Mike Fischer of
Aberdeen at Onaka and lives in Aberdeen where she is employed at the Holiday
Inn.  They have two sons Shone and Robert, both at home.

DUWAYNE AND AGATHA AMAN

        Joshua, DuWayne, Aggie, and Jared Aman

DuWayne Aman was born on April 28, 1960, to Herbert and Virginia Forkel Aman,
in Aberdeen, S.D. He grew up on a farm northwest of Hosmer, S.D. and graduated
from Hosmer High School in 1978.  Agatha Kappenman, was born on February 3,
1961, to Wilfred and Catherine Heier Kappenman in Eureka, S.D. She also grew up
on a farm northwest of Hosmer, just four miles from the Aman farm, and
graduated from Hosmer High School in 1979.

DuWayne and Aggie were married on November 17, 1979, in the Holy Trinity
Catholic Church in Hosmer. They established their first home in Aberdeen where
DeWayne was employed by the Milwaukee Railroad, until the embargo on March 1,
1980.  They then moved on a farm near Roscoe until DuWayne become employed by
the South Dakota Wheat Growers Elevator in Roscoe in the spring of 1980.  They
moved into Roscoe in the fall of 1980, where they live at the present time.

They have two sons: Jerrad DuWayne and Joshua Emanuel, both born in Aberdeen.

HENRY AMAN (Excerpt from Diamond Jubilee Book)

Henry Aman, born November 26, 1880, was the son of Frederick and Eva Aman who
lived in South Russia territory.  In 1885 his family moved to the United States
and settled two miles south of Eureka, where he attended school and helped his
father on the farm.

Henry married Elizabeth Kallenberger in 1903 and have lived on a farm northwest
of Hosmer, then moved to Hosmer and back to a farm near Eureka.  In 1929 they
moved to Roscoe where they had three sons and three daughters.  Henry worked at
various jobs including carpenter work.  He had served on the city council for
three years.

He retired in 1951 and in 1955 his wife died.  Henry died in 1961.  They are
both buried in Morningside Cemetery in Roscoe.

JOHN AND BARBARA AMAN

John Aman, the son of Jacob and Frederika Ackerman Aman, was born September 25,
1872, in Bergdorf, South Russia.  He came to this country in 1887 with his
parents and settled near Hillsview, S.D. in McPherson County.  He was married
to Barbara Kallenberger December 3, 1897.  She was born March 16, 1879, in
Odessa, South Russia and came to the United States and the Eureka, S.D. area in
1895.  John and Barbara farmed in the Hillsview area until 1927 when they moved
to Roscoe.

They had five children, Theodore, Emil, Philip, Emma and Pauline.

They were members of St. Paul's Lutheran Church in Roscoe.

John Aman died February 23, 1942 and is buried in Morningside Cemetery at
Roscoe.

In 1952, Barbara was married to John Ullrich of Bowdle, S.D. and they lived
there until 1962 when they moved to a rest home in Aberdeen, S.D. Mr. Ullrich
died in 1963 and Barbara moved to the Lutheran Home in Eureka, where she died
December 22, 1965.  She is also buried in Morningside Cemetery.

PHILIP AND ESTHER AMAN

Philip Aman was born in rural Hosmer, S.D. September 7, 1907, the son of John
and Barbara Kallenberger Aman.  He grew up on the farm and attended rural
school.  In 1927, he moved into Roscoe with his parents.

Esther Wittmeier was born June 23, 1910, in Zeeland, N.D. to Henry and
Christina Hieb Wittmeier. At the age of nine, Esther moved to Hosmer with her
family where she attended school.

Philip and Esther were married July 19, 1931 at Hosmer.  They made their home
in Roscoe.  Philip held various jobs over the years, including 10 years of
employment with Martin Scherf and 26 years for the Edmunds County Highway
Department.  Philip has been semi-retired since 1972.  Esther worked at
Schnabels Cafe for five years and at the school as a cook for about eight
years.  She did sewing and housecleaning for many people in this area for many
years.

They have two children: Luella, married to Gottfried Adam, has one son Gary,
they live in Roscoe where they own and operate Adam's Electric; Harvey, married
to Linda Crissman, has two children, Kristie and Nathan, they live in Aberdeen,
S.D. where Harvey is Vice-President at United Federal Savings and Loan.

They are members of St. Paul's Lutheran Church in Roscoe.

HAROLD AND BONNIE ANDERSON

        Harold and Bonnie Anderson Family

Harold, son of Archie and Myrtle (Melstrom) Anderson of Hager City, Wisconsin
was born in Red Wing, Minn.  He grew up at Hager City, Wis., and came to South
Dakota in 1948, to the Loyalton area.

Bonnie, daughter of Phil and Zeldia (Oban) Bukaske of Loyalton, was born and
grew up in the Loyalton area.

They were married in 1950 at the Loyalton Wesleyan Church, and have farmed in
the Loyalton area since. They were members of the Loyalton Wesleyan Church
until the church merged with the Ipswich Wesleyan Church in 1981, and now
attend there.

They have four children: Gory, born in 1951, lives in Davis, Calif.  Gary
married Amelia Christenson of Faulkton in 1972.  Gary is a Doctor of
Veterinarian Medicine, and also a Doctor of Pathology.  Amelia is a registered
nurse, and works part-time in the hospital at Davis.  They have two sons, Andy
Lee, and Kyle Allan.

Gail, of Pampa, Texas was born in 1953.  She married Raymond Winter of Scott
City, Kansas in 1978.  Gail is a Penegen consultant, and Raymond is a
pipefitter in the oil refineries.  They have one daughter, Samarah Danee.

Sandra, of Manhattan, Kan. was born in 1957.  She married Thomas Uphoff of
Manhattan, Kan. in 1979. Sandra is a Penegen consultant, and Thomas is finishing
his degree in business administration.  He works for Rainbow Bread Co., and
also an ice company.  They have two children, Jason Thomas and Lindsay Kay.

Rodney of Loyalton was born in 1959.  He married Coretta Weiss of Maurine, S.D.
in 1978.  They are partnership farming with Harold and Bonnie and have one son,
Jeremiah Phil.

RODNEY AND CORETTA ANDERSON

Rodney, son of Harold and Bonnie (Bukaske) Anderson was born in 1959.  He has
lived in the Loyalton area all of his life.

Coretta, daughter of Albert and Mazy (Miller) Weiss of Maurine, S.D. was born
in 1960.

Rodney and Coretta were married in 1978 at her parents ranch at Maurine, S.D.
They ore farming in partnership at Loyalton, S.D. They are members of the
Ipswich Wesleyan Church.

To their union was born Jeremiah Phil in 1980.

FRIEDA ARNDT

Friedaricka Kapp Arndt was born in Gluckstal, South Russia November 14, 1899.
She came to the United States with her parents and four sisters in 1901 and
settled on a farm southeast of Eureka, S.D. She married Frederick Arndt April
11, 1920 and they made their home in Roscoe.  They were engaged in farming and
she did domestic work for many people in the area.

Her husband passed away in 1953.  She sold her small one room home to William
Millers and it is now the garage on the Oscar Krebs place.  She purchased the
Gottlieb Ellwein house and after several years, donated it to the Roscoe
Lutheran Church.  She became a resident in a Senior Housing Unit in Roscoe
until 1982 when ill health forced her to move to a nursing home.

Frieda passed away October 2, 1982 and was buried in Morningside Cemetery.

ERVIN AND ADELINE BADER

Adeline Spitzer was born on a farm northeast of Roscoe on January 20, 1920 and
moved to Roscoe with her parents, John and Rosina Kapp Spitzer and sister Ann,
in April 1920, when three months old.  Her father owned and operated the John
Deere Implement and Ford Garage, and also had farming interests.  She attended
Roscoe School for 12 years and graduated from High School in 1937, as
Valedictorian of her class of 13. She attended the University of South Dakota
at Vermillion for two years and two summer sessions, taking teaching and
business courses.  She took several correspondence courses from USD and
Northern and was listed in College Who's Who.

She taught the Bishop School three miles north of Roscoe for two years.  It was
one of the three schools (rural) that belonged to the Roscoe Independent School
District.  She had to haul the drinking water to the school each day and during
the winter months had to get to school early enough to warm up the one room
school house before the 10 students arrived.

She married Ervin Bader of Bowdle on October 5, 1941, at the Roscoe Lutheran
Church.  Rev. Herbert Lau was pastor at the time.  Ervin was Assistant Manager
of the Montgomery Ward Store at Deadwood, and they established their home
there.  Adeline became the secretary to the manager of the Ward Store.

In January, 1942 Erv was transferred to the Wards Store at Belle Fourche, S.D.
as manager.  Adeline helped with the managing and secretarial work at the store.
After several months Uncle Sam sent Erv his greetings and salutations, and on
April 10, 1942 was inducted into the U.S. Army.  He was sent to Camp Roberts,
Calif. So back to Roscoe for Adeline, who then helped her father in the
Implement with parts sales and book work, as brother Erwin had also been called
to serve in the Army.  In the fall of 1942 she went to California to be with
Erv who was an NCO instructor at Camp Roberts.  Adeline took a Civil Service
course and become department head of the Auditing Department at Camp Roberts
Quartermaster Corps.  She spent four years at Camp Roberts.

In the spring of 1946 Erv received his Honorable Discharge, so they moved back
to Roscoe and helped father in the Implement business.  Adeline taught the
fifth and sixth grades for two years in the Roscoe School and was substitute
teacher until a few years ago. Adeline also served on the School Addition
Planning Committee.

In 1947 Erv and Erwin Spitzer purchased the implement Business from John
Spitzer, and erected a new building and warehouse.  Their home was built in 1948
and Adeline helped with the parts sales and book work through the 16 years they
were in partnership business.

On March 29, 1955 the Ivy Circle Garden Club was organized and Adeline was one
of the Charter members. Much of the playground equipment, benches and tables
for the City Park were made by husbands of the members at the Implement.  The
ladies did all the mowing, raking, planting and caring for flower beds, as well
as keeping the two rest rooms clean.  She held all offices in the local club
while a member and was State Program Chairman for the South Dakota Federation of
Garden Clubs for two years.

She has been a member of the Roscoe American Legion Auxiliary since World War
II and held all local offices through the years.

She has been a member of the Roscoe P.T.A. and assisted and was chairman of
many fund drives.  She has been a member of the South Dakota Rural Letter
Carrier Association Auxiliary and District 21 RLC Association since 1963 and
held all offices in District 21. She was State Secretary of SDRLC Auxiliary for
three years and served on the State Scholarship Committee for eight years.

Their son John was born December 23, 1949.  He attended Roscoe School for 12
years and graduated with Salutatorian Honors in 1968.  He attended Northern
State College for two years taking business courses.  He lives in Aberdeen, and
is in the construction business.  He has two children, Johnathon and Sarah.

Erv was born at Java, S.D. on May 25, 1920, to Christ and Minnie Mauch Bader.
He attended the first three years at Java School.  They moved to Bowdle and he
graduated from Bowdle High School in 1938 with Honors.  He took several courses
in Business Economics and Real Estate courses at Northern State College at
Aberdeen, S.D. As previously stated, after serving in the Army from 1942 until
his Honorable Discharge in 1946, he and Erwin Spitzer formed a partnership and
were engaged in the John Deere dealership until January 1963.  At this time,
Erv was appointed Rural Mail Carrier, the position he still holds.

During the past 35 years, Erv has served on many civic and political positions,
Church Council, and other boards.  He served was Trustee and Treasurer and
Chairman of St. Paul's Lutheran Church at different times from 1948 through 198
1. He is a present member and held many offices of the Roscoe Legion Post since
1949.  Erv is post officer and present member of the Roscoe Rod and Gun Club;
post officer and member of the Roscoe Chamber of Commerce since 1947; post
member and Committee Chairman of the Roscoe PTA; past member of the Roscoe Fire
Department; Edmunds County Chairman Polio Drive in 1964 and received an award
certificate for surpassing quota; member of the Advisory Board Prairie States
Insurance Company of Huron, and now of Rapid City, and was on the Founders
Committee.

He was a member of the Advisory Board Stockman's Notional Life Insurance
Company; and of the Executive Advisory Board of the Business Division of
Northern State College of Aberdeen, S.D. for eight years, 1958 through 1966; he
served on the Legislative Council of the South Dakota Implement Dealers
Association during the 1961 session; elected Mayor of the City of Roscoe three
terms, 1959, 1962, and 1978 and 1980, and also served on the City Council for
eight years.  He served on the Executive Committee for the South Dakota
Municipal League from 1962 to 1963 and was elected State President of the South
Dakota Municipal League from 1962 to 1963 representing South Dakota at
Philadelphia in 1962, and Houston, Texas in 1963 at the Notional Convention of
the League of Cities.

Erv was elected to three terms to the South Dakota House of Representatives
from Edmunds County from 1954 through January 1961.  He served on the Interim
Committee on State Fiscal Policy.  Adeline was a member of the Capitol Club
during these years.

He was appointed to the South Dakota Diplomatic Corps as Ambassador for
promoting South Dakota by Governor Nils Boe, a position that he is still
actively engaged in.

Through the devotion to civic, municipal and state duties, both Erv and Adeline
were selected in Who's Who in the Upper Midwest Editions.

Since 1963, Erv has served in many capacities with the local, District 21 and
State organization of the Rural Letter Carriers Association.  He served as State
President of SDRLCA, Vice-president, committeeman, Executive Board and elected
as delegate representing the South Dakota Carriers at the National Conventions
for the past 20 years.  Erv and Adeline served as Finance Chairpersons for the
1982 Notional Rural Letter Carriers Convention at the Rushmore Plaza at Rapid
City in August, the First National RLC Convention held in South Dakota.  In
April 1980, at a eight state meeting of Federal Employees at Minneapolis, Erv
was elected Chairman of the South Dakota FAIR Coalitions representing all
Federal employees of South Dakota. He served was Advertising and Book Chairman
for the Roscoe Diamond Jubilee in 1958.

ERWIN AND LORRAINE BAER

Erwin C. Boor was born May 17, 1924 at rural Hoven, S.D. to Barnhardt B. and
Katherine Miller Boor.  Lorraine Gorne was born April 5, 1925, at rural Roscoe
to Jacob and Elsie Walz Gorne.  Jacob was born in Crimea, Russia and came to
Roscoe in 1906 and Elsie was born at Bowdle.

Erwin and Lorraine were married at Roscoe December 4, 1955.

Erwin came to the Roscoe area in 1962, first living on a farm south of Beebe.
In 1969, Erwin and Lorraine moved on the Gorne family farm southwest of Roscoe.

They are members of St. Paul's Lutheran Church.

MICHAEL AND KATHERINE BAER

        Katherine, Michael, Oscar and Orville Baer

Michael Boer was born June 28, 1891 near Selby, S.D., the son of Jacob and
Elizabeth Nuss Baer.  He married Katherine Ernst on October 4, 1910.  Katherine
was born March 22, 1891 in Crimea, Russia, and came to the Roscoe area with her
parents in 1899.  She was the daughter of Jacob and Katherine Blumhardt Ernst.

They lived on a farm in the Hoven, S.D. area, later moving to Aberdeen and
finally retiring to Roscoe.

They had four children: Jacob, deceased; Oscar, living in Seattle, Wash.; Edna
(Mrs.  Edwin Bendewold), living in Seattle, Wash.; and Bertha (Mrs.  Sam
Johnle) living in Roscoe.

Michael passed away on March 8, 1978 and Katherine passed away in 1980.  They
are both buried in Morningside Cemetery at Roscoe.

Michael served on the City Council and they were members of St. Paul's Lutheran
Church.

JACOB AND HULDA BAER

        Back row: Mark, James, Robert, Orville and Jacob; middle row: Carolyn,
        Joanne, Virginia, and Hulda; front row: Tim and Scott Baer

Jacob Baer was born September 19, 1912, in Walworth County, to Mike and
Katherine Ernst Baer. November 12, 1933, he married Hulda Miller, who was born
June 20, 1914 at Hoven, S.D. to Jacob and Louisa Baer Miller.  They settled in
the Roscoe area in 1934.

They had three sons: Orville, Robert and James, all living on farms south of
Roscoe.

Jacob died on August 7, 1972 and Hulda died June 2 1, 1979.  They are both
buried in Morningside Cemetery at Roscoe.

ORVILLE AND VIRGINIA BAER

Orville Baer was born October 21, 1935 in Bowdle, S.D., the son of Jacob and
Hulda Miller Baer.  He attended South Dakota State College at Brookings, S.D.
and served in the U.S. Army.

Virginia Huber Baer was born June 25, 1928 at Herreid, S.D., the daughter of
Adolph and Dorothea Pudwill Huber.  She received her elementary education and
then took a nursing course in Bismarck, N.D. She worked in Bismarck, Mobridge
and Aberdeen.

Orville and Virginia were married June 21, 1964.  They live on a farm south of
Roscoe where they raise grain, cattle, hogs and Appaloosa horses.

Orville is presently serving as Hillside Township supervisor, on the WEB Board
Directory, ASC County Committeeman and as church councilman.

They are members of St. Paul's Lutheran Church in Roscoe.

MICHAEL AND NANCY BARONDEAU

Michael S. Barondeau, Jr. was born September II, 1945 in Endicott, N.Y. to
Michael S. and Cora Bender Barondeau from Onaka, S.D.

Nancy Jean Mogle Barondeau was born July 29, 1950 in Flandreau, S.D. to George
H. and Gladys Weigel Mogle.

Michael moved to Onaka, S.D. in 1952 from Pennsylvania. He attended East
Sherman country school, Cresbard High School and Northern State College in
Aberdeen, S.D. He graduated in 1967 from Northern State College with a B.S. in
Secondary Education.  In 1967 he took a teaching job in Huron, S.D.

Nancy attended a country school in Moody county, Flandreau High School and
Huron College.  Michael and Nancy met while both were in Huron.  In 1969,
Michael joined the Navy and was stationed in Virginia.  Nancy graduated from
Huron College with a B.A. in Elementary Education in 1971.

        Back row: Nancy and Mike; front row: Matthew and Benjamin Barondeau

Michael and Nancy were married August 13, 1971 in Flandreau, S.D. The next two
years were spent in Virginia with Mike in the Navy and Nancy teaching first
grade at a private school.  In 1973, after his discharge from the Navy, Michael
attended graduate school at the University of South Dakota in Vermillion and
was also a graduate assistant teaching college chemistry.  Nancy attended
college part time while holding down a port time job and helping at St. Agnes
Grade School in Vermillion.  In 1974 they moved to Herreid, S.D. where Michael
taught science and Nancy taught fourth grade for one year and remedial math for
four years.  In June 1981 Michael accepted the science position at the Roscoe
School.  In August of 1981 they moved to Roscoe after purchasing the Ruth
Erlenbusch house.

They belong to St. Thomas Catholic Church in Roscoe, the Roscoe Commercial Club
and the American Legion.

They have two children: Benjamin Michael, born July 10, 1975 in Eureka, S.D.
and Matthew George, born January 1, 1977, also in Eureka.

ADELINE LAMMLE BARTSCH

Adeline Lammle was born May 21, 1912 in Edmunds County to John and Christina
Spitzer Lammle.  They moved to Roscoe when she was a small child.  She attended
grade school and high school in Roscoe and went to Mankato, Minn. to take a
course in nurses training, graduating in 1936.

October 18, 1936 Adeline and Walter Bartsch, of Mankato, were married, in
German, in St. Paul's Lutheran Church in Roscoe by Rev. J. P. Scherf.

In May, 1937, they moved to St. James, Minn. where Wait sold John Deere
equipment.  Several years later Wait and William Lammle, brother of Adeline,
bought the John Deere business and named it St. James Implement. Bill and Walt
remained partners, even while Bill was in the military service for about four
years, until December 1957 when Adeline needed a change of climate for health
reasons.  It was difficult to leave St. James as Walt was involved in man civic
and ~community affairs.  He had been mayor, city councilman, winter carnival
king, held church positions, was on the airport committee and hospital board
plus others.

They moved to Scottsbluff, Neb. then Gering, Neb.  The climate was drier and
milder in Nebraska.  He sold ~AAL insurance five years and then they moved to
Columbus, Neb. where he continued to sell AAL for 13 years.

For retirement they moved to New Ulm, Minn. in 1975. From November to April
they spent the winter at their mobile home in Desert Park, Apache Junction,
Ariz.

Adeline and Walt have four children, Darlene Deitrich, Cass Lake, Minn., who
has three daughters.

Phyllis Kenning, of Mankato, has four children.

Armond, married to Karen Yunker.  They live in LeMars, Iowa and have three
children.

Johnelle, married to Lanny Haglund, of Butterfield, Minn.  They have four
children.

SEBASTIAN AND MAGDALENA BAUMGARTNER

Although not an early-day family of Roscoe, Baumgartners have lived over 50
years in the Roscoe area.

Sebastian Baumgartner was born in Strasburg, N.D. May 19, 1891.  Magdalena
(Lena) Gross was born in Hague, N.D. September 8, 1894.  Sebastian and Lena
were married at Strasburg, N.D. November 1913.

Life long members of the Catholic Church, they joined St. Thomas the Apostle
Church when they moved to a farm near Roscoe in 1931.  They farmed until
retiring in 1956 and moved into Roscoe.  Sebastian died January 3, 1972, and is
buried in the Morningside Cemetery in Roscoe.  Lena lived there until moving to
Colonial Manor in Ipswich in 1977.

They raised 13 children, six sons and seven daughters, Adam, now deceased;
Peter of Roscoe; Sebastian Jr., who died in World War II; Wendlin, Rapid City,
S.D.; Conrad, Campell, Calif.; Anton, El Sobrante, Calif.; Mrs. Margaret Warner
of Tacoma, Wash.; Mrs. Pius (Stephina) Biegler of Bismarck, N.D.; Mrs. William
(Kathleen) Murray of Renton, Wash.; Mrs. Ernest (Christine) Dunton of Hayward,
Calif.; Mrs. Mary Ann McKay of Middle Village, N.Y. and Mrs. Roy (Vernodette)
Ross of Bremerton, Wash.

Four of the sons were in the service during World War II. Peter was in the
European Theater of Operation. Sebastian Jr. (Cecil) was killed during training
in December, 1943; Wendlin was wounded during the fighting on Okinawa in 1944.
Conrad was in Japan with the Army of Occupation.

        Lt. Sebastian Baumgartner, Jr.

PETER AND KATHERYN BAUMGARTNER

Katheryn Geier was born June 2, 1919, in Glover Township, the daughter of Adam
and Theresa Geier of Roscoe.  She lived near Roscoe all her life except for a
few years when she worked for Boeing Aircraft in Seattle, Wash. during World
War II.

Peter Baumgartner was born July 7, 1919, in Strasburg, N.D., the son of
Sebastian and Magdalena Gross Baumgartner.  He came to Roscoe with his parents
and family in 1931.  He was in the 82nd 505th Parachute Regiment for four years
during World War II.

Peter Baumgartner and Katheryn Geier were married at Columbus, Go., September
22, 1942.  They have farmed in Glover Township for over 37 years.  Katheryn was
a school teacher in the Edmunds County rural schools for 15 years.  Katheryn
died at Ipswich August 5, 1981 and is buried at Morningside Cemetery at Roscoe.
She was a member of the Catholic Church all her life. Peter is also a life long
member of the Catholic Church.

Peter now lives on their farm in Glover Township.

EDWIN AND IDA BAUER

Edwin Bauer was born August 14, 1914 at Herreid, S.D. to Reinbold and Louise
Kneopfle Bauer.

Ida Orth Bauer was born November 13, 1916 at Artas, S.D. to Fred and Martha
Fried Orth.

Edwin and Ida were married June 21, 1939.  They lived near Eureka, S.D. for six
years.  They then purchased the Charles Fischer farm near Roscoe where they
moved in the spring of 1947.  They are retired from farming but remain on the
farm place.  They ore members of the United Church of Christ.

        Back row: Lila, Edwin and Ida; front row: Francine and Diane Bauer

Three children were born to this union: Lila Louise, Diane Martha, and Francine
