Steele Richards.  She graduated from Sundance High School in Wyoming in 1976.

Leland and Tracy were married April 3, 1976 in Sundance and made their home in
Roscoe.  They have two children, Jeremy Lee, born May 25, 1977 and Christel
Brooke, born May 4, 1980.

Leland was elected Charter President of the Roscoe Jaycees and Mayor of Roscoe
in 1980.  He is also a member of the Volunteer Firemen and is active in the
Roscoe Sportsmen's Club.  He is a licensed insurance agent and Real Estate
Broker and Appraiser.

Tracy is a licensed insurance agent and a certified 81 hour Emergency Medical
Technician.

They are members of the Roscoe Commercial Club.

THE HERMAN TSCHIRLEY FAMILY

        Lizzie and Herman Tschirley

        Mary and Herman Tschirley

The Herman Tschirley family, consisting of wife, Lizzie Vandre Tschirley and
two sons, Louis and Milton, moved to Roscoe from a farm in Davidson County,
Hillside Township  S.D. in the spring of 1909.  He had sold out at auction and
bought a farm four miles south of Roscoe where he farmed for one year.  The
family arrived in Roscoe in March of 1909.  The snow on the roads was
horse-belly deep and impassable.  They moved by wagon to the farm through
crests of rolling hills and through farm fields.

In 1910 or early 1911, he sold the farm and moved to Roscoe having purchased
part interest in a hardware business known as Hartell and Tschirley.  The store
was located on the corner of Main where Ann's Apparel is now located.  In 1912
or early 1913 the store burned down and they reopened in the rear of the brick
Stevenson building.  The front part of the building housed Corbins saloon.  In
1913 a daughter, Lucile, was born.

Later he sold out the hardware business and purchased controlling interest in
the Farmers State Bank from Robert LeFebvre who moved west. He stayed in the
banking business until 1933 when the bank closed.  After that he went into the
seed business in connection with a Case Machinery Dealership.  This business
was located on N. Main, formerly Redman's General Mdse.

During WWI he was a member of the Selective Service Board.  Another member was
Judge Mahlon T. Lightner. He was state representative from Edmunds County for
two terms, 1919 and 1921.  He was chairman of the school board in 1918-1919
when the new brick school building was built.

He was chairman of the Edmunds County Selective Service Board all during WWII
and served on the board for 19 years.

During the period between the end of WWI and the depression of the 20s, he was
very active in developing the area through the Tschirley Land Co. which brought
many new settlers to the area, the sale of farm products through the Tschirley
Livestock Co. and his interests in the Farmers Elevator Co.

He also sponsored the auctioneer service provided by Col.  A. W. Wilke who
lived in the old Bashford Farm which is now the southwest part of Roscoe.

Mrs. Tschirley died in 1932 and a year later Mr. Tschirley married Mary Novotny
Sporer.

Louis (changed his name to Shirley) now lives in Hopewell, Va.  He is a retired
Army Colonel, having served in WWII.  His wife is the former Edna Williams of
Ipswich.  They have one daughter, Judith Hutchinson and four grandchildren.

Milton, a retired maintenance man, died in 1979.  His wife is the former Viola
Thielen of Roscoe.  They had two children, Arvid (deceased) and Wilma Carlson
and one grandson.  Both Viola and Wilma live in Prescott, Ariz.

Lucile married Adolph Silverman who was in the automobile business in Dupree,
S.D. for over 40 years. He is retired.

Herman Tschirley passed away in 1967 and his wife Mary in 1976.  His whole life
was dedicated to the future of South Dakota which was his lifelong home.

MILTON AND VIOLA TSCHIRLEY

        Milton Tschirley

        Viola Tschirley

Milton was born December 1, 1905 in Douglas County, S.D. to Herman and Lizzie
Vandre Tschirley.

Viola was born January 13, 1910 in Leola, S.D. and is the daughter of Henry R.
Thielen and Minnie Lemke Theilen.

Milton (Mick) came to Edmunds County with his parents at a very early age.
They lived on a farm south of Roscoe.  He went to grade school and high school
in Roscoe, graduating in 1927.  There were four members in his class.

Viola came to Roscoe when she was in the fifth grade from Bowdle, South Dakota.
Her father managed the elevator at the time.  She graduated from Roscoe High
School in 1928.  There were eight in the class.

July 22, 1928, Milton and Viola were married in Woonsocket, S.D. The depression
years--who can forget them?  September 18, 1929 their son was born in the
Borsberry house.  He was Arvid Duane.  May 21, 1935 a daughter was born in
Roscoe.  She is Wilma (Billie) Elizabeth.

They left Roscoe in about 1940 to live on the Burke farm south of Ipswich.  As
a farm wife, she felt she left a good bit to be desired.  She never did learn
to milk a cow.  Their experiences on that farm could fill a book. Shortly after
Pearl Harbor they sold out and moved to Omaha, Neb.  Mick worked in the defense
plant until after the war.

Their son loved farming and during the summer vocation in 1944 he came back to
Roscoe to work on the Martin Schurr farm.  July 31 of that year he was killed in
an automobile accident.

After the war, Mick worked for Goodyear Tire Company for a time and then went
into Sheet Metal. From there he went to work for Midwest Packing Co. as their
Stationary Engineer.  He stayed there until he retired in 1972.  In the
meantime, Viola spent 12 years managing outlet stores for Omar Baking Co.

Their daughter married Robert L. Carson in 1957.  She was a registered nurse,
having graduated from Omaha Methodist School of Nursing in 1956.  March 10, 1958
their one and only grandchild was born.  He is Brian Richard.

In June of 1972, having both retired, they moved to Prescott, Ariz. to be near
their daughter and husband who were in Phoenix.  Their son-in-law, who is a
contractor, built them a lovely home.  They had eight beautiful years of
retirement and celebrated their 50th wedding anniversary in 1978.  December 9,
1979 Mick passed away.

Viola has sold her lovely home and lives in an apartment at 320 Pioneer Dr.,
Apt. 2 in Prescott. Her daughter and son-in-law have a jointly-owned
contracting business in Prescott.  Her grandson, after a "hitch" in the Air
Force, is attending Glendale College in Phoenix.

Every fall South Dakota seems to draw her back, maybe it is the pheasant and
grouse hunting!.  She has so many good and true friends and relatives in South
Dakota and they are glad to have her visit them.  They get together over coffee
and talk non-stop about all the escapades they took part in.  Were those really
the good old days"?  They think so.  They came under the heading, "Do you
remember when?"

GABRIEL AND MAGDALENA UHRICH

Gabriel Uhrich was born in Ukraine, Russia, March 7, 1884 and was married
February 26, 1906 to Magdalena Englehart who was born September 29, 1882.
After the marriage, he and his wife decided to came to America to live with his
brother, Stanuslous, on a farm six miles south of Roscoe.  He farmed with his
brother for 20 years and their farm was known as the "Uhrich Brothers".  After
that he purchased his own farm, seven miles south of Roscoe.  They continued
farming until November 25, 1941, when he sold his farm to his son Joe and
purchased a home in Roscoe and retired.

Gabriel and Magdalena became the parents of six children.  Elizabeth was born
May 2, 1908 and married Andrew Daschle of Onaka, S.D. November 9, 1927. They
have one son, Col.  Charles Daschle, in the Army, serving as military attache
to Austria.  He is married and has two children.

Elizabeth passed away July 9, 1981, and Andrew died June 26, 1982.  They are
both buried in Sunset Memorial Gardens in Aberdeen.

Joseph was born August 19, 1909 and married Mary Daschle of Onaka June 27,
1932.  He purchased his father's farm and lived there until 1956, when they
moved to Aberdeen.  They have three children, Marvin, Luella and Judy.

Joe passed away May 14, 1981 and is buried in Sunset Memorial Gardens.  Mary
lives and works in Aberdeen.

Helen was born January 24, 1911 and married Peter Sautner.  They farmed in the
Hoven area until Pete passed away, then Helen lived in Aberdeen until her death
May 7, 1978.  They are both buried in Hoven.  They had a family of five,
Donnie, Aester, Richard and Sister Adel in St. James Convent in Duluth, Minn.

Walburga was born June 3, 1912 and married Don Zipf of Bristol, S.D. They moved
to Oakland, Calif. where Don was a fireman.  They have one daughter Cairn.

Adeline was born April 5, 1914 and married John Meier of Roscoe November 16,
1933.  She passed away October 25, 1964 and is buried in Morningside Cemetery
in Roscoe.  John lives in Aberdeen.

Anna was born September 15, 1918 and married Willard Cain in Oakland, Calif.
They had three children. Anna passed away March 20, 1970.

They were members of St. Thomas Catholic Church in Roscoe.  Gabriel passed away
in 1969 and Magdalena passed away in 1961.  They are both buried in Morningside
Cemetery in Roscoe.

        Back row: Luella, Marvin and Judy; front row: Joe and Mary Uhrich.

RICK AND CLETA VARGASON

        Rick, Jesse, Cleta and Wendy Vargason

Rick Vargason was born at Ft.  Lewis, Wash. to Loren and Joyce Oban Vargason
September 21, 1955.  Rick and his family lived in Tacoma, Wash. for four months,
in St. Paul, Minn. for four years and then in Prior Lake, Minn. where he
attended the Prior Lake Grade School.

In 1968, they moved to Loyalton, S.D. where he attended a farm school for two
and one-half years.  Rick graduated from Roscoe High School after which he went
to work for the Pickard Bee Farm in October 1975 until March 1977.  July 9,
1977 he married Cleta Huft. Cleta is the daughter of Philip and Leona Haux Huft.

She was born at Bowdle, S.D. July 29, 1959 and lived the first 10 years of her
life on a farm northwest of Roscoe.  Her family of three older brothers and one
younger sister moved to Roscoe in the fall of 1969.  In 1972, another sister
joined the family.  Cleta attended Roscoe Public School for 13 years.  In the
school year of 1976-77, she was the first runnerup in the Snow Queen contest.

October 7, 1977 was the start of their family.  A little boy named Jesse Lee
was born at Ipswich Community Hospital.  Jesse had the adventure of many
travels in his first years.  He now goes to Head Start.

        In memory of Joe Uhrich and his parents, Mr. and Mrs. Gabriel Uhrich

From December 1977 to July 1978, they lived in Minneapolis, Minn.  In August
1978, they moved back to Roscoe where Rick is a truck driver for the Pickard Bee
Farms.  Cleta is also a part-time employee with the Bee Farm.  Their jobs have
given them the opportunity to travel.  Each year in January they move to Texas,
coming back north in the summer.  The job has also taken them to California.
Rick makes trips to Sioux City, Iowa to deliver honey to the Sioux Honey Assn.
and to Oregon for lumber that is needed for new bee boxes and frames.

Life settled down for Cleta when a baby girl named Wendy Lea was born May 13,
1982.  They plan to make their home in Roscoe for many years.

CHRISTEN (FINDER) VELTE (Excerpt from Diamond Jubilee Book)

Christen's family moved to Main Street, Roscoe, in April, 1904.  Her father
built the first cellar in town and then was busy many years building more.
They were also the first family in town to have a well dug.  It was 478 feet
deep and had very good water.  She recalled having to haul water from the old
town well before then in two barrels on a stone boat pulled by two fine horses.

JOHN AND ELIZABETH VETCH

        John Vetch

John Vetch, one of 12 children, was born February 15, 1894 to Anton and
Katherine Wymart Vetch at Eureka, S.D. The family moved to Roscoe in 1917.
December 17, 1918 he married Elizabeth Biernbaum.  Elizabeth was born March 28,
1902 to Peter and Margaret Gangler Biernbaum, who did masonry work, helping
build the St. James Hotel building and the drug store which are still located
on Main Street in Roscoe.

Peter was killed in an automobile accident in 1925, nine miles south of Roscoe.
Margaret continued to live in Roscoe until 1949 when she moved to Aberdeen.
She returned to Roscoe in 1963 and lived with her daughter and son-in-law until
her death later in 1963.

        Elizabeth Vetch

John and Elizabeth farmed in the Roscoe vicinity until they retired in 1965 and
moved to Bowdle, S. D. There they were members of the St. Augustine Catholic
Church.  They had 11 children, including twins, Iola, married in 1941 to Alfred
Bradke of Barnard.

Viola was married in 1943 to Fred W. Hettick of Onaka.

Lawrence was married to Milda Aldinger of Bowdle in 1947.

Eva died in infancy.

Paul married Katherine Malsom of Hosmer in 1948.

Johnnie married Orma Stoecker in 1948 of Hosmer.

Bernice married Athneal Buechler of Bowdle in 1950.

Anna Mae married Roger Kraft of Ipswich in 1951.

Walburga married Walter Zambo of Selby in 1952.

Gladys married Raymond Preszler of Hosmer in 1957.

LeRoy married Donna Waldmeier of Belvidere, Neb. in 1963.

They were blessed with 56 grandchildren.

Elizabeth passed away November 11, 1970 and John January 10, 1980.  They are
buried in Morningside Cemetery.

MERILYN LAMMLE VIELHABER

CHRISTIAN AND CRISTIANA VOEGELE

Christian Voegele was born in South Russia to John and Frederika Voegele (nee
Martz).  He came to America with his parents when he was three years old. The
family settled 15 miles northeast of Hosmer, where they homesteaded land and
lived in a sod shanty.

When Christian was a young man, he too, homesteaded some land 12 miles east of
Hosmer and established a new home.  July 1, 1906 Christian and Christiana
Ladner were united in marriage at Eureka, S.D. The family lived on the
homestead with the exception of three years, which they spent at Eureka, S.D.
until 1922 when they moved to a farm five miles northeast of Roscoe.  The
family included four children. They were Emma, Irene, Alvin and Reuben.  Alvin,
Emma and Reuben have died.  Christian died in 1939 at the age of 57.

        Back row: Alvin and Irene; front row: Christian and Christiana Voegele.

Mrs. Voegele died in 1964 at the age of 79 years.  They were members of St.
Paul's Lutheran Church at Roscoe, and are all buried in the Roscoe Cemetery,
except Alvin, who is buried at Texarkana, Arkansas.

Irene thinks back to pioneer days and with a grateful heart gives thanks to the
Giver of all gifts for 100 years of progress that have replaced the sod shanty
and the oxen team with the modern farm and it's electrical appliances, with the
power-driven equipment.  She also gives thanks for improved roads, the
beautiful churches that have taken the place of the rural churches, the much
improved school houses that are taking the place of the rural schools.

We remember our pioneer families that are in the back yard of the rural
churches and those in other cemeteries that are marked with grave markers.  Our
thoughts go back to those that did not live to enjoy the modern living in this
rural area.

JOHN B. AND PAULINE VOEGELE

John B. Voegele was born December 10, 1897 in Russia.  His parents were Bernard
and Elizabeth Voegele.  The family came to the United States in 1911. They
settled near Tolstoy and then south of Hosmer. Pauline nee Pleinis Voegele was
born in Russia August 19, 1904.  She was born to Mr. and Mrs. Martin Pleinis
who came from Russia and settled in the Roscoe area.

John and Pauline were joined in marriage March 8, 1922 at Hosmer.  They farmed
near Roscoe from 1934 to 1961.  In 1961, they moved to Aberdeen.  John passed
away November 6, 1963.  He was buried in Riverside Memorial Park Cemetery in
Aberdeen, South Dakota. He was a member of Trinity Lutheran Church.  Pauline is
currently living in Aberdeen and is a member of Trinity Lutheran Church.

        Back row: Elmer, Mavis, Helmuth, Irene, Lester, Edo, and Melvin; front
        row: Pauline and John B. Voegele.

John and Pauline had nine children.

Ervin lives in Bonners Ferry, Idaho.

Helmuth is married to Florence Gaub and lives in Aberdeen, S.D.

Elmer is married to Gladys Guthmiller and lives in Aberdeen, S.D.

Irene is married to Edwin Martz and lives in Roscoe, S.D.

JOHN J. AND ELIZABETH VOEGELE

John J. Voegele, Sr. and Elizabeth Jacober were both born in Gluckstal, South
Russia.  They were married in 1888 and Mr. Voegele was a medic with the Russian
Army for four years in the town of Odessa. Seven children were born, John,
Jacob J., Margret, Bernhardt and Lydia.  Two children died while very young.

Mr. Voegele later became a parochial school teacher in the Evangelical Lutheran
Church.  The family came to America in 1911 and settled near Tolstoy, S.D. on a
farm.  In 1941, they moved to Roscoe, where they lived the rest of their lives.
Mrs. Voegele passed away in 1945.  Mr. Voegele lived to be almost 97 years old
and passed away in 1955.  They are both buried in Roscoe's Morningside
Cemetery.  The only children still living are Bernhardt in Roscoe and Lydia,
Mrs. Ray Utke in Milwaukee, Wis.

JACOB J. AND LYDIA VOEGELE

Jacob Voegele was born May 17, 1893, the son of John and Elizabeth, nee
Jacober, Voegele.  He came to the United States from Gluckstal, Russia in 1911,
and settled near Tolstoy, S.D. He served in WW I to make things easier for the
rest of his family who at that time had not became United States citizens.  Mr.
Voegele spoke many times what a great privilege it was to became a citizen of
the United States.

In 1920, Jacob Voegele married Lydia Schumacher, daughter of Ludwig and
Christina, nee Heidt, Schumacher.  She was born January 20, 1900 near Hosmer,
S.D. The couple settled on a farm northwest of Roscoe.  Later they moved to a
farm six miles west of Roscoe.  In 1933, they moved one-half mile northeast of
Roscoe to the old "Judge Lightner" farm.  The farm was at that time owned by
Norgan & Hardt Insurance Co. as Judge Lightner had lost the farm during the
depression. In 1937, the Insurance Company tore all the old buildings down and
replaced them with new buildings. The old house was the only original building
left, and that was replaced in 1950 by their daughter and son-in- law, the
Oscar Jeschkes.

The Voegeles bought the farm and raised their family of five girls and one boy
there.  The children are, Martha, married to Oscar Jeschke.  They bought the
Voegeles farm in 1946 and lived there until their retirement in 1981.  Collin
and Gail Jeschke, grandson of the Voegeles, now owns the farm. Ellen, married
Norman Kuecker, and lives at Webster. They are retired now.  Ellen had been a
nurse for 25 years.

Ramona is married to Harold Markuson of Ipswich. They are retired.  Ramona
taught school for 25 years.

Lenore is married to Allen Brugmon.  They live in Lafayette, La., where her
husband is Vice President of Geologists of the Union Oil Co.

Adelida married Richard Lassle and lives in Aberdeen. She has been a secretary
of K. O. Lee Co. for 23 years.

The only son, Milbert, better known as Bert, lives in Watertown, S.D. He is
married to Ramona Kilber.  Bert is manager of the 3 County Electric Corp.

The Voegeles retired and moved to Roscoe in 1946. Mrs. Voegele passed away in
1970.  Mr. Voegele passed away in 1977.  They are both buried in the Morningside
Cemetery in Roscoe.

Mr. Voegele had been a Bryant Township supervisor. They were active in the
Congregational Church.  Mr. Voegele served as Sunday school teacher, elder and
choir director.  Mrs. Voegele was a charter member of the Ladies Aid.

Times were hard for the Voegeles in the dirty thirties. Big clouds of dust
would come swirling early in the morning.  By noon the sun was hard to be
found.  One could feel and see the eerie feeling of twilight at high noon.
Crops were poor from drought and the millions of grasshoppers.  One year the
grain binders and headers were not even brought out of storage.  There was
hardly a blade of gross.  The government bought the skinny, hungry cattle and
paid a mere pittance for them.  The Voegeles had nine cows.  Seven were bought
by the government.  Mr. Voegele and the oldest daughters, Martha and Ellen,
helped drive the cows into Roscoe to the stockyards on the west edge of town.
There a huge trench had been dug.  Mr. Voegele made the two daughters walk home
immediately.  He returned home a few hours later and with tears streaming down
his cheeks, told the cows were shot and thrown into the trench and covered up.
What a waste this seemed to the Voegeles who had no means of having meat on the
table.  Somehow the Lord provided for a family who trusted in Him.  Every
evening found the family gathered around the pump organ singing hymns. Family
love was the important happening in those days. It helped make things seem
easier.

BERNHARDT AND KATHERINE VOEGELE

Bernhardt Voegele was born April 7, 1901 in Larka, South Russia to John and
Elizabeth Jacober Voegele. They came to the United States June 10, 1910,
settling in the Tolstoy, S.D. area.  They moved to the Roscoe area in 1941.
Katherina Bashans was born July 2, 1901 at Roscoe to Henry and Elizabeth
Weindel Bashans who came to the United States about 1898.

Bernhardt and Katherina were married December 10, 1925 at Roscoe.  They farmed
in the Onaka, S.D. area until 1940 when they moved to a farm northeast of
Roscoe.  This is where they lived until 1958 when they moved to Roscoe.

Bernhardt served on the school board at Onaka, as township assessor in Bryant
Township and Sunday school teacher and treasurer of their church.

They had six children, Violet (Mrs. LeRoy Sauer) farming near Roscoe; Clarence,
farming near Roscoe; Vivian (Mrs.  Calvin Rohrbach) living in Rapid City, S.D.
where Vivian is employed at Control Data; Loyd, deceased in 1957 and Edward and
Edwin, (twins) deceased in 1976 and 1978.

EDWARD AND ESTHER WERRE

        Back row: Harry, Esther, and Ed Werre; front row: Jean, Darrin, and
        Frank Farr.

Edward Werre was born September 20, 1906, near Hosmer, S.D., the son of Philip
and Christina Hottman Werre.  They were born in Gluckstal, S. Russia and came
to the United States in 1891.  This is where Ed grew up and attended country
school.  Ed served in the Army during WWII from April 9, 1942 to June 28, 1943.
He was stationed at Fort Knox, Ky. and Camp Campbell, Ky.

Esther Knittel was born December 15, 1913 near Tripp, S.D., the daughter of
William and Eliza Walz Knittel.  In 1927 she came to Roscoe with her family and
over the years they lived on several farms in the area and settled on a farm
one and one-half miles northeast of Roscoe.  Esther attended rural schools and
graduated from Roscoe High School in 1937.  She attended NSC for one year and
taught rural schools for nine years and later did some substitute teaching in
the Roscoe School System.

Ed and Esther were married May 19, 1944 at Roscoe. They made their home on
Esthers' family farm where they are still living at the present time.  They did
small grain farming and raised livestock until 1978 when his health forced him
to retire.

They have two children, Jean, born January 25, 1946, and she is married to
Frank Farr and lives in South Haven, Miss. where Frank works for ITC.  They
have one son, Darrin.

Harry was born December 5, 1953, lives in Aberdeen, S.D. and works at the
Adjustment Training Center.

Ed and Esther are members of St. Paul's Lutheran Church in Roscoe.  Esther is a
member of the Ladies Aid where she served eight years as president.  She also
belongs to the American Legion Auxiliary and Ed belongs to the American Legion.

AUGUST WIEDENMEYER FAMILY (Excerpt from Diamond Jubilee Book)

Mr. Wiedenmeyer was born in 1873 in Krem, South Russia.  He left Russia with
his wife and children in the fall of 1907 and moved to Roscoe in 1908.  He had
various odd jobs such as unloading coal for $1.35 a day. By 1911, Mr.
Wiedenmeyer got a job with the Milwaukee Railroad which he held for 20 years
until his health failed.

He died the next year, 1932, but his wife lived here until the seventies when
she became a resident of the Colonial Manor in Ipswich.  She passed away in 1977
and was buried next to her husband in the Roscoe Cemetery.

GOTTLIEB AND MARTHA WIEDRICH

Gottlieb Wiedrich was born February 9, 1892 in Neudorf, Russia to Gottlieb
Wiedrich (born January 27, 1866 in Russia) and Fredricka Dewald (t) (born in
Russia April 22, 1869). In 1899, the family came to

        Sponsored by Marvin and Lorraine Uhrich

America, but the mother had to return to Russia for six months because of pink
eye.  The father and three boys and one girl stayed in Menno, S.D. until the
mother was allowed in the country.  The family then settled on a farm near
Hillsview and then they moved to a farm near Hosmer.

They had four more children before the father was killed in an accident while
moving to Hosmer.  He got ran over by a wagon loaded with railroad ties at the
age of 57.

Gottlieb married Martha Kirschenmann in February of 1916.  She was born August
15, 1894 on a farm north of Hosmer to Christian Kirschenmann (born August 15,
1851) and Philibina Walz (born May 3, 1852), both born in Kassel, Russia.  She
was the youngest of 11 children, six born in Neudorf, Russia, one in Menno and
four in Hosmer, S.D.

They made their home on a farm seven miles northwest of Roscoe and had eight
children: Lech Schnabel, Ellendale, N.D.; Frieda Schumacher Forbes, Aberdeen,
S.D.; Henry, Aberdeen, S.D.; Ruth Obenauer, Eureka, S.D.; Theodore, Rapid City,
S.D.; Milbert, Mount Vernon, Ind.; Alice Kallenberger, Eureka, S.D. and
Lorraine Reidt, Onaka, S.D.

They were members of the Congregational Church in Hosmer.  In May of 1945,
Gottlieb died and Martha moved to Roscoe where she lived 18 years.  She then
moved to Eureka and then Aberdeen where she died in 1979.

HENRY AND RUTH WIEDRICH

        Back row: Kenneth and Glenn; front row: Henry, Ruth and Henry Lee
        Wiedrich.

Henry Wiedrich was born May 25, 1920 on a farm seven miles northwest of Roscoe.
His parents were Gottlieb and Martha Kirschenmann.  He was the third child of
eight and attended country school six miles from Roscoe.

His wife, Ruth Evelyn Key, was born September 23, 1920, in Smith Mills, Ky. to
Reeves C. Key and Cora Hoy. Her father died in 1969 and her mother still lives
in Corydon, Ky.  Henry and Ruth were married in Evansville, Ind.  December of
1943 after meeting while Henry was in the service.  He went overseas in March of
1944 and in December of 1945 they moved to the Jake Mayer farm, three miles
north of Roscoe.  It was there that their first son, Ken, was born.

They farmed until 1946 when they moved to Roscoe and started Wiedrich
Construction.  They had two more boys, Glenn and Henry.

Ruth worked in the cafe and later in the school cafeteria.  They moved to
Aberdeen in 1973 where Henry is employed by Quint City Coin and Ruth works for
the Aberdeen School System in Food Service.

Ken, who has five children, and Glenn, who has two children, both live in
Roscoe.  Henry now lives in Keenesburg, Colo. and coaches football.  He has five
children, Cory, De Jay, Katy, Jeffery, and Henry.

KENNETH AND DIANE WIEDRICH

        Back row: Michael; middle row: Kenneth, Tracy; front row: Wade, Diane,
        Nathan, and Tamara Wiedrich.

Kenneth Clyde Wiedrich was born September 18, 1946 on a farm three miles north
of Roscoe to Henry and Ruth Key Wiedrich, who now live near Aberdeen, S.D.

Diane Bauer Wiedrich was born January 7, 1945 on a farm near Eureka, S.D. to
Edwin and Ida Orth Bauer. When Diane was two years old her family moved to a
farm northeast of Roscoe where her parents still live.

Both Diane and Kenneth are graduates of Roscoe High School.  Ken graduated in
1964 and Diane in 1963.

Ken and Diane were married September 5, 1965 at the Roscoe United Church of
Christ.  They first made their home in Aberdeen, S.D. After Ken graduated from
NSC, they moved to Minneapolis, Minn., where Ken worked as an accountant for
Cargill, Inc.  In November of 1979, they moved back to Roscoe, where Ken went
into the construction business with his brother, Glenn.  He is also an agent
for A. L. Williams & Assoc.

They are members of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-Day Saints in Aberdeen.

Their children are Michael Wayne, born February 17, 1967 at Aberdeen; Tracy
Lee, born December 20, 1968 at Aberdeen; Tamara Ann, born July 20, 1972 at Coon
Rapids, Minn.; Wade Owen, born June 6, 1977 at Coon Rapids, Minn. and Nathan
Evan, born February 12, 1980 at Ipswich, S.D.

GLENN AND RAE WIEDRICH

        Glenn, Rae, Nadya and Kasey Wiedrich

Glenn was born September 8, 1948 in Ipswich, S.D. His parents are Henry and
Ruth Key Wiedrich, now of Aberdeen, S.D.

He attended Roscoe High School, NSC in Aberdeen and United Theological Seminary
in Minneapolis, Minn. In 1973 he returned to operate Wiedrich Construction.

Glenn married Rae Hippen in June of 1974.  Rae was born October 21, 1948 to Ben
Hippen and Jeanne Steele and was brought up on her parents farm near Wilmot,
S.D. She attended Jamestown College and graduated from SDSU in Brookings.  In
August of 1969 she moved to the Twin Cities where she met Glenn.

They have two children, Nadya and Kasey.  Both Glenn and Rae are involved with
the construction company.

Glenn is a member of the Roscoe Volunteer Fire Department and Rae is a member
of the Ivy Circle Garden Club.

GEORGE O. AND GRACE WILSON (Excerpt from Diamond Jubilee Book)

Mr. Wilson was born February 10, 1883 in Berlin Township, St. Clair County,
Mich., the son of Warren Philemon Wilson and Alice Cobb Wilson.  The family
came in the spring of 1885 from Michigan to join Warren Wilson, who had
homesteaded three and one- half miles south of Roscoe in the spring of 1883.

Mr. Wilson spent his boyhood on a farm and was educated in Roscoe and Ipswich
and Aberdeen Business College.  He managed lumber companies in Selby, Eureka,
Fargo, Strasburg, Pollock and Roscoe.

February 1907 Mr. Wilson married Grace Lavinia Fountain at Pollock and they
returned to make their home in Roscoe.  He served as chief of the Roscoe Fire
Department, on the school board for several years, as town clerk and town
treasurer.

Mrs. Wilson passed away in 1952 and Mr. Wilson in 1961.  Both are buried in the
Morningside Cemetery.

GEORGE AND ROSINA WINKLER

George Winkler, born in Sarata Bessarabie, Russia June 1, 1878, came to the
United States in the year of 1900 December 20 and arrived at Roscoe, S.D.
December 24, 1900 with his uncle, Karl Winkler.

He took up a homestead in Glover Township, T. 122, R71, School District No. 22.
Hd 4-03-02.  Evidently he didn't improve the homestead, as there was no record
in Ipswich of final papers in his name.  Perhaps he had a crop failure and
couldn't survive without a income.

He went to Canada and took up a homestead in the Elkwater area, somewhere near
Medicine Hot, Alberta, Canada.

        Back row: Rosina, Oscar and George; front row: Carl, Albert, and Emma
        Winkler.

        E. W., B. H., H. A., John, George, A. F., Carl and Oscar Winkler

In the year of February, 1906 he married Rosina Engelhardt, born December 23,
1888.  She came to the United States in 1899 with her parents from Worms
(Cershon District) South Russia, who settled in or near Bowdle, S.D. and then
went to Canada.

In the year of 1913 George and family returned to the United States and settled
in Grant County in Washington state.

He died October 11, 1950.  Rosina died March 19, 1927.

KARL AND MAGDALENA WINKLER

Karl Winkler was born January 28, 1866 in Tiplix, . Russia.  Magdalena
(Dabbler) Winkler was also born in Tiplix January 1, 1870.  The couple were
married in Russia and came to the United States on the ship "Kaiser Wilhelm" in
1900 with the four young children their marriage had been blessed with so far.

After arriving in the United States, the family came by train to Bowdle and
settled in the Java area.  Karl worked for the railroad for a short time and
then homesteaded with his family in southwest Glover Township of Roscoe. In
1911, the family built a farm two miles south and two miles west of Roscoe.
The family owned a large threshing rig and did much of the threshing in the
area, sometimes working until the snow fell in the fall and finally finishing
the prior year's crop the following spring.

Karl and Magdalena built a new home in Roscoe in 1921 but lived there only a
short while before returning to the farm where Karl passed away September 28,
1934.  Magdalena returned to their home in town after Karl's death and lived
there until she passed away January 10, 1953.  Karl and Magdalena are both
buried in Morningside Cemetery north of Roscoe.

        Back row: Alfred, Ellis, Oscar, Bill, Theophil, Olga, Albert, Kate and
        Gust; front row: Magdalena and Carl Winkler.

The couple was blessed with 18 children during their marriage, nine dying in
infancy.  Six of the nine children who grew up in this area, Gust, Katherine,
Albert, Oscar, Ellis and Alfred have passed away.  The three surviving children
of Karl and Magdalena are Olga Buechler and Theophil Winkler of Roscoe and
William Winkler of Longview, Wash.  Members of the family operated the family
farm until it was sold.

THEOPHIL AND EMMA WINKLER

Theophil Winkler was born September 18, 1906 in Glover Township to Karl and
Magdalena Winkler.  He attended rural school and the Roscoe public school. Emma
(Buechler) Winkler was born March 7, 1907 in Walworth County to Fred and
Elizabeth Buechler.  She received her education in a rural school in that area.

Theophil and Emma were married March 6, 1930.  The couple lived on the family
farm southwest of Roscoe with two brothers until 1942 when they purchased a
farm two miles south and one-half mile west of Roscoe. They lived there until
1958, when they sold the farm and moved to their present home in Roscoe.  Phil
continued doing some farming until he retired in 1966. After retiring, he
helped some of the area farmers with spring planting and harvest.  Emma, a
housewife and mother, helped on the family farm, and, after moving to town, did
the cleaning for the First State Bank of Roscoe until 1981.  They are members
of St. Paul's Lutheran Church where Phil served as trustee for 12 years and
elder for three years.  Phil was township supervisor for 15 years and served on
the city council.  Phil and Emma are members of the Senior Citizens group in
Roscoe.

        Back row: Bernice and Janell; front row: Theophil and Emma Winkler.

Five children were born to the union.  Three of the children, Calvin, Norman,
and Janet died in infancy. Janell presently lives in Aberdeen and Bernice (Mrs.
Earl Hettick) lives in Roscoe.  The couple has five grandchildren and one
great-grandchild.

FRANKLIN AND FRANCES WITTER

        Franklin

Franklin Eberle Witter was born in Chagrin Foils, Ohio on April 7, 1848.  His
father was a doctor, Amos Witter, and they lived in various places in Ohio.
Franklin Eberle had three sisters, two older than he and one younger.

Dr. Witter decided he would go to the gold "diggins" in California, so they
sold what they owned and with a covered wagon and three teams of oxen, they set
out for California.  All went well until they reached Dubuque, Iowa where they
had to cross the Mississippi River.  There they found the ferry beat they had
to use was in very poor shape.  Finally, they bought the ferry, rebuilt it and
ran it for several years, having given up going to California.

In 1857, they finally decided to make their permanent home in Mt. Vernon, Iowa,
where there was a small college as he wanted his children to have an education.
Only one of his four children took advantage of the opportunity.  The oldest
daughter, Mary Ann, was graduated from Cornell College there.  She spent most
of her life teaching in Denver, Colo.

About the time they were settled in Mt.  Vernon, the Civil War broke out and
Dr. Witter enlisted and was commissioned as surgeon of the famous Iowa Seventh
Infantry.  He was so exhausted by his work that he was sent home for a rest
which proved to be final as he passed away.

After that, Franklin E., who was too young to enlist, and his cousin, George
Witter, ran away from home and enlisted as landsmen in the Navy.  They were
stationed on the gun boat "The Mound City" on the Mississippi River.

Mrs. Frances Eliza Witter, nee Foster, was born at Fountain Prairie, Wis.  July
7, 1852.  Her father was Richard Foster, her mother was Paulina Smith Foster.
Her father died and left his family with nothing but a small farm in Minnesota.
Eventually they traded this farm for one near Fairview, Iowa, near where
Paulina's brother and several sisters lived.

        Frances

Franklin Eberle Witter and Frances Eliza Foster were married August 16, 1876 at
Anamoso, Iowa.  They lived at Mt.  Vernon until 1898, when they brought their
family to the vicinity of Roscoe, S.D., near where he had filed on a homestead.
He had built a house and born there.  The family consisted of Franklin and
Frances and six children, five daughters and one son.  The oldest was a
daughter, Mary, who was old enough to file on a quarter section of land and had
already done so.  The son, Marvin, was the second child and he was looking
forward to the time when he could file on a homestead too.  Marvin went to Iowa
Agriculture College in Ames, Iowa, in 1899 and would start his second year in
1900. It was planned that he should come home in the fall and vote for the
first time and also file on that land for his farm.  But he never came home
again.  That fall they had a typhoid fever epidemic at the dormitory where he
lived and he was one of the casualties.  He is buried at Mt. Vernon, Iowa.

The land that Franklin Witter filed on as a homestead lies four miles south and
two miles east of Roscoe.  All the buildings ore gone now, nothing left but
memories.

Franklin Eberle bought a home in Roscoe and spent his last days there.  He died
in 1938 and Frances followed him in 1941.  They ore buried in Aberdeen, S.D.
One of their daughters is Mrs. Paul (Clara) Elford.

JACOB B. AND HARMKA WOLD

        Jacob B. Wold

Jacob B. Wold was born in Norway and lived his early childhood in Minnesota.
In the early 1900s he came to the Aberdeen area and worked with various
threshing rigs as an engineer.  In 1918, he came to Roscoe where he built the
first electric light plant.  In 1920, he married Harmka Brinkema.  The couple
lived their entire married life in Roscoe residing in the house now owned by
Randy's Building Supplies.

Mrs. Wold, who was a sister of Ben Mulder, Sr., immigrated from Bertjc, Holland
in the late 1800s.

The couple had two daughters, Mrs. Katie Wruck, New Liepzig, N.D. and Edna
Richardson who resides in California.

Mrs. Wold passed away in 1948 and Mr. Wold passed away in 1956.  They both were
laid to rest in the Morningside Cemetery at Roscoe.

EDD C. WOLFF

Edd C. Wolff was born November 17, 1892 in the Bowdle-Hoven area known as the
Baer Community.  His parents were Christ and Magdalena Fry Wolff who immigrated
to this country from Russia in 1886.  Louise Seidel was born February 10, 1893
in Glucksdorf, South Russia to Frank and Elizabeth (Kautz) Seidel.  At the age
of nine she came to America with her parents and settled at Meadow, S.D.

Ed and Louise were married at Bison, S.D. February 8, 1913. In the early days
of their married life they lived in the Meadow and Bison area and in November
1927 they sold their farm near Meadow and moved to the Tolstoy-Onaka area and
in 1941 they moved to a farm that they purchased south of Tolstoy and lived
there until they retired in 1957 and moved into Tolstoy.

In September 1976, they moved to Bowdle to be near a doctor and hospital
because of Mrs. Wolff's health. in February 1978, Mrs. Wolff entered the
Colonial Manor in Ipswich.  Mr. Wolff moved to Roscoe in September of 1978 so
he could be closer to his wife.  Mrs. Wolff passed away November 17, 1981.  Mr.
Wolff continues living by himself doing some of his household duties and taking
care of the yard.  He says he doesn't like to cook so he goes to Donnie's Cafe
for his main meal.  He is enjoying good health and walks down town whenever
possible.

Mr. Wolff has 12 living children who are: Mrs. Henry (Evelyn) Dickhout of
Tolstoy, S.D.; Rheinhold of Yankton, S.D.; Mrs. Katherine Engel of Colona, Ill.;
Adolph of Denver, Colo.; Mrs. Norman (Esther) Schatz of Roscoe, S.D.; Edward of
Moline, Ill.; Mrs. James (Clara) Stanley of Hoven, S.D.; Mrs. Marvin (Lillian)
Schroeder of Ft.  Collins, Colo.; Mrs. Charles (Lucille) Johnson of
Philadelphia, Pa.; Clifford of Hanford, Calif.; Robert of Lakewood, Colo. and
Mrs. Charles (Shirlee) Kleeman of Northglen, Colo.

Mr. Wolff is also the proud parent of 40 grandchildren, 66 great-grandchildren
and one great-great-grandchild.

LYDIA LAMMLE WOLNER

Lydia was born in Roscoe August II, 1910 to John and Christina Spitzer Lammle.
She was confirmed in St. Paul's Lutheran Church, attended grade school in
Roscoe and Aberdeen Business College after which she worked at the Farmers
State Bank in Roscoe, in Ipswich, then Aberdeen where she worked as a
stenographer for six years.

Lydia was married to Gordon Wolner July 4, 1942 at St. James, Minn.  She died
of Sarcoma February 12, 1943 and is buried in Mt.  Hope Cemetery in St. James,
Minn.

OTTO AND SOPHIA WUDEL

Otto Wudel was born January 5, 1883 in Parkston, S.D. to John and Sophia Henke
Wudel, who were born in South Russia.  In 1904, he married Sophia Bertsch at
Parkston.  She was the daughter of Henry and Christina Isaak Bertsch, who came
to this country from South Russia.  Sophia was born in Kaylor, S.D. September 2,
1885.

The Wudels arrived in Roscoe in 1916 from Parkston where he had been a well
driller.  He went into the Hardware business in Roscoe.  In later years, he
operated a shoe and harness repair shop.  Mr. Wudel was president of the Roscoe
Shipping Association for several years, served as Mayor of Roscoe for several
terms and was a member of the Roscoe School Board for 15 years.  Mrs. Wudel
managed the Roscoe Hotel for many years.

They have five children, Edwina, married to Henry Wiedenmeyer, retired, and
lives in Snow Grove Heights, So.  St. Paul, Minn.

Art, married Meta Walz, deceased, was in the shoe business in Huron, S.D. and
is buried there.

Bill married Rose Ellwein and was with Sears Roebuck for 30 years.  He lives in
Aberdeen, S.D. Elton, deceased, was in the military and is buried in Raymond,
Wash.

Martha is married to Harry Rohe, lives in Raymond, Wash.

Emma, married Elmer Lou, is widowed and lives in Tampa, Fla.

Otto died September 30, 1944 and Sophia died in March of 1960.  They are both
buried in Roscoe's Morningside Cemetery.  They were of the Lutheran faith.

AUGUST AND EMMA (LAABS) ZANDER (Excerpt from the Diamond Jubilee Book)

Emma Laabs came to the Roscoe area in the spring of 1884 from Wausau, Wis. with
her mother and sister to join her father, William Laabs.  They homesteaded on a
farm nine and one-half miles southeast of Roscoe.

When they first came, Roscoe consisted only of two buildings, a blacksmith shop
located where the Farmers State Bank was later built and part of the Basford
home. This house served as a stopping over place for travelers driving through
as well as a home.  Later a grocery store was built by a Mr. Sims.  At that
time it seemed the main street was going to be where U.S. 12 now is.  But when
it became apparent that the railroad was coming through, it took shape as it
now is.  Another early building was the Shipple Hotel and a produce building
which later was moved.  A harness shop operated by a Mr. Wake was built where
the vacant lot now is next to Scherf's Store.  Later a warehouse was built and
a large store.  The store was burned down before it was completed.

March 23 of 1892, Emma married August Zander. After working in a meat market in
Aberdeen and farming Loyalton, they came to Roscoe in 1904.  The home Emma's
parents built in 1884 was moved to Roscoe and the couple lived in it until
1944.  They operated a meat market for 14 years and a general store for two
years after which they had farming interests. Mr. Zander served on the city
council for many years and was chairman of the board.

August died in 1944 and is buried in the Roscoe Cemetery.  Emma went to make
her home with her children, mostly on the west coast, where she died November
30, 1970 and was buried at Dallas, Ore.

        John, LeRoy Allan, Bernhardt and Clarence Voegele

        Peter and Margaret Biernbaum

        The Henry and Lydia Trefz family

        Four generations: Pius K., Kasper, Daniel and Robert Malsom

ROSCOE HERALD May 1, 1890
Dr. Swengel, the dentist from Aberdeen, did a flourishing business at the
Roscoe House last night and today in extracting troublesome cuspids, bicuspids
and molars.

ROSCOE HERALD May 1, 1890
Hotel accommodations in this town have been wanting for the last week or two.
We are now afforded one hotel with the promise of a second one in the near
future.  Mrs. A. B. Morris this week opened the Roscoe House for public
patronage, and J. W. Jamieson proposes shortly to reopen the Edmunds House.

ROSCOE HERALD May 22, 1890
"Doc" for many years employed by the Jones Lumber Co. is dead.  "Doc" will be
remembered as the horse that, Alexander-like seemed born to rule.  His iron will
was unyielding, so he acted on the principle that he would have his own way or
die in the attempt.  He died.

ROSCOE HERALD June 5, 1890
J. W. Parmley has again taken up his residence in Roscoe--for two days at
least--he gets tired of the everlasting peacefulness of the county seat and gets
away every now and again into a live town!

                ROSCOE BUSINESS HISTORIES

ADAM'S ELECTRIC

Adam's Electric has been doing electrical work in commercial, farm, and
residential areas.  Over the first years, they also did complete carpentry and
masonry work.  Owners today are Mr. and Mrs. Gottfried H. Adam, Jr. and Mr. and
Mrs. Gary D. Adam and Heather.

The business started in 1946 working out of the home. In 1964, they built a 28
x 60 shop by the house.  At that time, they also went into the appliance
business.  The first major line they carried was the Westinghouse line. In
1977, they purchased the old Super Valu store on Main Street.  At that time the
building was completely remodeled on the inside and a new store front was
installed.  They took on the Whirlpool appliance line and RCA television line
at that time also.

BETTY'S BEAUTY SHOP

Mrs. Harold Peterson purchased Christine Taylor's beauty shop after working as
an apprentice under her for 18 months.

Siglinda Hieb Bieber bought the shop from Mrs. Peterson in 1945.  Mrs. Lawrence
Hettich bought the equipment from Mrs. Bieber and worked in the back of her
husband's barber shop next to the Post Office.

In 1952 Betty (Mrs.  Ralph) Preszler purchased the business from Lawrence
Hettich.  Betty rented the shop next to the Post Office after the Hettichs
moved from here.

Since 1958 Betty has had her shop in the basement of her farm home.  In 1976
Betty and her husband built on a new shop, office and double garage.  The new
shop is a different surrounding which Betty enjoys very much. She likes being
with people and hopes for many more enjoyable years.

BOBBY'S PLACE

        Taken in the Bar 25 years ago at the Diamond Jubilee. (LaVern and Helen
        Bobby)

LaVern and Helen Bobby purchased the "Community Bar" September 1, 1955 from
Ellwein and Kukrall.

They purchased the old Milwaukee Bar from Emil Bobby, Sr. and fixed it for
business.  January 8, 1956 they moved in and had two pool tables and 3.2 beer
plus lunches.  The place was named "Bobby's Place."

Many memories both good and bad are still with them as they spent nine and
one-half years among friends.

They sold the business to Mr. and Mrs. Al Zacher in July of 1964.

        Bobby's Place in April 1960.  Harry Himmerich and Harvey Aman ready to
        leave for the service.

CENEX FARMERS UNION OIL COMPANY

This company was started in 1946, with some people selling shares to form a
business.  The first manager was Art Himmerich,  who was manager for one year.
Ben Geffre was manager from 1947 to 1957.  Then Robert Garner became manager
from 1957 till the present time.

The sales went from$100,000 to over $1 million in the time it has been in
business with new commodities being added through the years.

In 1959 the old station burned, gutting the interior. They then moved the old
station and used it for a warehouse and built a new one.

In 1978 Jacob Hoffer was honored for serving 25 years as a board member.  The
following have been on the board of directors in order: Robert Schurr, Robert
Kilber, Perry Downer, Eugene Preszler, Jacob Hoffer, D. W. Reidt, George
Hammond, John Knittel, Martin Schurr, Dave Jeschke, Ernest Beyers, Melvin Roesch
Edward Heyne, Arnold Hettich, Joe Stockert, Verne Reidt, Morris Ernst, Math
Furhmann, Elroy Goetz, Melvin Beyers, Herbert Geist, Calvin Kilber and Joe
Goetz.  At the present time Verne Reidt is the president, Melvin Beyers is vice
president, Math Fuhrmann is secretary and Calvin Kilber and Joe Goetz are
directors.

The following have been employees of the company: H. E. Preszler, Marvin
Schmitt, John Heintzman, Solomon Reede, Jesse Barton, Edwin Schumacher, Milbert
Schumacher, Harold Peterson, Randy Huber, Edmund Schnabel, Betty Guthmiller,
Mervin Hoffer, Anton Heilman, Alvin Schmitt, Ervin Lammle, Inez Guthmiller,
Milbert Ellwein, Ervin Hieb, Marjorie Garner, Warren Schnaible, Peter Weron,
Orval Schneider, Allen Wiederich, Harley Mohr, Leland Vold, and Dale Miller.

A few years back the name was changed from Farmers Union Oil Company to Cenex.
The colors were also changed from green and yellow to red and gray.

The company is owned by stockholders.

DONNIE'S

Donnie had a dream and it started in 1979.  The town cafe had burned in a fire
that destroyed two city buildings in 1977.  The town was without an eating
establishment for nearly three years.  Donnie saw the need for the elderly ones
living alone to have a place where they could eat a hot nourishing meal.

In 1979 a 50 foot by 80 foot concrete block building with Mansard roof was
built at the intersection of Hiway 12 and Main Street of Roscoe.  The lounge
opened August 6, 1979 selling on and off sale liquor. August 10, 1979 the cafe
opened.

The coffee shop serves the working people of the day, plus serves as the social
scene for the coffee klatches. The lounge and dining room cater to the people
who enjoy a nice dinner in a quiet atmosphere.

Donnie strives to please the needs of the community, catering to wedding
receptions, serving anniversary dinners, baptismal celebrations, and the like.

The business has helped the employment situation in Roscoe and serves as a
community center, where friends meet after the local game or can leave personal
and business messages.

Donnie's dream has become a reality.

Donnie Roesch took Betty Stevens of Ipswich for his wife August 14, 1979.  They
reside in Roscoe.  They have six children between them, Doug Roesch, Roscoe; Pam
Roesch Keim, Volin, S.D.; Chuck Stevens, Clark, S.D.; Scott Stevens, Aberdeen,
S.D.; Donna Stevens Lewis, Riverton, Wyo. and Terry Stevens, Sheridan, Wyo.
They also have eight grandchildren.

EDDIE'S PLACE

        Marjorie and Eddie

In 1906 Theodore Muenz & Son, built and operated a saloon in what is now
Eddie's Place.  In 1910, Schmidt and Henning operated a grocery and clothing
store.  In 1918, Jacob Lehman operated a pool hall with Ed Tescher as
bartender.  William Bessie owned the pool hall in 1921.  Later a grocery store
opened by Christ Jacober.  Several years later Henry Lehr changed the business
to a movie picture house.

In 1929 the business was again converted to a pool hall operated by Bill Hill
and Ed Tiede.  Twice the building burned but was saved by the Fire Department.

In 1932 the business changed to Olson Drug Company. Ted Gross became the new
owner in 1941.  Ted's Place lasted until 1947.

At this time Elmer Mayer became the owner and operated Al's Place until 1956.

Milbert Ellwein bought the business in 1956.  Ellwein's Store changed hands in
1958.

It was a Halloween surprise when people walked in the Drug Store after 6 p.m.
and Eddie and Marjorie Dabbert were behind the counter October 31, 1958.

They themselves really weren't over the shock of their new adventure.

They have seen many changes price-wise.  In 1958, a bottle of 100 Anacin sold
for 59 cents.  Today it sells for $2.75.

Many young people have grown up with the Drug Store as their hang-out.
Saturday nights and Wednesday nights used to be the big thing.  All you had to
do was start the popcorn machine.  There would be times they'd be open until
two or three o'clock in the morning (which made for a long day) besides being
open six and a half days a week and in the evening.  Now they could almost
close Saturday afternoons.

There have been many interesting experiences that Eddie and Marjorie could tell
about.

THE ERLENBUSCH CREAM STATION

        Fred and Ruth Erlenbusch

The Erlenbusch Cream Station owned and operated by Mr. and Mrs. Fred Erlenbusch
was started by Mr. Erlenbusch June 1, 1943 in the building formerly known as
the George Goetz Cream Station.

Mr. Erlenbusch was born at Lowry, S.D. and raised in the Lowry, Java, Bowdle
and Tolstoy areas of South Dakota.  He was associated with the Farmer's Cream
Association in Tolstoy prior to coming to Roscoe.  He later drove the Roscoe
school bus in addition to operating the Cream Station.

Mrs. Erlenbusch assisted her husband in the Cream Station starting in 1945.
She was born and raised at Bowdle, S.D.

Mr. Erlenbusch passed away in March of 1964.  Mrs. Erlenbusch and the children
operated the Cream Station for several years after his death.  The building was
then sold and is now being used for storage.

Children born to Mr. and Mrs. Erlenbusch were Gary, who married Carol Wagner.
They have two children, Michelle and Chad and reside in Sioux Falls, S.D.

Linda married Gerald Rohrbach.  They and their two children, Carla and Cheryl
live in Blaine, Minn.

Glenn married Suzanne Potratz, who with their daughter Julie reside in New Ulm,
Minn.

Dan, unmarried, is at Rapid City, S.D.

Shari married Rodney Frankfurth.  The couple reside at Casper, Wyo.

Mrs. Ruth Erlenbusch sold the family home in August of 1981 and is now residing
in Sioux Falls, S.D.

FIRST STATE BANK OF ROSCOE

The First State Bank of Roscoe was organized May 23, 1903 and has served the
community continuously since that date.  The original stockholders and officers
were E. C. Hicks, president; J. E. Carroll, cashier; Fredrick B. Pease and
William C. Hicks, directors.

In August of 1904 the bank was purchased by Mahlon T. Lightner, Ward L.
McCafferty, J. W. Jamieson and W. T. Pratt.  In May of 1906 the stock was
purchased by R. W. Sherin and Cecil B. Sherin.

November of 1906 saw another transfer of ownership when E. Keller, H. A.
Warner, and Charles W. Christen purchased the stock from the Sherins.  E.
Keller was elected president, H. A. Warner, vice president and Charles W.
Christen, cashier.  This can be noted as the beginning of an era since the next
major change in ownership would not take place for nearly half a century.
William Christen joined the bank as assistant cashier in 1909 and served as an
officer and director until his death in 1978.  Charles W. Christen (better
known as Charlie) served as on officer and director until he moved to the Five
County Nursing Home in Lemmon in 1968.  H. J. "Hank" Schipke became associated
with the bank in 1912 and served as an officer and director until his death in
1951.  H. L. Morgeson joined the staff in 1948 and he served as an officer and
director until he retired as senior vice president at the end of 1982.

In July of 1954 another milestone was marked in the history of the First State
Bank of Roscoe when A. N. Johnson purchased controlling interest from Charles W.
and William Christen.  He served as president until 1964, at which time he was
elected chairman of the board.

        First State Bank, 1912.  Charles Christen in doorway, William Christen
        driver of fifth car from left.

In September of 1956, E. H. Johnson was employed as assistant cashier.  In 1964
he purchased control from A. N. Johnson and was elected president, a position he
has held since that time.

Catherine Meier was employed by the bank in 1956 and remained until 1980 when
she retired as assistant cashier.

John R. Beyers joined the bank in 1964 and is presently vice president and
cashier as well as being a director.

Terry Johnson was elected to the board of directors in 1969 and continues to
serve in that capacity.

Jeanne A. Schneider has been employed by the bank since December 1966 as
bookkeeper and teller.

Cindy Rohrbach joined the bank in February, 1977 as a bookkeeper and teller and
is now in computer operations.

Patricia Beck has been employed since August of 1980 as a bookkeeper and teller.

Some of the other employees who have worked in the bank over the years are
Leora (Heyne) Prisbe, Mary (Buechler) Bieber, Sharon (Strouckel) Guthmiller,
Rosella (Houck) Catlett, Viola (Lacher) Hettich, Dolores (Himmerich) Sahli,
Hugo Wiedenmayer, Lucille (Maisch) Schirmer, Ruby (Reede) Adam and Lorraine
(Scherr) Uhrich.

The First State Bank of Roscoe had it's beginning at the turn of the century
and has grown steadily with the community.  It has undergone many experiences
including two bank robberies, one burglary, droughts and abundant rains, good
times and bad, and will celebrate its 80th birthday in 1983.  It has witnessed
technological changes that saw all of the records being kept by hand at the
start and now the record keeping is entirely computerized.  The officers,
directors, and employees congratulate Roscoe on its 100th birthday and pledge
their continued support and cooperation to help both the bank and the community
continue progress in the years to come.

        First State Bank, 1944.

J. FISCHER SALES

J. Fischer Sales is an implement dealership which was begun by Jerome and Beth
Fischer in September, 1976. At this time, the business was run from their home
with the machinery lot being west of the old Lumber Yard building.

In 1977, Jerome Fischer purchased the building and lots used by Schneider
Trucking from Rena Schneider. This property is located on South Main Street.

The building was completely remodeled.  It now houses two offices; one used by
Jerome and the other by Fischer Trucking, a steam room, point room, storage
room, and a large shop area.

The business is primarily used for sales of new and used farm implements.  Feed
is also handled.  The assembling of inside grain drill disk scrapers is done
during the winter months.  These ore distributed to dealers in a four-state
area, Montana, Minnesota, North and South Dakota.

FISCHER'S SUPER VALU

Scherf's Super Valu was started by the Scherf brothers in 1925.  They carried a
full line of groceries and meat processing.

Because of health problems, Arthur Scherf was forced to sell his place of
business in October, 1968.  Jerome and Beth Fischer purchased the inventory and
fixtures from Art and operated the store as Fischer's Super Valu.  December 10,
1969, after only 14 months of business, fire destroyed the business.  A fire
sale was held, selling everything at half price.  The store was never reopened.
The building was purchased from Art Scherf by Gottfried Adam and is now Adam's
Electric.

GUTHMILLER BLACKSMITH

        Fred. G. Guthmiller, Tom Reidt, unidentified, and Daniel Junkert

This picture, taken in 1926 or 1927, shows the owner, Fred G. Guthmiller,
holding the welding torch.  The wheels are wooden wagon wheels with steel rims.
When the steel rims were in need of repair, they were put in the forge,
repaired, dipped in water, and then replaced on the wooden wheel.

The old shop was not only a center of hard work, but a place for young and old
to visit and to watch the fire in the forge and the flying sparks from the
welding torch.

HAAR'S RECREATION

From May to October 1969, Mr. and Mrs. Robert Hieb completed and opened a
business known as Hieb Recreation.

Haar's Recreation, a 3.2 bar, was purchased October 1, 1971 by Dale and Gloria
Haar.

The business was destroyed by fire December 21, 1976.  It was rebuilt and
opened for business June 24, 1977.

HEIER'S CARPENTER SERVICE

Heier's Carpenter Service originated in December 1980 when Bernard D. Heier
decided he would enjoy working independently.

Major projects completed during his first year were renovation of the South
Dakota Wheat Growers off ices, remodeling Arnie Wiedrich's home in Hosmer and
building the addition on Marvin Uhrich's house in Roscoe.

ARNOLD HETTICH TRUCKING

Arnold Hettich was born near Roscoe, S.D. May 18, 1920.  After going to grade
school, he attended the Mobridge Academy for one year.  Because of the hard
times, his parents could not afford to send him there any longer because it
cost $110 a year at the dormitory. He then returned and worked on a farm south
of Roscoe for room and board so he could attend school.  His senior year he
worked at Noehres Cafe in Roscoe, and he graduated in 1940.  Arnold worked with
his parents on the farm until 1944.  He then started working for George Hammond
which lasted two and one-half years.

In July, 1946, Arnold married Verna Scheid.  Also, that some year, he purchased
the trucking business and his home from George Hammond.

In those days most roads were made of gravel so it took longer to travel.
Then, it took 12 hours to go to Sioux City which now only takes seven hours,
and the load is much larger now.  In the 1950s, Arnold hauled over 1,000 head
of horses a year to Esterville, Iowa; Jamestown, North Dakota; Medford,
Wisconsin; and Buyrus, Ohio.  Most of the horses were hauled for Martin Scherf.
Also, in 1955, Arnold took a load of rabbit skins for Mardian Hide & Fur out
to the docks in New York City and saw them loaded on a ship bound for Germany.
That was quite an experience.  Now his main commodity is livestock.  He also
does some grain hauling.

Arnold has won several safety awards from Sioux Falls and Sioux City Stockyards
for hauling the most livestock with no losses.  He has run his business on the
idea of always being on time.  People have learned to set their clocks by
Arnold Hettich.  Arnold Hettich Trucking is one of the oldest businesses in
Roscoe with the original owner still in operation.

Besides his businesses, Arnold also found time to serve as county commissioner
from 1965-1977.  He also served on the board of directors for Farmers' Union for
nine years.

DALE HETTICK CONSTRUCTION

Dale Hettick Construction saw its beginnings in the spring of 1979.  Owner Dale
Hettick states that the business consists of general construction such as new
construction, remodeling, and cabinet work.  The cabinet work is done in a shop
located on the Earl Hettick residence at the west edge of Roscoe.  At the
present time, the business employs one person.

DENNIS HETTICH CONSTRUCTION

In February 1973, Dennis Hettich went into carpentry on his own in Hoven, S.D.
After three years, he moved to Roscoe and has done carpentry for the post six
years.

Larger projects completed were the Hoven Country Club, Ervin Ellwein's house in
Ipswich, interior work on the Spitzer Implement building and the building of his
own home in 1981.  He's done many shingling and remodeling jobs and also some
pole barns.

HIEB IMPLEMENT

First of all I want to give a little history of the brothers Herbert and Erwin
Hieb.  Herbert was born in 1920 in McPherson County 20 miles northeast of
Roscoe on the John Spitzer farm (grandfather of Ervin Spitzer).  Erwin was born
in 1922 in McPherson County 17 miles northeast of Roscoe, on the Grandfather
Jacob Hieb farm. Both of us grew up on the farm.  We both quit farming in 1957
and in February of 1959 we joined together in a partnership and opened Hieb
Repair Shop March 24, 1959.  The repair shop was started in the old Parkins
Grocery Store building which was purchased from Martin Bashans.  We repaired
all makes of cars, trucks, and tractors.

In November 1961 the Allis Chalmers farm equipment contract was accepted.  In
June 1963 the Dual Loader franchise was acquired.

In 1966 additional lots were purchased from the Wilson Lumber Co. These lots
are to the north of the Hieb Implement.  In 1967, two of the lots were sold to
the Bell Telephone Company, so they could build the new building they now have.

During the summer and fall of 1972, a 40 by 48 foot shop area was added to the
north side of the old building.  In 1974 tragedy struck Hieb Repair Shop when
we learned with great sorrow that my brother had cancer and passed away in
March 1975.  It was a terrible loss to Hieb Repair Shop and the community as we
lost an excellent mechanic and salesman.  I purchased Herbert's shore of the
business from his wife Irene in December 1975.  The name then was changed from
Hieb Repair Shop to Hieb Implement.

In 1976 the Morris Rod Weeder (manufacturers of farm tillage tools) franchise
was added to Hieb Implement.  Also the some year Teem gravity flow grain boxes
were obtained.  During the severe winter of 1977 Lundell and Loftness
snowblowers contracts were added to the dealership.  A new roof was installed on
the old building in 1979.  Plans at this writing are to have the south wall of
the old building completely repaired by the Centennial celebration.

Elroy Scherbenski (our mechanic) has been with us for 11 years.

All of us at Hieb Implement want to congratulate the city of Roscoe on its
100th birthday and we also want to thank everybody in the Roscoe community for
their patronage and support over the years.  I hope we are worthy of your
continued support in the years to come. Thank you all.
                Erwin and Alice Hieb
                Elroy Scherbenski

A. N. JOHNSON AGENCY

The A. N. Johnson Agency was formed in 1956 by A. N. and Olive Johnson and
Emmett and Terry Johnson. The agency handled all types of insurance and was
managed by H. L. Margeson until his retirement at the end of 1982.

In June of 1982, Emmett and Terry Johnson purchased the insurance business from
Leland and Tracy Treichel and merged it with the A. N. Johnson Agency.  Leland
Treichel was employed to act as solicitor and assistant manager of the agency.

Later in 1982, the business was moved to the recently remodeled building just
north of the bank that was formerly occupied as either a barber shop or beauty
shop.  The agency represents a number of strong and competitive companies and
looks forward to growing with Roscoe during the second "100".

JO ANN'S APPAREL

        Moving Day 1960

Twenty-six years ago Joe and Ann Prisbe purchased the inventory and fixtures
from Mr. and Mrs. Fred Guthmiller who owned and operated the Variety Store for
several years.  The store then was located across the street, which is now the
Roscoe Senior Center.  That building was constructed in 1949 by Emanuel Hofer
and sons, and was a small cafe and recreation spot operated by the Hofer family.

About a year later it was said to Mrs. Katherine Haupt, who took over the cafe
business until 1952 when the building was sold to Martin and Arthur Scherf who
owned it up to the time the Senior Center opened.  This building was rented
from Scherfs until 1960 when the new building was constructed across the street
on the corner where the Noehre Cafe once stood. Needing much more space since
more lines of wearing apparel and dry goods were being added, moving day
finally came.  On the hot, windy Labor Day weekend, with the help of friends
and relatives, across the street they went carrying clothes on hangers, hats,
notions, shoes, etc.

It almost seemed as though they had acquired much more space than needed, at
first, but it didn't take long to find out shortage of space again is the
problem.

It is interesting in looking back over the post 26 years of the many, many
changes, not only style or pricewise, but the many valued customers they had
from the start.  The parents that brought in their little children are now.
adults themselves with another generation growing up.  It's this kind of
loyalty and spirit that keeps the community strong.

MEIER'S CLOCK SHOP

Meier's Clock Shop is owned and operated by Roy Meier.  In April of 1982, Roy,
Erma and Jennifer left the farm and started a woodworking shop.

Roy was tinkering with wood since the winter of 1977 and as time went on he got
more interested in wood crafts.  Finally he converted his hog barn into a wood
shop.  In 1982 he decided to build a house and shop all under one roof in
Roscoe.

Today Roy is busy building grandfather clocks, various other kinds of clocks,
china hutch, kitchen cabinets and many other things.

NORTHWESTERN BELL

There may be a few Roscoe oldtimers who can still remember the first phones in
the community.  These were magneto telephones that had to be cranked to get the
operator, who rang the party requested.

Records aren't specific about the date these first telephones came to Roscoe.
One telephone company report notes that the first long distance line to and from
the community was established about 1898 and connected Roscoe to Aberdeen.
Participating in this very early venture was W. G. Bickelhaupt, who later became
president of the Dakota Central Telephone Company. Bickelhaupt made his home at
Roscoe for several years. He and J. W. Zietlow organized the Western Dakota
Telephone Company and extended long distance telephone lines from Aberdeen to
Roscoe, Ipswich, Leola, Bowdle and Eureka.

The Dakota Central Telephone Company was operating a local telephone exchange
here in 1915, perhaps earlier.  That company's newsletter reported in 1918 the
resignation of Marion E. Wilson who had served as Dakota Central operator and
manager in Roscoe since 1915.  Another news bulletin reported that there were
36 telephones in Roscoe as of December 20, 1916.  In comparison, there were 318
main telephone lines served by the Roscoe office as of August 31, 1982.

The Dakota Central Telephone Company became a direct subsidiary of the
Northwestern Company (Bell) on December 7, 1936, and was operated as such until
the close of business June 30, 1942, when it was merged with the Northwestern
Company.

The first reported major telephone service improvement in Roscoe was completed
July 2, 1940.  A new "community dial" telephone system was placed in operation.
This new equipment enabled Roscoe phone users to by-pass the operator when
making local calls. All they needed to do was dial the local telephone number.

September 16, 1977 Roscoe telephone numbers were changed to seven digits with a
"287" prefix.  Also on that date, Direct Distance Dialing became available
here.  This new service enabled phone users to dial their own
station-to-station (1+) long distance calls to over 90 million phones
throughout the continental United States and Canada.

Also in 1977, a $198,000 rural service improvement project was completed.  Some
131 area rural customers were provided upgraded storm proof telephone service;
all eight-party telephone lines were eliminated.

Last year Roscoe formally entered the age of electronic telephone switching.
August 26, 1981, Northwestern Bell technicians placed into service a remote
switching system (RSS) and took out of service the electro-mechanical office
that had switched phone calls here since 1977.  The new Roscoe office is
connected to an electronic switching system (ESS) in Aberdeen.  By using that
system's call-processing power, a number of calling features became available,
including Zero Plus Calling and International Direct Distance Dialing.  With
Zero Plus Calling, Roscoe telephone users are able to dial their own
person-to-person, collect and credit cord calls with minimum operator
assistance.  International Direct Distance Dialing enables Roscoe phone users to
direct dial long distance calls to 82 different countries.

Four other telephone features, called Custom Calling services, were introduced
in Roscoe last year.  They are Call Forwarding, Call Waiting, Three-Way Coiling
and Speed Calling.  These features are optional and can be purchased for a
monthly charge.

Finally, late last year on optional one-party residence service plan, called
Conserver's Service, was introduced here.  The monthly charge for this service
is less than for the traditional one-party service plan and includes 40 local
calls each month; additional charges apply for local calls over 40.

In June 1982 a new service that allows callers from Touch-Tone phones to
complete their own credit cord calls without operator assistance was introduced
here. With this service, called Calling Card Service, the phone user simply
inputs his Calling Card (credit card) number after "O" plus the digits of the
distant telephone number are pressed.

PHILLIPS 66

When Mr. Ladner from Eureka, S.D. purchased this station, there was only a
small building and two old gas pumps.  After buying several lots, Mr. Ladner
completely remodeled the service station in October, 1940, and put in two new
gas pumps.  Then Mr. Ladner had the two Diede boys from North Dakota run the
service station for a couple of years.  Later on Mr. Ladner moved a house to
the west of the service station which was later occupied by Emil Maisch.  In
1942, the bulk plant was built, and another house was moved to the west of the
first house which was occupied by Harold Klifel. Emil Maisch ran the service
station and Harold Klipfel ran the bulk plant until 1946.  In October, 1946,
Fred Beck purchased the service station and the bulk plant from Mr. Ladner and
took possession in December 1, 1946.

On January 1, 1966, Fred retired and sold "Beck Oil Company" to his son Reuben
who had been employed there since December 1, 1946.  January 1, 1971, the
service station business was discontinued.  He continued to operate the bulk
business until July 1, 1977 when he decided to devote his full time to his
farming and ranching operation.

EUGENE PIETZ TRUCKING

        Darrell and Eugene Pietz

In 1957 Eugene Pietz purchased Phillip Lemmle's trucking business which
included a 1951 Ford twelve foot grain and livestock box.

Today Mr. Pietz and his son Darrell run the grain and livestock hauling
business.  They are running a freight liner tractor with a cattle trailer, on
International tractor with a forty foot cattle trailer, a forty foot grain
trailer and a two ton tandem Chevrolet with a 26 foot box.

ROSCOE POST OFFICE

        Randy, Huber, Ervin Bader, Bruce Sahli, Jerome Prisbe and Earl Hettick.

The oldest business going in the City of Roscoe is the Post Office which had
its beginning in a tent in the summer of 1883 at a time when the people began to
gather here at this point before a town was organized and many buildings were
put up.  The office was started by Charles Morgan who was a relative of J. P.
Morgan. After him Lorenzo G. Sims was appointed postmaster and served from 1884
to 1885.  Zack T. Sutley served as postmaster from 1885 to 1886.  Wilber F.
Drury was appointed postmaster and served from 1886 to 1889. After him William
S. Campbell was appointed postmaster and served from 1889 to 1892.  His wife
Sarah served from 1892 to 1895.  Almer L. Jacobs served as postmaster from 1895
to 1897.  In 1897 Henry Huck was appointed and served until his death in 1899.
His son Wolter E. Huck was then appointed and served until 1905.  His office
was located in the building formerly known as the Zander Butcher Shop.  Milton
Huck served about one year as postmaster under his brother Walter's term and
again when he was appointed from 1905 to 1914.  He had moved his office to his
store known as the Huck building.

        Frank Brenton and Charles Decker, 1934.

Ward L. McCafferty was appointed January 29, 1914. He moved the office to the
present location and served until December 8, 1919 when Howard M. Price was
appointed and served until June 10, 1921.  Next Charles P. Decker was appointed
and served until June 4, 1935. Bernard Mayer was the first Postmaster to be
appointed through Civil Service.  He was appointed June 7, 1935 and took off
ice July 3, 1935.  Elmer R. Mayer was appointed acting postmaster October 11,
1963 and served until September 1966.  Jerome D. Prisbe is the present
postmaster and was the last one to receive a presidential appointment receiving
his commission September 9, 1966.

During Howard M. Price's term the first rural route was started, known as Route
No. 1 and served by Neal Hammond for about a year.  Then Merrith Price served
the route until an examination was held whereupon Leslie P. Oehler was
appointed as rural carrier.  Two more rural routes, No. 2 and No. 3 were now
added to the service.  Leslie P. Oehler transferred to Route No. I and James N.
Brenton was appointment to Route No. 3.

Numerous switches and changes took place until the three routes were
consolidated into two.  Paul E. Elford was appointed to Route No. 1 in 1930.
Walter Lammle was appointed April 5, 1954 to Route No. 2 and served as rural
carrier until he exchanged routes with Mr. Leonard M. Tripp of Peru, Neb. in
1965.  Ervin Bader was appointed rural carrier in 1963 after the retirement of
Mr. Elford and is the present carrier on Route No. 1. Mr. Tripp retired as
rural carrier in 1974 and Marvin Pfitzer, carrier at Java, S.D. was transferred
to Roscoe to fill the position.  He served as carrier on Route No. 2 from
January 1975 to 1978 until he returned to the same position at Java.

The present Rural Route 2 was changed to a contract route (open bidding by a
private contractor) July 1, 1979.  Harold Sahli is the present contractor and
the route is presently served by Bruce Sahli.

June 12, 1977 saw a change in rural delivery as Roscoe extended its delivery
boundaries when the Loyalton Post Office was closed and the route was combined
with the two Roscoe routes extending each route by 40 miles.

Present clerks serving the Roscoe Post Office are Joseph Prisbe and Earl
Hettick and present substitute rural carrier is Randolph Huber.

During the war years 1941-1945 the post office was the center of activity with
mail arriving four to five times a day, with Reinhold Kilber serving as Railway
Clerk who carried mail from the Railroad Depot to the Post Office.

The Post Office interior was remodeled in 1962 with the lockboxes and working
areas being rearranged to provide easier access to the lockboxes and more
efficient modern work areas.

RANDY'S BUILDING SUPPLIES

        Erna and Randy Huber

This place of business which was built for a Standard Service Station, but was
never used as such, was purchased in 1969 from Carl O. Swanson.

A year later he bought the (Heyne) property joining this place, built a lumber
storage shed, and bought a cement shed, from what used to be the Wilson
Lumberyard and moved it here.

It has been a very successful business for the post 13 years.

ROHRBACH BROTHERS DIGGING AND WELL-REPAIR

In 1919, Jacob Rohrbach began his operation of well-drilling and repair.  He
carried on this profession for 40 years.  In 1959, his two sons, Herbert and
Jerald, took over the business.  The business has undergone some definite
changes.  In 1960, the well-drilling rig was remodeled.  During 1963, the
Rohrbach Brothers began expanding their business by including excavating.  The
business continued in this way until 1975 when they quit well-drilling.

Their business now included digging and well-repair. In 1979, the brothers
decided to purchase a new digger which increased working capability.  As of
today, the Rohrbach Brothers are still busy digging and repairing wells.

        Jerold and Herbert Rohrbach
