        Rohrbach Brothers digging and well-repair.

ROSCOE AUTO COMPANY

Roscoe Auto Company, owned by Roy and LeRoy Bieber, was originally started in
1946 under the name of Bieber's Service.  This business was a Texaco franchise
and was operated in the location of what is now Randy's Building Supplies.

In 1952, Roscoe Auto Company was formed when Martin Scherf joined the
partnership.  At this time, they became a Mobil distributor and acquired a
Dodge-Plymouth new-car dealership.  Also, a new building was constructed on
lots 13, 14, and 15 in Block 14.  The original building had two service stalls;
then, in 1957, on additional two stalls were added to the east side of the
building.

In 1965, Martin left the business and Roy and Roy continued the operation.
They added the Chrysler franchise to their new-car line in 1976 and purchased
lots 9, 10, 11, and 12 in Block 3, (formerly the Philip Lammle residence), in
order to provide parking space for their new cars.  In 1978, they acquired
additional parts inventory from the Kromer-Corbin Chrysler dealership of
Mobridge and a year later built an addition onto the northwest corner of the
original building to accommodate the expanded inventory.

At present, they are selling a full line of automobiles, including Chrysler -
Dodge - Plymouth - Imports.  They also carry a full line of Mobil products and
do service and repair work.  Present employees of Roscoe Auto Company are Dean
Lehr, Leland Stern, and Marilyn Bieber.

On October 1, 1982, Ray bought his brother, Roy's share of the business and
became the sole owner of the Roscoe Auto Co.

ROSCOE HARDWARE

The hardware line was started in Roscoe in 1884 when L. T. Sims built a
hardware and merchandise store.  Mr. Sims sold the business to C. E. Kittinger
in 1899, who in turn sold it to A. S. Vaughn in 1902.  Mr. Vaughn sold the
store to the Hartelt Brothers in 1909.

The building burned down in 1913 and was rebuilt by Otto Wudel.  Mr. Wudel
operated the store until December 1, 1923 when it was purchased by August and
Martin Adam and operated under the name of Adam Brothers' Hardware.

In 1945, the Adam Brothers sold the hardware business and building to Mr. and
Mrs. Ruben Kirschenmann who, at that time, farmed 11 miles south of Roscoe.
The new owners called their business venture Roscoe Hardware which it is called
to this date.

The Kirschenmanns proceeded in improving both their building and business
almost immediately after taking it over.  A new brick front and thermopane
glass were put on in 1949 and the building was enlarged to a width of 42 feet
and a length of 90 feet.  They added a plumbing and heating service and did
extensive interior remodeling.  A television and service department were added
during the last years that the Kirschenmanns operated the store.

In August of 1969, the store was purchased from Kirschenmanns by Earl and
Bernice Hettick who are the present owners.  The Hetticks have added a second
glass door to fight the cold and a permanent steel awning to shade the building
from the sun.  A new heating and air conditioning system have been installed.
The store has taken on a new look inside during the past couple years with a
suspended ceiling in 1980 and new floor and wall covering, new shelving and a
rearrangement in 1882.  In 1978, the Hettick's son, Donald, joined the business
as a full-time electrician and the store now offers a full line of electrical,
plumbing and heating service to the community.  The business also carries
sporting goods, hardware supplies, housewares, large and small appliances and
gift items.

ROSCOE-HOSMER INDEPENDENT

The Roscoe-Hosmer Independent traces back to the Edmunds County Weekly News
founded by Joseph W. Parmley and Henry Huck in 1883.  That paper became the
Roscoe Herald which in 1910 Parmley merged into the Ipswich Tribune.

        Fran, Jim, Mike and Mark Morlock.

Frank J. Tracy purchased the Ipswich Tribune in 1927 from Walter L. Johnson, he
also published the Ipswich Independent.  He retained the Tribune in the county
seat and moved the independent to Roscoe.

H. J. and Merrill B. Bebermeyer purchased the Independent in 1932.  W. L. Combs
became publisher in 1965 and sold the paper to James A. and Frances Morlock in
1976.  The name of the paper was changed from the Roscoe Independent to
Roscoe-Hosmer Independent in April 1977 to include a broader coverage area.

ROSCOE REAL ESTATE & INSURANCE

Roscoe Real Estate and Insurance was opened January 1, 1958 by Harold Ellwein
and Elmer R. Mayer.  March 22, 1958 was their opening day.

Leland and Tracy Treichel purchased the business January 1, 1976.  They kept
the business in the old hotel until September of 1977.  It was moved to the
present location which is one and one-half blocks east of the school.

Leland became a licensed insurance agent in January 1976, a licensed appraiser
in December 1978, and licensed real estate broker in July of 1980.

Tracy became a licensed insurance agent in February 1981 and has completed
insurance schooling with Milbank Mutual and Aid Insurance Services.  She is also
a certified Emergency Medical Technician.

June 1, 1982 Roscoe Real Estate and Insurance merged with the A. N. Johnson
Agency.  All insurance will be conducted under that name.  Real estate and
income tax preparation will continue at the former Roscoe Real Estate and
Insurance building.

ROSCOE SENIOR HOUSING APARTMENTS

Roscoe Senior Housing, a non-profit organization, was organized in 1973 with a
membership of 32, each contributing $50.

In the year 1974, the first four-plex one-bedroom apartment building was
constructed with an FHA loan for $48,500 with a 50 year repayment schedule.

The location of the apartment is two blocks east of Main Street on Broadway.
Immediately the apartments were rented with a demand for a second unit.  In
November of 1975, plans for a second unit were mode with an application to FHA
for a loan in the amount of $50,500.00. Construction was started and the
building completed in 1976.  The two identical units are located on the some
lot.  Immediately after completion, the second unit was occupied, with full
occupancy since the units were finished and a waiting list.

A third unit has been planned for the future and a lot has been purchased.

The officers have been the some since 1974: Robert Garner, director; Dale
Roesch, director; Rueben Beck, president; Harold Margeson, vice president;
Berneice Margeson, secretary-treasurer.  Committee members are Gary Adam,
Gottfried Adam, Henry Trefz, Lydia Trefz, Helen Downer and Randy Huber.

ROSCOE SCHOOL BUS SHED

The Roscoe School bus shed has had a varied past.  In 1908 an old livery barn
housed the original machine store owned by Peter Malsom.

In 1921 Mr. Malsom traded his store to the Wage Brothers for land in North
Dakota.

William Lammle purchased the property from the Wage Brothers in March 1922.
His machinery business thrived for 32 years.

In 1923 the livery barn was replaced by a larger building.  A modern shop was
added in 1946.  January 6, 1947 the building and its contents were destroyed by
fire.

Later that year, Mr. Lammle rebuilt his store and his son, Walter, went into
business with him.  Lammle & Son sold their business in 1954 to the B & E
Company, a partnership between Erwin Lammle and Robert Auchampach.

Bob's Farm Store was the name of Mr. Auchampach's new business with an IHC
franchise after he bought out Erwin Lammle in 1954.  Several years later he
sold out.

Henry Trefz purchased the empty building from William Lammle in 1967.  Trefz
Standard, a repair shop, operated until December of 1975 when he sold it to
Jerome Malsom.

Doc's Repair Shop, owned by Jerome Malsom, operated about two years until he
sold the building to the Roscoe Public School in 1977.  The building is
presently used to house the school buses.

SAHLI'S RED OWL

        Julia and Kap Sahli, Customer Harold Bauer, 1944

This business had it's origin in 1904, when Milton Huck finished the last year
of his brother Walter's term as Postmaster and started a small grocery, candy
and postcard business at the some time in a building on the site where Adam's
Electric is now located.  The building was sold to August Zander who started a
butcher shop in it.  The building burned down while the Zander Butcher Shop was
in it.  Mr. Huck had moved his grocery business to what was known as the Huck
building located north of the railroad tracks.  The building is now owned by
Ralph Preszler who uses it for storage purposes.  The Post Office was also
located in this building as Milton Huck was still serving as postmaster at that
time.  About 1917, he sold out to Ben Redman, who bought the I. Parkins grocery
business early in 1936 and moved his business to the Parkins building, which
now houses the Hieb Repair.

Kasper Sahli bought the grocery and dry goods business in 1939 and changed it
to a Red and White Store.  It became a "family" store with his wife, Julia, and
all the children having a hand in the running of it.

The building was sold in 1945 and a new location for the business was needed.
Sahli's bought the Finder building and tore it down and also the John Fetzer
Cream Station, which was moved to the John Schlichenmeyer farm.  A new building
was constructed on this site and the move to the new location was made in
February 1946.

In 1954, their son Harold, became a partner in the business.  In September
1956, they joined the Red Owl Franchise and it became Sahli's Red Owl.  At this
time the dry goods department was sold out and the store was completely
remodeled.  Harold bought his father's remaining share in 1961 and became the
sole owner.

In 1972 extensive remodeling was again done.  The north wall was removed which
opened into the adjoining cream station.  This had last been managed by Alton
Schnabel.  It was a part of the Sahli building and provided the much needed
additional space.  The store received a new "brick" front in 1976.

It is still primarily a family store run by Harold and Dolores Sahli with
part-time help from Bruce, Cindy and Robin Sahli.  Esther Kilber has been
employed there since 1968 and George Rohrbach has worked for the Sahli's for
eight years.

CALVIN S. SCHAD, D.O.

September 14, 1942 Dr. C. S. Schad, a 1940 graduate of Kirksville College of
Osteopathic Medicine, Kirksville, Mo., opened his office in the John Kelsch
house for the practice of general medicine.  He later moved to the Wiedenmeyer
building on Main St. and finally to the Trantow house in north Roscoe.  He
officiated at many home deliveries, ushering new citizens into the community.
With the closest hospital 40 miles away he was often called on to provide
emergency care, sometimes performing "kitchen surgery" and often ambulance
service to Aberdeen in emergencies, in addition to his usual duties as a
general practitioner.

Dr. Schad was also civic minded, being active on the Board of Education, and in
the Chamber of Commerce, where he served as president of both groups. in
addition, he served several terms as County Coroner. During WWII, the school
did not have a coach so Dr. Schad volunteered his services and coached high
school basketball for two years.  He also organized and supervised grade
school, intramural high school, and city basketball leagues.  Adding high
school A and B teams and an independent team to the program, Roscoe boosted 15
teams in its basketball program.  He also formed and sponsored a winning amateur
basketball team made up of former Roscoe high school stars, known as "Doc's
Canaries." This team won 16 of 19 games in their first year.

        Dr. Schad in consultation with patient, Mrs. Jake Mayer.

As president of the Board of Education, he pioneered school reorganization by
successfully encouraging the closing of three rural schools, the construction
of a new elementary school, and busing all students to a central educational
system.

During his term as Chamber of Commerce president, he spearheaded a drive to put
the issue of a municipal water-sewer system to a vote of the people.  The issue
carried and the City Council saw the project through to a successful
completion, making Roscoe a "modern" city.  Continuing his interest in
basketball, which he played, coached and officiated, he was one of the
organizers of the South Dakota Amateur Basketball Association.  He served as a
member of its' first board of directors and later as president of the
organization.  He was elected into the South Dakota Amateur Basketball Hall of
Fame in 1973.  He served as a scout leader and arranged for Roscoe area boys
under 16 to join the Aberdeen Pheasants Knot Hole Gang, which admitted them to
games free and allowed them to sit in a special "Knot Hole" section of the ball
park.  He also played on the local amateur baseball team.

In 1956, Dr. and Mrs. Schad and their three children, Jacqueline, Roxanne and
Mark moved to Rapid City, S.D. where daughter Patricia, was born several months
later.

Dr. Schad is still in active practice and also provides other services to his
community today.

        Dr. Schad treating a patient.

SCHERF'S SUPER VALU

On August 13, 1925, Arthur Scherf, in partnership with his brother Martin,
opened up their grocery business under the name of the Sanitary Meat Market.
The brothers purchased their first store building from the late August Zander.
Previously this building housed the Zander Meat Market, the Grandahl Meat
Market, and the Carlson and Allgier Market.  About one year later, they entered
the IGA program in which they participated for the following 16 years.

        Bill, Wudel, Art Scherf and Fr.  Smoley

On January 14, 1930, their store was gutted by fire, and it was only by prompt
action of the Roscoe Fire Department in 20 degrees below zero weather that the
building was saved.  The gutted building was bought by Johnny Henrich who moved
it near the old fair grounds and used it as a barn.  Later it was purchased by
Gust Winkler who moved it three miles east of town.

After the fire, the stock and fixtures that remained were moved into the
building which in later years housed the Schnabel Cafe.  After they received the
insurance adjustment, the grocery stock was given to the Scherf Brothers to
dispose of in any practical manner.  A fire sale followed with no advance
advertising at which no one offered to purchase any items until Dr. Aldrich
arrived at the scene and started to buy. In less than one afternoon, the
complete stock of groceries was sold at approximately 50 percent discount.
Customers practically fought to make their purchases of canned goods.  They had
to guard their selections until the Scherfs were able to check their purchases,
or else other eager bargain hunters would grab them.

Three days later or five days after the fire, the Scherfs, after having
completely remodeled and painted, opened up again for business in the Schnabel
Cafe building with a complete new stock.

Plans for a new store building were under way immediately.  Baumeister and Son
of St. Paul, builders of the Lutheran Church, were contracted to build their
building.  May 31, 1930 the Scherfs moved into their new building.  During the
28 years that followed, in order to keep modern, the Scherfs completely rebuilt
and remodeled their store eight different times.  Each time new equipment and
the most modern type of shelving were used with the latest addition being air
conditioning in 1958.

In 1942, the Scherfs joined the Super Valu organization of which they remained
members until Art's selling of the business.  Arthur Scherf received many
honors and awards from Super Valu, one of which was being placed on the
Advisory Board of Super Valu Stores, Inc.

In January, 1953, the Scherfs dissolved partnership, leaving Arthur Scherf as
owner and operator of Scherf's Super Valu.  In September, 1968, Arthur Scherf
sold his business to Jerome Fischer because of poor health.

SCHMITT OIL COMPANY

        Alvin Schmitt

Standard Oil Company started doing business in Roscoe in the early 1920s.  Mr.
Lehman was the first agent.  August 26, 1924 August Schumacher became the
agent.  He retired March 14, 1960.

Alvin E. Schmitt, the present owner, started as agent March 14, 1960.  January
30, 1976 Standard changed to the Jobber system and Mr. Schmitt became their
Jobber.

Mr. Schmitt started with an 800 gallon truck.  In 1968 he changed to a 1500
gallon truck.  He is presently using the some size truck.

Many changes have come about in the 22 years he has been in this business.
When he started March 14, 1960 stove fuel was 16.9 cents per gallon.  March 14,
1982 it was $1.12 per gallon.  Mr. Schmitt believes the petroleum business will
probably change even more in the next 20 years than it did in the last 20.

Schmitt Oil Company would like to take this means to wish the Roscoe Community
the very best on their one- hundred anniversary.  "Thank you for letting me
serve you.

SCHNEIDER'S HOTEL

Schneider's Hotel, one of Roscoe's familiar landmarks, was known as the "West
Hotel" in the early days.  Built in 1897 by C. E. Kittenger the upstairs was
used as an opera house.  The first floor was a bank and doctor's off ices.
Later the new owner, Theo.  Muenz, remodeled the upstairs changing it into a
hotel.

September 9, 1922 Helen Muenz, who married a man named Cozad, purchased the
hotel.  They ran the hotel awhile.  Later they rented it to Mrs. Wudel.

Philip and Rena Schneider purchased the hotel March 1, 1945.  The lobby was
remodeled and redecorated in 1955.  Mrs. Wudel managed the hotel until 1957.
Mrs. Schneider took over the operation of it and served as the agent of the
Roscoe bus depot.

About 1976 Rena Schneider decided to close the hotel at which time she moved
into her newly remodeled home.  From her home she took care of the Jack Rabbit
bus line depot until her death in March 1982.

SCHUMACHER TV AND ELECTRIC

        Alvin Schumacher

Schumacher TV and Electric is a television, radio  and small appliance sales
and repair business owned     and operated by Alvin Schumacher.

Mr. Schumacher graduated in 1954 from the Philco Radio and Television School
and in 1957 from the Emerson Television School.

In 1958, Mr. Schumacher started his television and radio business in the
basement of his home.  In 1961 he remodeled the home two blocks north of the
bank on Main Street.  Parts and equipment were moved to the basement.

        Alvin Schumacher

Graduation from the Sylvania Color Television School came in 1967.

In addition to the business in the basement of his home, Mr. Schumacher uses a
Ford van to service televisions in the home.

SOUTH DAKOTA WHEAT GROWERS

Some farmers of this area formed the Roscoe Farmers Elevator Company about
1919.  They bought the house the furthest to the West in Roscoe.  Various
managers from 1928-1942 were E. J. Heiser, Henry Huber, and Ed Bernet. in the
fall of 1942, John Himmerich became the manager.

In 1943 the first large truck scale in the area was installed.  John Himmerich
and a silent partner bought the elevator in 1944, and renamed it The West
Elevator Company.  In 1945 the elevator was completely rebuilt. In 1946 Arthur
Himmerich bought out the silent partner. In 1948 Arthur Himmerich sold his
share to John Himmerich who became the sole owner of the West Elevator Company.

There was another elevator closer to Main Street, known as the old Potter,
Garrick & Potter Elevator.  At that time this house was operated by Vern Henry.
The elevator later became known as Roscoe Grain Company.  In the spring of
1950 this house burned down.

In 1951 the Hartung Construction Company built a new and modern elevator on the
site where the Roscoe Grain Company house burned down.  They then incorporated
and formed the Roscoe Grain and Feed Company.  This house was then managed by
Ray Murray.

        Jake Hoffer, John Nehlich and John Himmerich, West Elevator 1944

In 1955 John Himmerich traded the West Elevator in to the Roscoe Grain & Feed
Company for stock in the Corporation.  John managed the elevator with Alvin
Schmitt as assistant.  In 1958 Hartungs built the Annex to the east of the
elevator with a capacity of 110,000 bu.  It was completed during harvest.
Personnel started filling the annex at 3 p.m. on a Monday, taking in grain from
7 a.m. to 12 midnight and running the cleaners all night, and had the annex
filled to within 2000 bu. of capacity at 3 p.m. that following Saturday.

In October of 1959 Alvin Schmitt left the business and Leo Himmerich became
assistant.  In 1963 Leo became manager with John Himmerich as bookkeeper.  John
retired in 1968.

        Leo and Georgia Himmerich, Alan Schmidt, Dennis Dayton and DuWayne Aman.

Three large quonsets used for grain storage are located five blocks north of
the elevator.  One was built in 1956 and the other two were purchased from
Hartung's elevators and moved from Woonsocket and Lane, S.D. in 1964-65.  Two
of these quonsets were sold to Schurr's in December 1981.  The remaining one is
used for chemical and storage of equipment.

In February 1976, South Dakota Wheat Growers purchased Roscoe Grain & Feed.
Leo Himmerich remained as manager and bookkeeper; John Malsom, who joined
personnel in June 1967, became assistant; and Dennis Dayton was employed as
fertilizer man. Bulk fertilizer tanks and an anhydrous tank were installed.

The former West Elevator was dismantled in 1978.

Philip Huft was employed from 1977-78.  Georgia Himmerich became bookkeeper in
October 1979. DuWayne Aman became employed in the spring of 1980 and became
assistant when John Malsom retired in the spring of 1982.  Alan Schmidt became
employed in the spring of 1982.

A 28' x 70' Feed Warehouse was built to the west of the elevator in the summer
of 1982.

SPITZER IMPLEMENT INC.

This business dates back to 1920 when in April of that year, John Spitzer moved
to Roscoe and was engaged in the Implement and Garage business with John Lammle
and Ben Mayer.  They purchased the Yellowstone Garage from Jacob Schumacher,
located at the present Cenex station site.  They had dealerships in Model T
Ford, Fordsen Tractors, and Durant Cars.  In 1925, Ben Mayer sold his share to
John Lammle.  They continued with the Ford Agency, Advance Rummely Tractors and
Threshing Machines.  From 1925 to 1927, they also added the Case Tractor and
Machinery contract.  In 1927, they cancelled the Case contract and took on the
John Deere Implement line.  That some year the two partners drew lottery as to
who would handle the Ford line and who would handle the implement line.  At this
time, they dissolved partnership and John Spitzer, having drawn the implement
business, purchased and moved the old dance hall from the Edmunds County
Fairgrounds on Roscoe's east side, to the newly purchased lot where the
Jamieson Hotel had been.  The business was operated out of this building until
1947, when he sold it to his son, Erwin, and his son-in-law, Ervin Bader.  At
this time, a new, larger, block and brick building was constructed at the some
site.  In the following years they added the Farmhand, New Holland and
Versatile lines of machinery to the business. Through many of these years, all
the aspects of the business, such as the selling, mechanic work, maintenance,
bookkeeping etc. was all done by the two families.  Later Leo Geist was the
first outside employee hired as a mechanic.

January 1, 1963 saw another major change take place, when Erwin bought Ervin
Baders' interest in the business and Erv and Helen became the sole owners of
Spitzer Implement.  Soon after this time, a second warehouse was built on the
lots east of the Roscoe Auto Co. As the business grew, it became necessary to
stock more machinery and parts, creating a need to employ more people.

June 1, 1978, the business was incorporated, using the name Spitzer Implement,
Inc.

The need for more space in all departments prompted the decision to build a new
and larger building on west US 12.  The construction work was begun in the
summer of 1979 with completion seen in the fall of 1980.  The Grand Opening was
held December 5, 1980, which was well attended by the farmers, townspeople, and
representatives of the companies associated with Spitzers.

At the present time, the family members associated with the business are:
Erwin, manager; Helen, secretary-treasurer; son John and son-in-law Don
Caldwell, salesmen; and daughter Pat and sister Adeline Bader,
secretary-bookkeepers.  In addition to these there are two partsmen, three
tractor mechanics, three used equipment mechanics and one truck driver.

From its origin in the 1920s to the present time, this is the oldest family
owned business in Roscoe with the third generation of Spitzers now involved.

WIEDRICH CONSTRUCTION & PRO-MIX CONCRETE

Wiedrich Construction has been operating in Roscoe for 36 years.  Originally,
Henry Wiedrich started the company by himself doing carpentry, plastering and
cement work.  In 1961 the old Roscoe train depot was moved to its present site
and used for a shop area. Henry ran the company until 1973 when he moved to
Aberdeen to work for Quint City Coin where he is still employed.

Glenn Wiedrich, Henry's son, returned to Roscoe in the spring of '73 to take
over the company.  In 1977, he added a steel building line and started to
specialize in cement work.

Wiedrich Construction was incorporated in May of 1979 by Glenn and his two
brothers, Ken and Henry, when Ken returned to Roscoe after working for Cargill
in Minneapolis for 10 years.  Pro-Mix Concrete, Inc. was also started at the
same time to provide the construction company with concrete as well as customers
within a 50 mile radius of Roscoe.

Since that time the companies have continued doing cement work, block work,
agricultural building and commercial building.  In 1980, the middle room of the
old depot was remodeled to make an office and in 1982 the old Phillips 66 gas
station was moved to their lots as a shop and storage area.

        Otto Wudel in his shoestore

                ROSCOE CHURCH HISTORIES

FIRST PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH OF ROSCOE

        First Presbyterian Church and Manse

In July of 1883 the Rev. H. B. Johnson came from LeBeau and held the first
church service in Roscoe.  He preached in Roscoe again in September and also
August 17, 1884.  These services were held in the schoolhouse.  The Rev. Mr.
Johnson was a Presbyterian Missionary stationed at Watertown, Dakota Territory.
He organized the First Presbyterian Church of Aberdeen January 1, 1882.

The First Presbyterian Church of Roscoe was organized September 27, 1885, by
the Rev. Mr. H. B. Johnson and the Rev. Mr. G. A. Brandt with the following
charter members: Mr. and Mrs. L. G. Sims, Mr. and Mrs. J. C. Cram, Robert
Landoo, Hector, Belle, Neil, Mary and Kate McLean and Robert Strehlow.  The
church was without regular preaching services until the summer of 1885 when the
Rev. Mr. Kennedy was appointed by the Presbytery to take charge of the field.
Not receiving the support asked for from the board, he withdrew from the field
to what is now North Dakota. His commission was forwarded soon after his
withdrawal.

In April, 1886, the Rev. G. A. Brandt was appointed to take charge of the
church.  A Congregational meeting was held in the school house October 31,
1886, to incorporate.  Trustees elected were: L. G. Sims, J. C. Cram, James
Frazier, Byron Ripley, Zack Sutley, George Skinner, and Edgar Sharpe.

The present building was started in the summer of 1886.  The lists of materials
and wages paid are quite complete.  If the members of the church worked on the
building, they were not paid.  Labor and material amounted to about $2,000.00.
There is a mention of Louis Noreen of Faulkton, Dakota Territory, as carpenter.
The siding cost 85 cents a square, shingling $2 a day, hauling stone $1.50 a
day, tending mason $1.50 a day.

J. C. Cram was the first Elder elected in the church. Mahlon T. Lightner become
the second. The Rev. G. A. Brandt asked to be relieved of his duties September
8, 1889, to accept a call in Duluth Presbytery.  He was succeeded September 15,
1889 by the Rev. Mr. A. S. Gardiner.

June 1, 1890 J. Cairns Cram resigned as clerk which office he had held since
the church was organized.  He went to Niobrara, Neb. as a Sunday School
Missionary, where he was ordained into the Gospel Ministry.

March 26, 1893 when the Rev. Mr. Gardiner left the Roscoe Church, the Rev. Mr.
J. Cairns Cram was called to supply the pulpit for one year and remained until
1897.

The Rev. W. J. Krieger served as pastor from 1897 until 1901.The Rev. A.
Glendenning from 1901 to 1902. The Rev. William Davidson came in June of 1903
and left early in 1904.  The Rev. Hanibal Johns was here in 1904 and 1905.  The
Rev. Thomas Price was pastor in 1905 to 1908.  The Rev. Carl E. Kearns served
from June 1908 to 1911.

In 1909 the Bethel Holland Church of southeast of Hosmer was disbanded and most
of its members transferred to the Roscoe Church.

The Rev. A. G. Martyn was called May 1, 1911. In 1914 he was succeeded by the
Rev. John M. Bates, who in 1923 was asked to hold services at Glenham and Java
in addition to Roscoe.

In 1924 the church was remodeled and repainted. Barney Miller and F. A. Price
were the carpenters.  Paul Elford did the painting.  This was the time when the
steeple was lowered and the iron bars removed from the body of the church.

In 1926 the Rev. Mr. Bates resigned and was succeeded by the Rev. Mr. Cecil
Allen who served until 1930.  In 1930 electric lights replaced the gas lights.
Jake Wold installed the electric lights.

In 1931 the Rev. Mr. John Rhys Evans was called to serve Roscoe and Powell.  In
1934 Java and Glenham churches were added to his circuit.  He left Roscoe June
30, 1940.

The Rev. Mr. Albert Howland was installed as Pastor May 20, 1941 and served
until June 3, 1945.  Lewis Conrad served as a student supply for the remainder
of the summer.

Allen Justad served as student supply from April 7, 1946, to August 10, 1947.

The Rev. Ernest Meyer was installed as pastor March 2, 1948.  He left April
30, 1950.  During this time the church was moved off its foundation, a basement
built and the church moved back onto its new basement. While this work was
being done services were held in the Congregational Church.

The Rev. Mr. Leslie B. Lewis began his ministry in Roscoe May 4, 1951.  He
served until May, 1954.  The Rev. Christ Weintz supplied until July, 1956.

The Rev. Edward D. Freeman arrived in Roscoe July 4, 1956 and was installed
July 8, 1956.  He served Roscoe, Java and Powell Churches.  He resigned
effective October 1, 1959.

November 1, 1960 the Rev. Herman Rozendaal began his ministry in Roscoe and
stayed until June 2, 1963. The Rev. Harold W. Barhauer was installed June 14,
1964.  He and his family lived in Hosmer as he served the Hosmer Congregational
Church and the Roscoe Presbyterian and Roscoe Congregational Churches.  The two
Roscoe congregations worshiped together as one congregation.  He left May 29,
1966.  A student, Leonard Bertsch, came in June of 1966 and served until July 2,
1967.

        First Presbyterian Church 1982

The Rev. Donald Heeringa was installed July 9, 1967. He also served the Roscoe
congregations and Hosmer. He resigned effective May 31, 1969.

The Rev. Donald Stoebner was installed October 12, 1969.  He was a
Congregational pastor.  His parish was the Ipswich Congregational Church, the
Roscoe Congregational Church, the Roscoe Presbyterian Church and the Powell
Presbyterian Church.  He conducted three worship services each Sunday forenoon.
He left February 29, 1976.

The Rev. Delbert H. Gault began his ministry in the parish May 15, 1976 and
left October 1, 1978.  During this time the Roscoe Congregational Church members
withdrew from the parish arrangement.  The parish arrangement was dissolved
when Rev. Gault left.

The Rev. George R. Backer began serving the church on a Sunday to Sunday basis
in October 1978.  He is a U.C.C. pastor.  Presbytery appointed the Rev. Mr.
Backer to serve as interim pastor from July 1, 1979 to July 1, 1980.  Since
Rev. Backer left, the church has been served on a Sunday to Sunday basis by
retired pastors and a number of lay persons.

At the moment Rev. Sam Allison (Pastor of the Reformed Church in Hosmer) is
serving this church on a Sunday to Sunday basis.  He began his ministry here
October 11, 1981.

PRESBYTERIAN LADIES AID

The Presbyterian Ladies Aid is believed to be the oldest women's organization
in Roscoe.  The old record books show it was first organized in September 1886.
They had a written constitution such as they still have, with a few differences.

Admission as a member was 25 cents and dues 10 cents per month.  One article
said that gentlemen could become honorary members by paying a fee of one
dollar.  There was a list of eight gentlemen who had availed themselves of the
privilege.  They elected officers every six months and at first had meetings
once a week.  They sewed for anyone needing help and received 10 cents for
making an apron, five cents for a pillow slip.  They made and sold all kinds of
fancy articles, such as work bags, plush handkerchiefs, cases, knit "wristers",
crocheted lace "tidies" and fancy aprons.  They had many socials and gave
suppers also.

Membership fluctuated from eight to 15, but how they did work.  They pledged
$100 to the church building fund and paid it up.  They paid $5 on the communion
set, bought a set of decorated dishes and also furnished the minister's study.
In 1894, they purchased the church bell for the total price of $88.  They also
bought wall paper and carpeting for the church, also purchasing the pulpit for
a total price of $56.75.

        Hilda Schurr, Helen Rohrbach, Emma Nehlich, Jeanne Schurr, and Helen
        Downer

Another thing they did to raise money for the aid was to sell pictures of the
church for 25 cents.  There is no record from 1894 to 1897 when the Aid was
reorganized and when the constitution was revised.  Of all the older members
listed we still have the name of Elford, Clara joins them when she is in town.
They now live in Harlingen, Texas.

The present Aid is much as it was then and has followed the early example of
hard work and devoted to the church.  There was also a missionary society and
the Aid and some women belonged to both.  The two organizations merged and
became the United Women's Fellowship, under which they work now.  The present
membership is now Emma Nehlich, Mrs. Robert, (Hilda) Schurr, Mrs. Jake (Katie)
Finkbeiner, Helen Downer, Mrs. Arnold (Jeanne) Schurr and Mrs. Herbert (Helen)
Rohrbach.

ST. PAUL'S EV. LUTHERAN CHURCH

The early seeds of St. Paul's goes back to at least 1885.  Among the earliest
pioneer Lutherans we find the names of Adolph Jeschke, Karl and Julius Schipke,
and August Zander.  The latter came from the Wausau, Wisconsin area, and
settled southwest of Roscoe.  The

        1907 Church and parsonage

Deden family soon joined this group of Lutherans.  Oxen and wagon provided
transportation on the South Dakota trails.  A team of horses was a luxury.
Education was gained from books that were brought along and was taken care of
in the home by the family members. Desiring to continue to hear God's Word
these early settlers contacted Pastor Prey, who agreed to come from Aberdeen
every four weeks.  The Zander home served as his lodging place and the church
services were also held there.  Later the Schipke, Jeschke, and Deden homes
also served for German Lutheran services.  At this time the pastor was also
asked to be of help to them in their home education projects.

In 1889 a small schoolhouse was built north of Roscoe and church services were
conducted there.  By this time other German families had settled in the
community and the attendance at the services was increasing.  At this time
these Lutherans were being served by Pastor Helmreich, who came from Loyalton.

On September 4, 1893, this group of German Lutherans organized St. James Ev.
Lutheran Church of Roscoe.  Pastor F. A. Kiess from Mansfield was serving the
congregation at this time.  In the following years this young congregation was
also served by Pastor Wm. Meyer, who came from Wecota, S.D. These early pastors
represented the Iowa and Missouri Synods. With the services of Pastor Fehlau
the congregation was served by the Minnesota Synod.

Pastor J.C.A. Gehm of Bowdle served the congregation from 1900 to 1904.  Then
Pastor Keller, also of Bowdle, served from 1904 to 1907.  It was during his
pastorate that the congregation reorganized and adopted the name St. Paul's
Evangelical Lutheran Church.  Those who signed the constitution at that time
were: C. Beyers, M. Deden, J. Laabs, Julius Schipke, and Karl Schipke.

In 1906 the first church was erected under the guidance and direction of Pastor
Keller, who was also efficient as a carpenter.  At this time the membership
consisted of 27 voting members.  The first child to be baptized in this new
church was Cecelia Jeschke.  An organ was donated by Mrs. Deden and her daughter
Ellen, who was the first organist.

In 1907 the first parsonage was erected.  Also in that year Pastor A. C. Bartz
become the first resident pastor of St. Paul's.  Pastor Bartz served St. Paul's
until 1911. He was succeeded by Pastor Kerber who remained only one year.  In
1912 a call was extended to Rev. Ernest Birkholz who served the congregation
from 1912 to 1917.  During his pastorate, the Ladies Aid was organized.  After
his departure the congregation called Pastor Fred Manteufel who remained in
charge until 1921.

        Old Church and new church built in 1928.

In 1921, a call was extended to Pastor J. P. Scherf of Balaton, Minn., who
accepted and served faithfully for 18 years.  The Lord's blessings were evident
also in outward growth of the congregation and its membership increased to 100
voting members.  It became apparent that new facilities for worship were needed
and so it was decided to build a beautiful gothic style church.  The architects
for the church were A. F. & Ray Gouger of St. Paul, Minn.  The following served
on the building committee with Pastor Scherf, J. F. Hardt, August Zander, Jacob
Guthmiller, Karl Winkler, and Chas.  Christen.  The new church was dedicated to
the service of the Lord December 16, 1928.  This building has a seating
capacity of about 350.  Over the years it has become a landmark in the
community.  The old church was purchased by St. James Congregational Church of
Roscoe and is still standing two blocks north of its old site.  The church bell
was a donation from Pastor Scherf.  The pews and carpeting were provided by the
Ladies Aid.  The beautiful art glass window above the entrance, which is of
imported German antique art glass was a donation of Mr. Samuel Ernst.  The
window in the chancel was a gift of the Pastor Scherf family.  The first Sunday
School was also organized during the ministry of Pastor Scherf.  In 1939,
Pastor Scherf retired from the active ministry.

        Church interior

        St. Paul's Lutheran Church

Pastor Scherf was succeeded by Pastor Herbert Lou, who served faithfully until
February, 1951.  During his pastorate the congregation was able to retire its
church indebtedness in 1941.  October 24, 1943 St. Paul's celebrated its 50
years of grace.  Former Pastors A. C. Bartz and Ernest Birkholz delivered the
festival messages, as did also Pastor Wiedenmeyer, a son of the congregation.
during Pastor Lou's pastorate, there was a large increase in the congregation's
membership and also much growth and development of the Sunday School.  In 1947,
a beautiful eight-rank Wicks pipe argon was installed to lead the voices of the
members in singing praises to God.  In the summer of 1950 German and English
services were conducted every Sunday. January 10, 1951 a peaceful release was
granted Pastor Lou to accept a call to Elkhorn, Wis.

In June 1951, Pastor George Boldt accepted the call extended by this
congregation.  Under his leadership the present beautiful parsonage was built.
The parsonage building committee was Theo.  Winkler, Alb. Beyers, Emil
Borkirchert, Fred Wiedenmeyer and Erwin Bader.  Many members of the
congregation donated material and labor to see its completion.  At the some
time the interior of the church was remodeled and redecorated.  In 1952 the
German and English constitutions were revised.  Pastor Boldt started a family
Bible class and a Sunday School class for the newly confirmed youth.  Also
during his pastorate choir work was continued.  February 18, 1955 Pastor Boldt
was granted his peaceful release to accept a call to Morton Grove, Ill.

        Parsonage

Pastor W. Schuetze was installed as pastor of St. Paul's congregation May 15,
1955.  Pastor Schuetze served the congregation faithfully until April 1958.

Pastor Martin Janke accepted the call to be St. Paul's shepherd and was
installed August 17, 1958.  During his pastorate the stained glass windows were
installed in the church.  The English constitution was adopted in 1959.  In
1962 a new classroom building was dedicated to the Lord.  The building has two
large classrooms with folding doors and five small classrooms and lavatories,
all of which at the present time are used for Saturday School and Bible
classes.  After serving St. Paul's for seven years Pastor Janke accepted a call
to Faith Lutheran Church of Fon du Lac, Wis.

        Education Wing built in 1962

St. Paul's new pastor was Pastor Pingel who was installed June 27, 1965.  In
December of that year the congregation suffered a considerable loss when an
electrical fire damaged the education building.  In May, 1968, Pastor Pingel
accepted a call to Lincoln-Mio, Mich.

September 29, 1968, Pastor G. Geiger was installed as shepherd of St. Paul's.
During his pastorate a Young People's Society was organized.  Many improvements
were also made to the church building.  The basement kitchen was completely
remodeled. In 1972 a new sound system was installed in the church.  In 1975, new
sidewalks were laid around the church property and sod next to the sidewalk and
in front of the church. The first Sunday in December, 1976 marked the final
German service at St. Paul's. In 1978 the interior of the church was
redecorated.

Pastor Geiger was called to his eternal home in heaven February 28, 1982, after
a long illness.  A memorial service was held March 5, in St. Paul's church.

May 18, 1982, Candidate Timothy Winkel, a 1982 graduate of Wisconsin Lutheran
Seminary, was assigned to serve St. Paul's congregation.  He was ordained at
St. Paul's on the 27th of June, 1982.

Church officers, as of the writing of this history are: chairman, Jerome
Fischer; secretary, Russell Beyers; financial secretary, Duane Haar; treasurer,
Mark Beyers.  Trustees are Orville Baer, Calvin Kilber and Larry Ellwein.
Elders are Ken Vilhauer, Erwin Boer, Emil Dabbert, Eugene Conn, Lester Kilber,
and Melvin Beyers.

Saturday School teachers are Mrs. John Beyers, Mrs. Orville Baer, Mrs. Morris
Ernst and Mrs. Timothy Winkel.

Organists ore Minnie Pietz and Tonia Conn.

ST. PAUL'S LUTHERAN LADIES AID

        Adelina Krebs, treasurer; Esther Kilber, president; Lydia Trefz,
        secretary

The first meeting of the Ladies Aid of St. Paul's Lutheran Church was held in
1912 in the German language, when Rev. Ernest Birkholz was pastor.  Mrs.
Charles Christen was the president and Mrs. August Weidenmeyer was secretary
and treasurer.  Members were Mrs. Birkholz, Mrs. Charles Christen, Mrs. Jacob
Ernst, Mrs. Luie Deden and Mrs. Christ Beyers.

In 1916, the Aid was reorganized and more members were added.

The years 1916, 1917, and 1918 were very busy years, and in 1918 it was
advisable to discontinue the meetings.  Due to the war, objections were raised
to using the German language in any meetings.  In 1924, the Ladies Aid began
having meetings again every three weeks in private homes.  In 1928 when our
present church was built we were very active and were able to purchase the
pews, piano, carpeting and many more things that were needed.

Later on the church was decorated and they helped with the basement and kitchen
work.  Sunday School was organized in 1921 after Rev. J. P. Scherf came and the
ladies from the Aid helped.  Mrs. Charles Christen was the superintendent and
Ellen Deden was the teacher.

At the time of Roscoe's 75th Jubilee, officers for the Ladies Aid were: Mrs.
Martin Scherf, president; Mrs. Wm. Fischer, vice president; Mrs. John Knittle,
secretary; Mrs. Harold Ellwein, treasurer, with 42 members in the Aid.

In 1959 the Aid purchased a range for the kitchen.  In 1960 the ladies voted to
have meetings once a month instead of every three weeks.  In 1972 the Ladies Aid
helped when the kitchen was remodeled.  This post year the ladies helped paint
the ladies lounge and bathrooms in the basement.

The Aid purchased the "Meditations" which are offered to all interested church
members.  Cards are sent to the sick and bereaved, also visitors cards to all
people that visit the church.  The Aid sends gifts of money once a year to all
church affiliated missions: "The African Mission"; "Lutheran Home for the Aged"
and the "Lutheran Home" for retarded children.  At Christmas time we have a
free will offering and the proceeds go to the Apache Indian Mission in Arizona.

Our Aid is divided into three circles and each circle takes a turn at serving
for funerals and wedding anniversaries.  At present there are 61 members.  The
officers are: Esther Kilber, president; Mabel Winkler, vice president; Lydia
Trefz, secretary and Adelina Krebs, treasurer.

The Aid is very active and they are willing to help in whatever they are asked
to do for the church and its members.

PASTOR AND MRS. TIMOTHY WINKEL

Timothy John Winkel was born February 17, 1955 in Sparta, Wis., to Pastor and
Mrs. H. W. Winkel.  Doris Nolte was born December 18, 1954 in Mobridge, S.D. to
Professor and Mrs. W. Nolte.

        Dori, Jessica and Pastor Timothy Winkel

Pastor and Mrs. Winkel were married in St. Paul's Lutheran Church in New Ulm,
Minn., July 9, 1977.  They lived in Milwaukee, Wis., from 1978 until 1982 while
Pastor Winkel was attending Wisconsin Lutheran Seminary.  May 18, 1982 Pastor
Winkel was assigned to St. Paul's congregation.  They arrived in Roscoe June 15,
1982.  Pastor Winkel was ordained and installed as St. Paul's pastor June 27,
1982.  They have one daughter, Jessica Marie.

ST. THOMAS CATHOLIC CHURCH

        Old St. Thomas Catholic Church

The Catholic Parish of Roscoe was founded in February 1906 by Father John Krebs
who was the pastor of St. Augustine Church in Bowdle.  The parish was small and
nearly all the members were Germans born in Russia having immigrated to the
United States.  The Roscoe parish was named St. Thomas the Apostle.

Pastors came from the parishes of Aberdeen, Zell, Hoven, Bowdle and Orient from
1885-1910 to serve the parish, holding services in the public school building.
Father Robert Haije served from 1885-86; Father Bernard Bunning from 1887-88;
Father J. A. Stephan from 1888-90; Father J. L. Wulf from 1890-91 ; Father John
Lamesch from 1894-95 and Father John Krebs from 1906-10. There are no records
of who served from 1891-94 and 1895-1906.

Construction of the church building began in 1908 and, was completed in 1911.
Father Joseph Zimmerman of Ipswich parish was the first pastor to hold services
in the new church.  Pastors continued to serve the parish from Ipswich, Bowdle,
and Orient, they being Father Frederick Bowmann from 1912-17, Father John
Kettler from 1917-20, and Father John Krebs from 1920-26. Father Krebs came
from Orient by means of the train Saturday, held Moss Sunday and returned to
Orient Monday by train.  It was at this time that an addition was built on to
the church.

        First Church Council: Arnold Schmidt, vice-president; Phyllis
        Guthmiller, Fr.  Don Marohn, Anton Mardian, Theresa Malsom, Joe Goetz,
        Georgia Himmerich, president; and Emmett Johnson, Secretary-treasurer.

Father P. J. Cafferky of Ipswich took charge of the parish from October 26,
1926-June 6, 1927.  The parish house was completed in 1927.

Father John C. Smoley was the first resident priest and served until 1932.  The
parish had a resident priest and regular services from that time on until 1969
when it became a mission.  Priests who served during that time were: Father V.
J. Huckestein from 1932-34; Father E. J. Keefe from 1934-39 (he died while
serving), Father Clarence Schilling and Father Ed Mellemann during 1939; Father
W. M. Keane from 1939-41; Father Conrad Ludevig from 1941-43; Father Ludgar
Harding from 1943-51 (he died while serving), and Father A. Siebert from
1951-60 with Father Edward Foote helping in 1958 during Father Siebert's
illness.

Father Alphons Hojenski served the parish from April 27, 1960-63.  It was
during this time that a building fund was started for the new church.

Father John Adamiec served from January 1, 1964 to October 1968.  The present
church and parish hall were built in 1967 at a cost of $110,000.00 The church
was dedicated October 22, 1967.  The old church was torn down.

Father John Louis served from October 11, 1968 to July 1969.

        Present Catholic Church

        Church interior

        Present Church Council: Arnold Schmidt, president; Fr.  Don Marohn,
        Theresa Malsom, secretary-treasurer; Anton Maridan, Roy Meier, Joe
        Goetz, Dennis Stocker and Phyllis Guthmiller, vice-president.

Roscoe become a mission in 1969 to be served by Onaka.  It was then that Father
Alphons Hojenski resumed his duties as the pastor of Roscoe from August 9, 1969
until December 1979.  Father Paul Latusek, Father Joseph Presinell and several
priests from Sisseton served our parish until February 1980.

In February 1980 Roscoe became a mission of Hosmer and Father Francis
Wisniewski became pastor and served until November 1980.  Father Don Marohn
become pastor at that time and is the present pastor.

The baptism records start with January 1911. All baptisms recorded from
1906-1911 have been registered at St. Augustine's in Bowdle.  Those who were
baptized before 1906 were recorded at Holy Cross in Ipswich.

The first baptism registered in St. Thomas records is Conrad Menzia, son of
Anthony and Helen Volk Menzia January 29, 1911. The first marriage was that of
Anton Mardian, Sr. and Martha Schmidt November 22, 1910. Both were performed by
Father Joseph Zimmerman.

        Ladies Altar Society Officers: Theresa Malsam, president; Rosella
        Heyne, secretary and Marion Scherr, treasurer.

A parish council was formed in January 1981, consisting of six parish members
(elected by the parishioners), the Ladies Altar Society president, and the
pastor.  The first officers were Georgia Himmerich, president; Arnold Schmidt,
vice president; and Emmett Johnson, secretary-treasurer.  Theresa Malsam was the
Altar Society president and the remaining council persons were: Joe Goetz,
Phyllis Guthmiller and Anton Mardian.  Roy Meier and Dennis Stockert were
elected to replace Himmerich and Johnson for the 1982 term.

The Ladies Altar Society is an organization which serves as a unifying
structure of the ladies in the parish and the pastor, in whatever capacity the
parish may need.  They serve lunch for funerals, First Holy Communion and
Confirmation.  They hire someone to take care of the altar linens and mass
server garments.  The ladies of the society help with the annual church
cleaning.  Present officers are Theresa Malsom, president, Rosella Heyne,
secretary and Marion Scherr, treasurer.

REV. DONALD MAROHN

Father Donald Marohn, present pastor of St. Thomas Catholic Church in Roscoe,
was born November 14, 1936 in Minneapolis, Minn. to Herman and Catherine Wagner
Marohn.  Both of his parents were born in the United States.

Father Don, as he is known to his parishioners, attended elementary school in
Omaha, Neb., Minneapolis, Minn. and Aberdeen, S.D. He attended and graduated
from Central High School in Aberdeen.  He attended St. John's University in
Collegeville, Minn. from 1954-1962 and was ordained into the priesthood May 26,
1962.  Father Don has attended summer schools at the University of Son
Francisco and Loyola University at Chicago, Ill.

Assignments since Father Don's ordination have been: associate pastor and high
school teacher at Sioux Falls, S.D. Cathedral and O'Gorman High School from
1962 to 1965; pastor and high school teacher at Lennox, S.D. and O'Gorman High
School from 1968 to 1969; associate pastor and high school teacher of Notre
Dome High School and Holy Family Church at Mitchell, S.D. from 1969 to 1970;
pastor of St. Patrick's at Cavour, S.D. and St. Paul's at Iroquois, S.D. from
1970 to 1973; and pastor of St. Rose of Lima Church at Garretson, S.D. and high
school teacher at St. Mary's at Dell Rapids, S.D. from 1973 to 1974.  From 1974
to 1980, Father Don joined the Benedictine Monks at Blue Cloud Abbey at Marvin,
S.D. where he did missionary work for three years at Belcourt, N.D. on the
Turtle Mountain Reservation.  He become pastor of St. Thomas at Roscoe and Holy
Trinity at Hosmer, S.D. in 1980.  While serving these parishes, he was also
temporary pastor of St. John's at Onaka, S.D. during 1981 to 1982.

Traveling has been a highlight of Father Don's life.  He has traveled
throughout most of the USA except for the northeast Yankee country.  He has
traveled to Israel, Rome, Mexico, Canada and the Bahamas.

SISTER CLARICE SCHMIDT

Sister Clarice Schmidt (Helen) daughter of Katherine and Stanis Schmidt entered
the Benedictine Community of Sacred Heart Convent, Yankton, S.D. in 1947.  In
1960, she was chosen as one of the founding members of Mother of God Priory,
Watertown, S.D. After completing her college work at Mt.  Marty, Yankton, Sister
Clarice started her teaching ministry at Holy Trinity High School, Hartington,
Neb.

During the summer months she continued graduate work in home economics, science
and moth in Lincoln, Neb., Portland, Ore., Lake Forest, Ill., Fargo, N.D. and
Aberdeen, S.D. From 1955 to 1964 Sister was a teacher and principal of St.
Mary's at Richardton, N.D.

Her great interest in science spread to her students resulting in winners in
the State Science Fair for three successive years and one National winner.  Two
years of her teaching ministry was spent at the Indian Mission of Stephan, S.D.
From there Sister Clarice volunteered to be an exchange teacher which took her
to Cathedral High School in St. Cloud, Minn. where she taught moth and home
economics for four years and was a visiting professor at the St. Cloud School
of Nursing, teaching physics and chemistry to the anesthesia and nursing
students.

It was during this time that Sister made a decision to prepare for pastoral
work and began her studies at St. John's University, Collegeville, Minn. during
the year and Loyola University, Chicago during the summer months.  Since the
completion of her graduate studies in theology, scripture, and counseling, she
was director of Harmony Hill Continuing Education Center, Watertown for two
years and a member of a pastoral team the past eight years in Highland, Ind.
and currently at St. Mary's Parish, Plymouth, Minn.

Her work at St. Mary's is primarily in outreach ministry to new families,
inactive, aged, counseling, spiritual direction, training lay people in the
caring ministry, leadership training and coordinating social ministries. Sister
Clarice is the only member of St. Thomas' Parish who joined a religious
community in the history of the parish these past 100 years.

ST. JAMES CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH U. C. C.

The history of the St. James Congregational Church of Roscoe begins December 8,
1929, with the conducting of a meeting of the Bishop School house, located three
miles north of Roscoe.  At that time all interested families, formally
organized themselves into a body, agreeing to start a church in the
Congregational faith, agreeing to call it the St. James Congregational Church.
The organizing committee was assisted by the Rev. J. D. Gross from Redfield.
The Rev. J. Weber from Hosmer and the Rev. T. W. Rodach from Selby.  The charter
members at that time being Mr. and Mrs. Gottlieb Kirschenmann, Mr. and Mrs.
Adolph Lehr, Mr. and Mrs. Fred Hoffer, Mr. and Mrs. William Scherbenski, Mr. and
Mrs. Fredrich Huft, Mr. and Mrs. Philip Huft, Mr. and Mrs. Jacob J. Moyer, Mr.
and Mrs. John Rohrbach, Mr. and Mrs. Christian Voegele, Mr. and Mrs. Andrew
Lehr, Mr. and Mrs. John Fetzer, Mr. and Mrs. John Voegele, Mr. and Mrs. Jacob
Huft, Mr. and Mrs. John Huft and Mr. and Mrs. Fredrich Roesch.

The present church was moved to its present location in the year 1930.  The
building was purchased from Mr. John Spitzer and the lots were purchased from
Mr. F. C. Poeschl December 31, 1929.  The building was the former Lutheran
Church in Roscoe and they had at that time built themselves a new church.  The
church was formally dedicated September 14, 1930, at which time the first
mission fest was also held.  The occasion was honored by serving dinner in the
old school building, which at that time was located where the present school
shop and bus garage is located.  The dedication and dinner brought a great
number of people from all the different parts of the state.  The church, at
that time being yoked with the Bowdle parish, under the director of Rev. Jacob
Kessler who served from 1930 to 1936. The new church progressed due to the
members giving both their time and donations, such as the Altar being a
donation of Mr. and Mrs. Fredrich Roesch.  The next pastor to serve the church
was Rev. Danial Schurr, who served from 1936 to 1942; succeeded by the Rev.
Gottlieb Keppler, who served from 1942 to 1946; the Rev. Henry Hoersch, who
served from 1947 to 1955 and the Rev. Herbert Rath, who served from 1956 to
1963.

In January 1964 the Roscoe Congregational, the Roscoe Presbyterian and the
Hosmer Congregational Churches, formed the Hosmer, Roscoe, Larger Parish and
May 6, 1964 the Rev. Harold Borhauer was called. He served the parish until May
1966.  Then student pastor Rev. Leonard Bertsch served the parish from June
1966 to July 1967, at which time the Rev. Donald Heeringa was called and served
the parish from July 1967 until May 1969.  In June of 1969, the Rev. John
Iszler of Bowdle started to serve the Hosmer Congregational Church and the
Roscoe Congregational Church.  The Roscoe Presbyterian Church joined the
Ipswich Congregational and the Powell Presbyterian Churches and the Rev. Donald
Stoebner was called in October 1969, serving until February 1976.

Rev. Delbert Gault was called and served the Roscoe, Ipswich & Powell churches
from May 1976 until October 1978 and in January of 1977 the Roscoe
Congregational Church went again on their own and again yoked with the Hosmer
Congregational Church.  The Rev. Alvin Schmierer had started to serve the
Hosmer Congregational Church in 1976 and April 1977 Rev. Alvin Schmierer
started to serve the Roscoe Congregational Church and is still at this time
serving the Roscoe and Hosmer Congregational churches.

ROSCOE HERALD November 26, 1891
A sheep from Geo. Chappell's flock was killed by wolves last Sunday night
almost in the edge of town. Wolves are becoming very bold of late and are a
menace to the sheep growing industry.  Active measures should be taken to
reduce their numbers.

ROSCOE HERALD November 26, 1891
Lightner and Bickelhaupt's office served the purpose of dormitory Saturday
night for a lot of Russians who brought wheat to market and found the roads
almost impassable.  Jim Skinner was on duty as night clerk.

                ROSCOE SCHOOL & FHA HISTORIES

ROSCOE PUBLIC SCHOOL

Roscoe's first school was in a room above John Rice s store.  This building was
at the corner of Mitchell Street (today Main Street and Merrill Avenue).  Miss
Mattie Welsh was the first teacher.  J. W. Parmley was the County
Superintendent.  This school was held in the year 1883-1884.

Finding a suitable place to hold school was difficult This led to the
organization of a school district.  The first school board members were El!
Mack, H. H. Aldrich, and Joel Proctor.  Elmer Lewis was the first treasurer

The first meeting of the Glen School District No. 11 was held March 3, 1884, at
Roscoe, Dakota Territory The first bonds ($800.00) to build a school were sold
June 2, 1884.

G.S. Barker had a lumber yard in Roscoe.  The lumber for the school cost
$197.20. Bricks for the chimney were purchased from L. B. Edmunds for $9.00.
Other bills were: digging cellar, $8.31; carpenter work, $97.00; cement work on
chimney and foundation $122.60; putting in seats and cleaning, $3.00. The total
cost of the building was $4371.11. August 28, 1884 the building was completed.
Furniture was purchased June 13, 1884 from the U. S. Furniture Co. and cost
$136.75. The first teachers were Mary Welsh and Sadie Rice.

        First school built in 1884, present home of Gertrude and Lizzy Heintzman

        School built in 1888

This school was erected about where the Lutheran Church now stands.  Later this
school was moved three miles west of town and called the Snell School.  After
that Ben Heintzman bought it and moved it to town. Members of the Heintzman
family still live there.

In 1887 bonds were sold to build a new and larger school.  J. W. Parmley sold
the bonds in the amount of $2004.00. The cost of the building and furniture was
$2,351.12, The school was built on the lots where the shop building now stands.
It was two stories high and had four large rooms.

In 1891 a compulsory education law went into effect. Every child between eight
years and 14 years had to attend school 12 weeks a year, six of which must be
consecutive.  Also, no one of these ages could be employed during the school
year unless the school board gave a permit.

        Students in Roscoe School in 1925

In 1918 an architect drew up plans for a new school (including high school and
gym).  The site for the new school was in Morgan's Addition, Block 13.  The
cost of the school was $35,000.00. John A. Bishop was chairman of the board.
The school was completed in 1920 and in 1922 Roscoe High School graduated its
first class of seniors.  They were Martin Scherf, Donald Price, John
Scholfield, and Arden McCafferty.

Two new rural schoolhouses were added to Roscoe Independent School District No.
11. These were Bishop and Hauck Schools.  In 1934 the Dunn School was also
added. It was the first W.P.A. Project   in the area.

Also during the 1930s the school and Roscoe city made an agreement to hold
extracurricular activities, such as basketball games, etc. in the City Hall.
The school was too small to accommodate the crowds of spectators.

In 1953, the Board of Education purchased the Miner Larson home.  This home was
to be used for the superintendent, rent free.  Later on, the Robert Kilber home
became the superintendent's house.  It still is today.

        New school and old school taken in 1926

Again the school become too crowded and in 1953 Marion Smith drew up plans.
June 16, 1953 a bond election ($60,000.00) was approved.  The modern six- room
building become the grade school.  Consequently, Dunn, Bishop, and Houck
schools were closed and sold.

In 1954 the school district purchased two buses to transport the rural
children.  Spitzers and Farmers Union shared the responsibilities for the buses
and supplied the bus drivers.

A fire in March, 1954 in the heating plant room caused $10,000.00 damages.  A
new boiler was purchased and the high school completely redecorated.  The gym
was mode into a lunchroom in 1958.  Hot lunches were now served.  The head cook
was Mrs. Philip Aman and the assistant was Mrs. Lydia Heyne.  In 1957, the
garage-shop building was constructed, just north of the present grade and high
school.

Roscoe was now outgrowing its high school and the city hall was no longer large
enough for the crowds during sports activities.  Marion Smith drew up plans for
a new gym and high school classrooms.  Kyburz Construction of Aberdeen was the
general construction contractor.  McFarland of Aberdeen did the electrical work
and Bullock of Aberdeen had the mechanical contract.  This new facility was
completed in 1963. Roscoe schools now had a separate band room, home economics
room, science room, plus resource center and two other classrooms.

        Present Roscoe School

In April of 1969, bids were taken for a school library. Aslesen and Hermann
were the architects.  Jake Mohr and Sons of Hosmer received the base bid and
Adams Electric of Roscoe did the mechanical and electrical work.  The total
cost was $28,445-46.  The Roscoe Public School System is the only school in
Edmunds County that has built a separate library facility for their school
district.  The library was completed in 1971.

        Aerial view of school grounds

By 1978 the bus garage would not house all the buses. On October 9, 1978, the
school district purchased the present bus garage (north of Red Owl on Main
Street), from Jerome Malsom for $10,000.00.

There have been no new additions to Roscoe School in recent years.  However,
there is always upkeep and constant updating to do.  Presently, the roof is
being repaired and new fire doors have been installed.  A new gym floor is in
the plans.

Roscoe schools have many activities and clubs.  Some of the histories will be
found in this book and some histories were not obtainable.

        Back row: Edgar Light, William Schipke, Robert Parkans, Ernest
        Williams, Martha Adam, Joseph Redman, Ervin Fetzer. Front row: Wilbur
        Williams, Edward Guthmiller, Adeline Lammle, Raymond Decker, Laurence
        Decker, Mr. Alton Rogness, Director. (1930)

Roscoe High School has been classified as a "B" school since 1936 in athletics.
It has also been a member of District V and Region II.  The Yellowstone Trail
Conference was organized in 1938 and Roscoe has been a member since.  This is a
ten school conference and it sponsors an annual basketball tournament and track
meet for girls and boys.  Roscoe's athletic history has been a rich and
successful one, therefore, no team or individual will be singled out for
special notation.  At present, Roscoe has nine-man football, girls and boys
basketball, and girls and boys track.

        Present Roscoe School

Many graduates have passed through the halls.  Some have stayed in this area
and pursued their careers. Graduates presently working in the Roscoe School
System are: Annie Martz Nehlich (custodian), DeWayne Jeschke (bus driver), Deb
Dabbert Swalve (business manager), Marjorie Fischer Dabbert (third grade),
Bernice Roesch Beck (fifth grade), Phyllis Ehnes Guthmiller (Home Ec.), Harry
Himmerich (Social Science), Gerold J. Beck (Principal).

Present Public Schools are a level I Accredited School by the State Education
Department.

        Present school board, Gary Adam, Dale Roesch, Gerold Beck, principal;
        Don Fauss, superintendent; Richard Lester, Deloris Mitzel, Dwayne
        Leboldus, chairman and Debra Swalve, business manager.

The community has experienced some growth in the last few years and there are
high hopes the school system will continue to exist and educate its future
citizens.

        FHA

The Future Homemakers of American Chapter at Roscoe was chartered on December
14, 1964.

The first officers were Pat Nehlich, president; Rita Haar, vice president;
Donna Kirschenman, secretary; Kay Margeson, treasurer; and Katherine Hoffman,
advisor.

The FHA purposes are to provide opportunities for self-development and
preparation for family and community living and for employment.  To strengthen
the function of the family as a basic unit of society.  To encourage democracy
through cooperative action in the home and community.  To encourage individual
and group involvement in helping achieve world-wide brotherhood.  To institute
programs promoting greater understanding between youth and adults.  To provide
opportunities for decision making and for assuming responsibilities.  To become
aware of the multiple roles of men and women in today's society.  To develop
interest in home economics, careers and related occupations.

The FHA has projects which are social and service projects set up to fulfill
eight purposes.  Some projects have been helping to beautify the school grounds
and city park, serving large group suppers when there was no cafe in town,
helping with community celebrations, especially Roscoe's Bi-Centennial
celebration, for which recognition was received in the national FHA
publication, Teen Times.

Advisors have been: Katherine Hoffman, 1964-1969; Beth Pond, 1969-1972; Jackie
Schumacher, 1972-1973; Susan Smit, 1973-1976; Kathleen Winberg, 1976-1979; and
Phyllis Guthmiller, 1979-1983.

The post state officers from the chapter were Ingrid Reuer, 1969-1970 and
Dorothy Faw, 1974-1975.

        Donnie's Clown, a favorite at Roscoe's Homecoming parades.

ROSCOE ROCKS

C. P. Morgan has just returned from Chicago, where he has completed
arrangements for building his new hotel.

Mr. Androc has let the contract for his general store building and it will be
commenced in a few days.  Mr. Androc is a live man and means business all the
time. Success attend him.

What we need is a lumber yard and a drug store.  Both businesses will thrive,
for Roscoe is bound to be a great railway center.

Our community was in a state of excitement a few days ago over the appearance
of three buffaloes, and our nimrods were ready and willing to bring them down,
but did not succeed.

We are getting to the front in the best shape, our town growing rapidly and
lots selling right along.  We expect to be right after your growing city,
Aberdeen, in a short space of time.

From The Aberdeen Republican August 9, 1883

                SPORTS IN ROSCOE

Athletic sports have been an important element in the life of Roscoe, beginning
in the early years with baseball, which has remained popular down through the
years.  The Roscoe Herald recorded baseball games back in the 1890s.  Many will
remember the games played on hot Sunday afternoons in farmers pastures during
the "dirty thirties" when there wasn't much else to do for entertainment and
the sheer joy of playing the game brought these farmers together to challenge
each other's playing ability.  Roscoe also had "town teams" for many years, one
of them pictured here.  The team of 1946 was a strong team with a very
impressive record, boosting exceptional ability in all areas.

In years past, the American Legion has sponsored a summer baseball program for
the youngsters in the Roscoe area, when there were enough interested boys and
girls to participate in the program.  Some years the City has contributed some
financial aid to help keep it going.

In the middle 1970's, independent baseball in Roscoe gave way to slo-pitch
softball for both men and women. The men hold an invitational tournament here
annually.

        Back row: Hugo Wiedenmeyer, business manager; Harold Sahli, Hugo
        Leidholt, Milbert Ellwein, Jiggs Barton, Ervin Sonnenfeld, Larry Meier
        and Vern Henry, Manager. Front row: Doc Schad, Ervin Lammle, Joe
        Prisbe, Harold Schumacher, Jerome Prisbe, Bat Boy, Al Meier, Walter
        Lammle and Lester Kilber.

Roscoe's High School athletic program can be traced back to 1918 when an
intramural system of boys and girls basketball was set up.  Games and practice
seasons were in an old church which was located on the site of the present
public library.  Roscoe become active in inter-school athletics in '20 and '21
after the construction of a new school building which contained a basement
gymnasium.  This marked the real beginning of Roscoe High School basketball.

In the years between 1920 and 1935 there was no real state-wide system of
basketball, even though state tournaments were being held as early as 1912.  The
large and small towns were thrown together into one class, which usually
resulted in a state tournament dominated by the larger schools.  During these
years, Roscoe was unable to achieve any real basketball success.

        1978 Slo-pitch softball team.  Back row: Glen Hieb, Bruce Sahli, Rick
        Garner, Reed Garner, Mark and Keith Schumacher. Front row: Steve and
        Darwin Rohrbach, Bob Hoyle, Ron Rohrbach, Ron Schumacher, and Al Grau

In 1936 the South Dakota Athletic Association set up the classified system of
"A" and "B" basketball which we have today.  Since 1936 Roscoe High School has
been classified as "B" school and has been a member of District V and Region
II.  For 21 years Roscoe was not able to win a single District championship,
although, they did capture District Runners-up honors in 1947 and 1957.  The
1958 team finally was able to break the 22 year tournament jinx by defecting
Hosmer for the District V championship.  The Hornets went on to defeat Selby
(District VI Champions) and then lost to Cheyenne (District VII champions and
top rated team in the State.) in the Region II finals.  The some team posted a
10-1 conference and 22-5 seasons record to make '58 Roscoe's most successful
basketball year.

        First basketball team.  Back row: Harold J. Christen, John Scholfield,
        Donald Price, Martin Scherf, Arden McCafferty. Front row: Milton
        Tschirley, Howard Cooper, Edmund McCafferty.

        1982 Slo-pitch softball team.  Back Row: Bob Hoyle, manager; Sue
        Schoenfelder, Marilyn Lehr, Zady Meier, Cindy Sahli, Deanne Hettick,
        DeLaine Kraft and Dennis Hoyle, manager. Front row: Diane Kraft, Bev
        Becker, Jennifer Meier, Nancy Johnson, Stacey Jacobson, Donna Kraft and
        Lora Bieber.

        1930-31 Hornets. Back row: Joseph Redman, Ernest Williams, Ervin
        Fetzer, Wilbur Williams, William Wiedenmeyer, Coach Rovang. Front row:
        Hildor Pearson, Raymond Decker, Kenneth Alcorn and Edgar Light.

Roscoe has been a member of the Yellowstone Trail Conference since it's
organization in 1938.  The conference provides for competition in basketball,
football and track.  In addition to 1958's District Champions, the years 1965
and 1968 also saw the Roscoe Hornets advance to Regional Tournament play.  The
years 1964 and 1978 gave us Yellowstone Conference Championships.

The first high school girl's basketball team was organized for the 1925-1926
season.  At that time the centers played full court, the guards and forwards
played half court.  After several years, girls basketball as an inter-school
sport was discontinued.  It was revived in 1968, still a half-court game, until
1975.  At that time a major change was made in girls basketball, when they went
to playing the game full court with the rules much the some as the boys form of
basketball.  A regular season was set up with the Roscoe girls participating in
Yellowstone Conference and in District V Tournament competition.  The 1982 team
saw many victories as they boasted a 16-5 win-loss season record, capturing the
Runners-up spot in both the Conference and District tournaments.

        1958 District V Champs.  Back row: Harry Himmerich, Donald Ellwein,
        James Baer, Elmer Pietz, Norman Schurr, Wendlein Menzia, Aloyce Menzia,
        Harvey Aman, Harley Kirschenmann, Leo Heyne and Jerold Preszler. Front
        row: Gerold Beck, assistant manager, Virgil Eisenbeisz, coach and
        Donald Meier, student manager.

        Cliff Schwoerer, Ned Jeschke, John Bader, Curt Jeschke, Todd Nehlich,
        Doug Prisbe, Henry Wiedrich, LeRoy Roth, and Larry Preszler.  Harry
        Himmerich, Coach.

Independent basketball has been popular for many years.  One of the more
successful teams was "Doc's Canaries".  In 1947, an amateur basketball team
known as "Doc's Canaries" was formed from young athletes who had graduated from
Roscoe High School.  The team was sponsored and managed by a local physician,
Dr. C. S. Schad.  What the young, small team lacked in size and experience, it
made up for in enthusiasm and talent, and sported an enviable won-lost record
in its first year against the best teams in the area including Aberdeen City
League teams.

        1978 YTC Champs. Back row: Bruce Geier, Flint Jeschke, Scott Baer, Ken
        Vogel, Robert Vetch, Bruce Fuhrman, Allan Jung, Keith Schumacher, David

The independent team at the present time is being sponsored by Spitzers
Implement and has met with considerable success.  Several of Roscoe's teams have
won a spot in State tournaments throughout the years, some of them being 1947,
1956, 1968 and a team made up of teachers in 1976.

The earliest report of football we found was in the Roscoe Herald February 11,
1898 as follows: "The Roscoe and Ipswich football teams played on the ground of
the latter on Saturday afternoon which resulted in a second victory for the
visiting players.  Two or three of the Roscoe boys got hurt in the early part of
the game, and had it not been for the fact that they had some old players among
them, the result might have been different.  As it was, the game was hotly
contested. The score at the close of the game stood 2 to 0 in favor of Roscoe.
W. P. Wilson was the umpire."

On February 10, 1900, the Roscoe Herald reported: "The Roscoe Football eleven
went to Faulkton to play the return game between trains last Saturday.  A hard
game was played, there being no goal scored on either side until five minutes
before time to end the game, when the umpire, who did not understand the rules,
made a decision which the Roscoe boys would not stand, and the game was called
off, it being near train time.  Roscoe claims the best football team in this
port of the state, and she has maintained it so for.  Faulkton has one or two
good players, but they don't understand the game!"

The re-introduction of Football was made in 1948, when Roscoe became part of
the Hiddenwood Conference schedule.  The first coach was C. L. Huckenpoehler.
Football has been a major sport in our school since that time and boosts many
successful seasons. They were Conference co-champs in 1950 and 1956, and were
Conference Champs in 1957, 1968, and 1973.

        1968 YTC Champs.  Back row: Coach Harry Himmerich, Ron Preszler, Glenn
        Spitzer, Doug Prisbe, Henry Wiedrich, Greg Beck and Wayne Buechler,
        student manager. Middle row: Dallas Schneider, Glen Erlenbusch, LeRoy
        Roth, Dennis Hoyle, Larry Preszler and Richard Schmaltz. Front row:
        LeRoy Kilber, Jon Trefz, Karvel Haug, Dick Beck, Mark Johnson, Chuck
        and Mark Trefz.

For many years the high school boys and girls have maintained Track teams.
Outstanding records have been set and many track stars have represented Roscoe
at the State Track Meets in the Spring.

Wrestling was a part of Roscoe High School's athletic program for two years--
1972 and 1973.  Over the years, Roscoe has also had participants in
Cross-country, Golf and Baseball.
