                ROSCOE ORGANIZATION HISTORIES

AMERICAN LEGION POST NO. 259

        Andrew and Harold Schauer

        Jim Kilber in front of first Legion Home.

Some time in July, 1937, a group of World War I veterans met to plan the
organization of an American Legion Post in Roscoe.  Dept. vice commander, Wm.
Kane, was consulted and an application was mode and filed with the department.
July 24, 1937 a meeting of all the veterans in the Roscoe community was called
and assembled in the Roscoe City Hall.  Temporary officers were appointed and
it was voted to hold the first regular meeting August 4, 1937 to elect regular
officers.  This first regular meeting was called to order by the temporary
commander, Paul Elford, for the election of officers, which were as follows:
Commander, Paul F. Elford; First Vice Commander, Chas. Fischer; Adjutant, E. E.
Curtiss; Finance Officer, Barney Miller; Chaplain, W. C. Peterson;
Sgt.-at-Arms, John Roesch; Historian, Bernard Mayer; Service Officer, Albin P.
Nystrom; Executive Committee, Wm.  Bertsch and W. C. Peterson.

The selection of a name for the Post was discussed, and it was voted to honor
Andrew Schauer, Jr., a Roscoe serviceman, who died in Camp Funstan, Kansas, the
latter port of October, 1918, during the Spanish influenza epidemic.

September 9, 1937, Wm. Kane, department vice commander, came for the
installation of the newly organized Post officers.  He brought with him, Marie
Koch, state president of the American Legion Auxiliary, and the officers of the
Aberdeen American Legion Sidney L. Smith Post.  Commander Paul Elford called the
meeting to order, then turned it over to the department vice commander, who
then appointed Chris Cacavas, commander of the Aberdeen Post, to conduct the
installation.  A temporary charter, numbered 259 was presented to the Post, and
was immediately signed by the following members: Paul F. Elford, Albin P.
Nystrom, George L. Price, L. E. Larson, John Roesch, Charles Ernst, Samuel
Bishop, Wm.  Schurr, Eugene Curtiss, W. C. Peterson, Jacob Voegele, Wm.
Bertsch, Thomas J. Hopper, Barney Miller, Reinhold Kilber, Wm. Stafford,
Charles Fischer, Bernard Mayer, Dwight W. Reidt, Jacob Rohrbach, Pius Fuhrmann,
Solomon Reede and Joseph Kraft.

In 1939, the Post needed a home, and after much negotiation, and with the
cooperation of the N.Y.A., a stone building was started in March of that year.
It is located on the west edge of Roscoe on lots purchased from J. W. Parmley
for the sum of $5.00. This home was used until 1961, when the present Legion
home was completed.  An ice skating rink, adjacent to the old Post home served
as a pleasure spot in the winter months for youngsters.

During WWII, the Roscoe Post participated in the selling of bonds and defense
stamps.  Parties were held for boys leaving for the service, and flags were
presented to their parents.  For several years during the 50s, the Post
sponsored a Legion Indees Basketball team.  Rifles were acquired for the Post
Firing Squad and in 1953, 13 helmets, belts, and leggings were purchased for
this detail.  They were pointed white by Auxiliary members.

In 1947, the Roscoe Cemetery Assn. officially granted the Post a permanent plot
for the annual Memorial Day service programs.

In November 1950, the Post voted to include the name of Harold Schauer, another
son of Andrew and Katherine Schauer, Sr.  Harold served on the battleship "The
Chicago", and was killed in combat in January 1943 in the Pacific War Theater.
The new charter was received May 18, 195 1, and the name of the Post is now
"Andrew and Harold Schauer Post No. 259.

In 1959, the Post decided that they must either remodel or repair the old Post
home, for the purpose of expanding their activities and programs.  After several
months of discussion, planning, looking for a new and larger building, through
the efforts of the late Martin Scherf, they become aware of a large church in
Aberdeen that was to be moved or razed.  The church was purchased for $1,250
and members dismantled it and used the materials for the new Post home.  Several
acres of land, which had to be cleared and landscaped, were acquired through
negotiations with the City of Roscoe.  About 40 acres of land was purchased from
Fred Sauer in January 1960, for $75 per acre.  At the same time, Martin Scherf
donated 14 acres of land, and gave the Legion a 99 year lease on an additional
six acres.  Phil Schneider donated another six acres of land.

        Present Legion Post Home

This enlargement and acquisition of property necessitated changes in the Post's
structure and were advised to incorporate.  Our Articles of Incorporation were
completed in May 1960.

A plan was finally agreed upon as to the size and shape of the building and
construction was begun in 1960.  Many area citizens, other than Legionnaires,
donated labor and money for the cement blocks for the basement and building.
During the building, many problems were encountered such as basement walls
collapsing due to heavy rains and delay of work in general.  The first Legion
meeting was held in the new home January 5, 1961.  Misfortune struck again in
February 1961, when fire caused extensive damage.  It took several months to
repair and rebuild.

The Legion building completed, plans were made to construct a golf course just
south of the Post.  June 2, 1960 a Golf Assn. was formed, and it was agreed to
rent the land from the Legion Post.  This become a beautiful nine hole golf
course.

A ball park and athletic field, north of the Legion home, were completed in
1961.  This is now a lighted field and is used for all high school home football
games and other athletic functions.  These grounds are also used for the Summer
Junior Baseball program and the Men's and Womens Softball teams.

The Rod and Gun Club was given a lease on grounds east of the building and
permission given for them to erect their equipment for the purpose of holding
trap shoots.

The Post had to make a substantial loon to cover their building expenses and to
carry out their many programs.  Many fund raisers were held such as Smokers,
raffles and various other projects.  The Auxiliary gave them as much financial
assistance as they could, furnished much labor for interior decorating, and
completely furnished the kitchen.

For many years the main floor of the Post consisted of the meeting rooms,
restrooms, and the large area housed the bowling alley from 1962 to 1969.
During that time the lower level consisted of a large club room used for
banquets, bingo, dances, receptions, meetings, etc., the kitchen, restrooms and
the furnace room.  The large meeting room on the main floor was used for the
Special Education Class in the 1962-63 school year.  For several years,
beginning in May 1964, it was used as an office for the Selective Service Board.

The old stone Legion building with two lots was sold to Glen Township in July
1961 to be used as a meeting and election hall.

In 1972, the upper floor was completely remodeled to become what is known as
the "Legion Lounge" complete with a large dance floor, with a small stage area
for an orchestra and tables on three sides.  It also features a long bar.  In
1977, the kitchen was moved upstairs to the main floor for a more convenient
arrangement for the functions held there.  The "Legion Lounge" has been the
site for many wedding receptions, dances, the annual New Years Eve party, all
other Legion and Auxiliary activities and the functions of many other
organizations.  The District 14 Spring Meeting was held in Roscoe in March 1962
and again in April 1977.  The District Fall Meeting was held here in September
1968 and again in September 1972.

In connection with our country's Bi-Centennial in 1976, the Post obtained an Ml
15 Howitzer from an Army Depot in Pueblo, Colo., which Ervin Spitzer and Orval
Schneider transported here with the Spitzer equipment. It was mounted on a
permanent cement platform facing the city.  This was also the year the final
payment was made on their building loan and a "mortgage burning" ceremony was
held.

In 1981, the need for new flags in Roscoe was recognized and arrangements were
made with the downtown merchants to purchase flags from the Legion.  Brackets
were then mounted on the light posts by Legionnaires and the flags are put up
by the Legion on all the flag days.

        Present officers: Kenneth Vilhouer, adjutant; Dennis Hettich,
        commander; Orville Schneider, finance officer.

The Legion has received many certificates of merit and awards for achievement
over the years.  A high school boy is sponsored annually to Boys State and
Americanism awards are given to a senior boy and girl every year.  The Post
conducts the Memorial Day services, participates in community activities, which
includes leading the Homecoming parade with our Legion Color Guard, and
conducts graveside military rites for deceased comrades.

With the recent loss of our comrade, John Roesch, September 14, 1982, Paul
Elford remains the sole living charter member.

THE AMERICAN LEGION AUXILIARY

        Dolores Sahli, treasurer; Lorraine Reidt, first vice; Bernice Hettick,
        president; Georgia Himmerich, second vice and Carol Hettich, secretary.

The Andrew Schauer Auxiliary Unit 259 of Roscoe, S.D. was organized August 7,
1937 when a group of women, wives of World War I veterans, met in the home of
Mrs. Hilda (W.  C.) Peterson, for that purpose. The American Legion Post 259
was being organized at this same time.  Mrs. Marie Kelly of Aberdeen, S.D. who
was   District  14  President,   assisted   with    the organization.  Clara
Elford acted as chairman.

The Unit originally was named after Andrew Schauer, who died in October 1918,
during the Spanish Influenza epidemic, while stationed at Camp Funston, Kan.  He
was the son of Mr. and Mrs. Andrew Schauer, Sr. of Roscoe.

A Charter was issued to the Unit September 14, 1937 and the following 18 ladies
were made Charter members: Marie Bishop, Gertrude Bertsch, Minnie Curtiss,
Paulina Ernst, Clara Elford, Victoria Hopper, Emma Mayer, Lillian Nystrom,
Hilda Peterson, Clara Reidt, Martha Roesch, Hulda Reede, Margaret Rohrbach,
Esther Price, Viola Tschirley, Lydia Voegele, Caroline Fuhrmann and Katherine
Roth.

The first officers of the Auxiliary were elected the same evening of the
organization.  The first meeting of the Auxiliary was a joint installation of
the two groups, the Legion and the Auxiliary.  This meeting was held in the
city auditorium September 9, 1937, with Department President Marie Koch acting
as installing officer. Department Secretary Amy Anderson and District President
Marie Kelly, as well as a number of other members and officers of the Aberdeen
Auxiliary, assisted with the installation.  The gavel was presented to the
first Auxiliary President, Hilda Peterson, and the following were stationed at
their perspective offices: Clara Elford, lst vice; Viola Tschirley, 2nd vice;
Lillian Nystrom, secretary; Emma Mayer, treasurer; Clara Reidt, sgt.-at-arms;
Marie Bishop, chaplain and Minnie Curtiss, historian.

The next few years saw on increase in membership partly due to a number of
Bowdle and Loyalton ladies joining our group.  Bowdle left the unit in 1944 to
organize their own unit, as did Loyalton in 1950.

In 1939, the Gold Star Mother, Mrs. Katherine Schauer, mother of Andrew
Schauer, for whom the Post was named, was honored with the presentation of a
Gold Star pin and an honorary paid up membership. Gold Star pins were also
presented to the two Gold Star sisters, Ann Hammond and Martha Diegel.  Our
World War II Gold Star Mother, Mrs. Lena Baumgartner, was presented a Gold Star
pin in 1943.  She is our only surviving Gold Star mother and holds an honorary
membership.  We remember her with a gift every Mothers Day.

During WWII, the Auxiliary came to the aid of the Red Cross, sewing and
knitting, baking cookies for servicemen and their families, assisted in the
Aberdeen Canteen, furnishing food and willing hands.  Christmas boxes were sent
to 24 local boys.

With many WWII veterans returning, an all-out membership drive was held in
1946.  The membership increased from 50 to 81, with Adeline Bader being the
first president after WW II.  Lillian Nystrom had the honor of being elected
District 14 President at this time, the only Roscoe member ever to hold this
office.  The all time high membership of the unit was in 1947, with 87 members
and Erma Auchampach serving as president.

For Memorial Day 1946, a permanent graveside plot with white crosses was set up
in Morningside Cemetery.  These crosses were dedicated to the men who served
our country during the wars, and have died while in service or since their
discharge.  The Legion and Auxiliary hold special services at this plot on each
Memorial Day.  The Auxiliary also purchased graveside markers for deceased
members, a practice which has continued through the years.

In 1947, Leona Voegele was chosen the first girl to represent Roscoe at Girl's
State in Mitchell, S.D. The following girls were chosen to represent us since
that time: Betty Streifel, Dolores Kotschevar, Deloris Hettich, Esther Hettich,
Shirley Barton, Ramona Kilber, Elsie Hettich, Bernice Goetz, Luella Uhrich,
Dixie Schneider, Janice Werre, Carol Trefz, Sheila Schneider, Susie Ellwein,
Ann Marie Streifel, Mary Raba, Peg Meier, Sharon Beyers, Theresa Geier, Rocky
Schneider, Peggy Margeson, Debra Dabbert, Pam Schatz, Cindy Reidt, Cindy
Johnson, Cora Hieb, Patty Uhrich, Karen Meier, Maria Uhrich, Diann Reidt, Robin
Sahli, Stacy Himmerich, Kari Ernst, Francine Goetz and Marie Goetz. During the
polio epidemic, a John Deere Tractorcycle was purchased and donated to the
polio ward at St. Luke's Hospital in Aberdeen.

On May 28, 1951, the Unit name was changed to honor Harold Schauer, who lost
his life in 1943 in the Pacific theater of World War II, when his ship, the USS
Chicago, was torpedoed and sunk.  In tribute to these two brothers, the Unit
became the Andrew and Harold Schauer Unit 259.

Tiny Tots were added to the membership roll in 1954. The first Tiny Tots were
Vicki Schneider, Kay Margeson and Mary Murray.  This number has increased and as
they grow up, they become Junior members.  They are entertained by the
Auxiliary at the annual Christmas party.

Since 1960, the new Legion Home has been used as the meeting place.  Prior to
that time, meetings were held in the old stone Legion building and in the homes.
The Auxiliary helped the Legion complete the new club rooms with cash
donations, completely equipping the kitchen, furnishing the drapes, and
assisting with the interior painting.

The Auxiliary is proud of its many accomplishments over the years since it
began.  Every year we make our donations of cash, gift shop articles, sewn
items and whatever is needed to support the Auxiliary program in their efforts
to help the veteran and his family.  Locally we have sponsored First Aid
classes, immunization clinics, Vial for Life program for Senior Citizens,
Americanism Essay and Poetry contests in the school, Poppy poster contests,
sell the Poppies made by the disabled veterans, present flags to the
kindergarten class every year for saying the pledge to the flag, enter the
yearly Homecoming parades, observe Education week by presenting the local
teachers with a treat, send gifts to the State Home in Redfield, S.D., have
served many banquets and special groups, sponsored many fundraisers and assist
the Legion with their fundraisers such as the annual Smoker and the New Year's
Eve Party.

The District Girls State Tea was hosted in 1973 and the Spring District 14
meetings in 1974 and 1977.  Three of our Junior members, Deanne Hettich, Diann
Reidt and Robin Sahli, served as Pages at the State Convention in Aberdeen in
June 1975.  Mary Johnson won first prize for her Poppy Story at the State
Junior Conference held in Huron, S.D. in October 1973.

The present officers are Bernice Hettick, president; Lorraine Reidt, 1st vice;
Georgia Himmerich, 2nd vice; Carol Hettich, secretary; Dolores Sahli, treasurer;
Theresa Malsom, Sgt.-at-arms; Kitty Hauck, chaplain and Bonnie Roesch,
historian.  There are 40 members.

4-H CLUBS

An important activity for the youth of the Roscoe community has been the 4-H
program.  Several citizens of Roscoe have been leaders in the program for
various clubs over the years.

The first record of a 4-H club in Roscoe was in 1934 and was led by Anna Roesch
with a membership of 10 girls.  The early club work consisted of one main
project such as livestock, corn, gardening, conning, sewing, cooking,
handicraft, etc.  Over the years, club work has changed to include a variety of
projects of interest to the members which could be both boys and girls rather
than just boys or girls and one project area.

The current club in Roscoe is the Jacks and Jills 4-H club led by Floyd and
Virginia Reidt with Rae Wiedrich as assistant leader.  It was organized in 1979
and consists of 10 members: Chad Beyers, John Jung, Amy Rohrbach, Sally
Rohrbach, Kay Reidt, Jessica Roesch, Eugene Thomas, Tommy Wiedrich, Tracy
Wiedrich and Mike Wiedrich.  They are enrolled in a variety of projects and
programs which include arts and crafts, beef, clothing, dairy, foods and
nutrition, home environment, horse and safety.  They attend 4-H camp held
annually at Richmond Lake; participate in judging schools held throughout the
county, the county special foods, demonstration and speech contests as well as
the annual county achievement days held in August of each year at the 4-H
grounds in Ipswich.

They are actively involved in several community service projects including the
adoption of a grandmother and grandfather at Colonial Manor in Ipswich, safety
measures for citizens of Senior Citizen Housing Unit, as well as several other
safety projects in the area.  They also help with other community affairs;
selling poppies for veterans and Easter lilies for crippled children.  In
September 1982, they sponsored a Bike-Trike-A-Thon to raise funds for the
Roscoe Centennial.  It was very successful, having 35 riders riding 425 miles
and 142 blocks, raising $1,800.00 for the Centennial celebration.

LOYALTON HUNTERS 4-H CLUB

The Loyalton Hunters 4-H Club was organized in 1969 at the Vernon Oban home
with County Agent Herb Brandner present to explain the 4-H program to the
group.  Angelus Mitzel and Orville Baer assumed leadership of the club.  In the
fall of 1970, Angelus Mitzel helped organize a 4-H club for the Onaka area and
took over the leadership of the Sharpshooters 4-H Club.  Orville and Virginia
Baer were leaders of the Loyalton Hunters 4-H Club for five years.  In 1975
Vernon and Verna Oban took over leadership and still serve in that capacity at
this time.

        Back row: Richard and Debra Lester, Robert and Diane Allbee, Verna and
        Vernon Oban. Second row: Greg Lester and Scott Allbee. Third row:
        Charlotte and Cheryl Huft, Judy and Kathy Oban and Charlotte Allbee.
        Front row: Michael Lester, Johnny Oban and Terry Allbee.

The following area youth have been members of the club: Darwin Rohrbach, Ronald
Rohrbach, Rick Vargason, Scott Baer, Tim Baer, Mork Baer, Susan Ryman, Barb
Bruckner, Kathy Bruckner, Donald Bruckner, Wesley Faw, Dorothy Faw, Tamra Faw,
Dean Oban, Karen Oban, Alan Schmidt, Carol Schmidt, Duane Schmidt, Loren
Schmidt, Leonard Stern, Melody Stern and Laurie Stern.  Karen Oban and Dorothy
Faw later became members of the Roscoe Jr. 4-H Club.  Dean Oban graduated from
4-H in 1978 and was selected as county 4-H king that year.

At the present time the club has 10 active members. They are Johnny, Kathy and
Judy, children of Verna and Vernon Oban; Michael and Greg, children of Richard
and Debra Lester; Scott, Charlotte and Terry, children of Robert and Diane
Allbee, and Cheryl and Charlotte, children of Philip and Leona Huft.  Arts and
crafts and dairy are the main projects of the club's members but they also
enter foods, poultry, rabbits, crops, gardening, clothing, photography and
others.  The club has been active in club safety, community service and
citizenship projects for which they have received several awards.  Members are
also active in 4-H judging areas and most of the members have been on county
judging teams at the State Fair.  Cheryl Huft received a trip to Minneapolis in
1982 for placing fourth individual in the state of photography judging.  Scott
Allbee placed 10th individual at the State Fair in dairy judging in 1982.
Kathy Oban was a member of the Edmunds County rabbit judging team which placed
second at state in 1982.  Judy Oban was a member of the Edmunds County dairy
judging team for six years at State Fair.  They placed fifth at state in 1977,
sixth in 1978 (Judy placed sixth individual) and fourth in 1980.

Older club members are active on the county council, work as 4-H club
counselors and have attended 4-H club conference at Brookings.  Judy Oban
currently serves as a state 4-H ambassador.  Judy was selected as one of three
Edmunds County 4-H youth to attend Washington Focus in Washington, D.C. in the
summer of 1982.  They traveled to Washington for a week of leadership and
citizenship training, plus tours of historical sites. in September 1982, Judy
was selected as one of four South Dakota delegates to attend the National Dairy
Conference in Madison, Wis.

Some of the citizenship and community service projects the club is and has been
involved in are: floats for the Bi-centennial parades in 1976, selling slow
moving signs, play bingo and take popcorn and cookies to nursing homes, painted
fire hydrants, sell poppies, and Easter lilies, contribute to cancer, mow local
cemetery and township grounds annually, marking some graves, planting trees and
flowers, sponsoring community picnics, annual parents and grandparents potluck
supper, Good Neighbor Days, recognizing teachers, promoting poison and fire
prevention, cleaning ditches, Christmas item rummage, greeting card and bake
sales, annual Bike-A-Thon for cystic fibrosis, promoting agriculture days and
4-H week, making favors for local nursing homes and hospitals, entertaining and
serving lunch at the senior center.

HOW AND WHY EXTENSION CLUB

The How and Why Extension Club was organized May 29, 1950.  Sixteen members
joined.  Mrs. Mickey Bovee, Ipswich, was home agent.  Due to lack of interest
by the members, August 2., 1951 was their last meeting. Charter members of this
club were Hilda Schurr, Evelyn Larson, Ann Hammond, Marion Houg, Irene Roesch,
Emma Roesch, Emma Mayer, Berneice Margeson, Clara Elford, Erma Auchenpach,
Angie Lammle, Esther Lammle, Violet Neifer, Jeanne Schneider, Linda Bieber and
Tillie Job.

A new club was re-organized in 1956 Using the same name.  Ten members started
off this club.  The charter members were Marvelyn Roesch, Irene Roesch, Freida
Schurr, Marilyn Roesch, Emma Roesch, Lydia Dabbert, Ida Heyne, Marjorie
Dabbert, Dorothea Jung, Emma Schnaible.

May 31, 1957 was the first outing, to Helen's California Kitchen in Aberdeen
and since have made outings annual off airs with tours such as the School for
the Blind, Siebrechts Florists, Presentation College, Redfield School and Home,
Lux Candle Factory, Horse Races, Museums, Northern College plays and many
others.  These tours were usually followed by supper and perhaps a movie.

In 1957 a music festival was held in Roscoe.  Japan was the country
illustrated.  Japanese songs were sung by Noma Benfit and several Japanese
items were on display.

        Ida Heyne, Marvelyn Roesch, Irene Roesch, Emma Schnaible, Dorothy Jung,
        Emma Roesch and Frieda Schurr.

June 1958 Roscoe held their DIamond Jubilee. The club entered a float in the
parade. It was a large decorated birthday cake with 75 candles and several
flowers.

May meeting 1958 the meeting date was changed from the second Tuesday of each
month to the fourth Tuesday. It was suggested that the club be re-named but
after some lengthy discussion the name was left the same, How and Why Extension
Club.

April 26, 1960 each mmeber wore an Easter bonnet which was an original creation
consisting of utensils, Easter nests, egg cartons, etc.

Several dollars in contributions were given several years in a row to cancer,
heart, Easter seals, March of Dimes, Redfield School and Home, 4-H Merit
Systems, The Anne Carlson School for the Ohysically Handicapped at Jamestown
and many other organizations.

Probably one of the biggest tasks the How and Why Extension club ever tackled
was a style show. August 30, 1963 the club and Ann's Apparel together sopnsored
this event. One-hundred guests witnessed "The Art of Fashon Fall '63". Womens
and childrens clothing were modeled by members, members children and some
friends. A dessert lunch was served to the guests. This was a very rewarding
accomplishment.

April 30, 1963 the District Meeting was held in Roscoe for 240 guests. The club
was in charge of decorations with a patriotic theme.

In October 1966 one of the members, Ann Knittel, attended the National
Extension Homemakers Council Meeting in Honolulu, Hawaii.

In December 1966, the club sponsored a Litterbug Contest and awarded three
prizes. March 8, 1968 a Medical Self-Help Training Course was started
consisting of eight weekly training sessions. The course was open to the
public.

Starting September 1972 the meeting date was changed to the third Tuesday of
every month.

Two 25 year members, Emma and Irene Roesch, were honored at the Heritage Tea in
Ipswich in 1974 and the club honored them by taking them to supper and the play
"Fiddler on the Roof" in April 1974. Twenty-five year necklaces wre also
presented from the club.

In June of 1976 the club had a float entry in the 4-H Bicentennial Parade.

Roscoe hosted the District VI Extension Homemakers Meeting with the club
serving coffee, cake and bars after the meeting in 1976. Roscoe hosted the
meeting again in 1982.

November 14, 1980 at the Edmunds County Holiday Fair Ida Heyne and marvelyn
Roesch were honored as 25-year members.

The how and hwy Extension Club worked with the following Area Home Economics
Agents: Eleanore Grubele Krokosh, 1954-1972, Karen Zilberberg Geditz, 1972-1976
and our present Area Extension Home Economist Carole Sweeter, who started March
1, 1976.

how and Why Extension Club also entertained in the area nursing homes at
different times. They also sponsored the CPR classes in 1981 and 1982, along
with entertaining the husbands at various times over the years. Many auction
sales were served through the years.

Some of the members have also served in different offices at the county level.
Freida Schurr also served as district president from 1976-1979.

Over the years some members dropped out an new members were welcomed. They try
to keep the membership at 12. The following ladies are now active members: Lynn
Conn, Dorothy Ernst, LaVerne Fuhrmann, Sharon Guthmiller, Martha Finkbeiner,
Ida Heyne, Deloris Lehr, Elda Hoffer, Theresa Malsam, Gertrud Preszler, Jeanne
Schurr and Marvelyn Roesch.

IVY CIRCLE GARDEN CLUB

The interest in Horticulture prompted the action which saw several ladies of
Roscoe organize a local Garden Club. March 29, 1955 twelve ladies met in the
home of Mrs. Erma Auchampach for that purpose. Mrs. Effie Kindred of Miller,
S.D. was present at this meeting and assisted with the orgainization and
program planning. Eleven of the 12 became active members at this meeting and
decided to join the South Dakota Federation of Garden Clubs which gives then
added privileges that non-federated clubs do not have. The group of ladies who
started the Garden Club were Betty Schad, Berneice Margeson, Tilly Job, Ruth
Lammle, Mathilda Kirschenmann, Angie Lammle, Caroline Lammle, Erma Auchampach,
Marion Haug, Adeline Bader, and Ann Prisbe.

        Back row: Jeanne Schneider, Bernice Beck, Pam Garner, Phyllis
        Guthmiller, Rae Wiedrich, Bernita Flannery and Bonnie Roesch. Front
        row: Sharon Grau, Irene Schnaible, Lydia Trefz, Leora Prisbe, Berneice
        Margeson, Marjorie Dabbert and Beth Fischer.

The first regular meeting was held April 19, 1955. The name Ivy Circle Garden
Club was legally adopted and the project of the club became the City Park. The
park was started in 1930, a W.P.A. project and in the following years was
greatly neglected.  The park was cleaned, and a flower bed in the center with a
cement bird bath and flag pole was installed.  The flag is raised every day
during June, July and August.  Three outdoor grills and a water fountain were
added and signs depicting the City Park were placed on the outskirts of town.
Playground equipment, picnic tables, park benches, and a picnic shelter were
added in cooperation with business people, the City of Roscoe and other
citizens.  In 1980 many of the beautiful elm trees developed Dutch Elm Disease
and many trees had to be cut down, to be replaced by new trees and picnic
shelters.

The Garden Club motto is "We pass this way but once. Let us beautify the way as
we go so that the world may know which way we went."

Hopefully the Garden Club has done just that.

Plans for the Centennial are under way and the Garden Club will cooperate in
making Roscoe a city to be proud of with an abundance of flowers in the park
and elsewhere.

The present members ore Bernice Beck, Marjorie Dabbert, Beth Fischer, Bernita
Flannery, Pam Garner, Sharon Grau, Phyliss Guthmiller, Berneice Margeson, Leora
Prisbe, Bonnie Roesch, Irene Schnaible, Jeanne Schneider, Lydia Trefz and Rae
Wiedrich.

Since the organization in 1955 there have been many changes in membership with
Berneice Margeson being the only charter member.

Hopefully there will always be a garden club in Roscoe, one of the
organizations interested in continued beautification of Roscoe.

MORNINGSIDE CEMETERY

        Morningside Cemetery

The Roscoe Cemetery, a corporation, filed a plot known as the Morningside
Cemetery on January 3, 1888.  Records from that dote until November 13, 1913
are incomplete other than Mr. C. H. Spencer was secretary and Rev. G. A. Brant
was an ardent worker to get it started.

November 13, 1913, at 3 p.m. a group of citizens met for the purpose of
reorganizing said corporation. Constitution and by-laws were approved at this
meeting and the following officers and trustees elected.  Ward L. McCafferty,
president; J. W. Jamison, vice president; George O. Wilson, secretary; Robert
LeFebvre treasurer.  Officers elected A. H. Bomgren, William Christen, and
Charles Christen as trustees.

M. Plin Beebe, attorney-at-law, Ipswich, S.D. was hired and November 24, 1913
filed articles of corporation with the secretary of state.  April 13, 1914, Mr.
G. L. Merriman, a licensed surveyor, platted said cemetery on the northeast
quarter of section 24 in township 123 north of range 71 east of the fifth P.M.
in Edmunds County, S.D. The plat includes 215 lots each measuring 20 feet
square with room for 10 graves per lot and are numbered clockwise starting at
the northeast corner of each lot.  Ten foot alleys run each way between the lots
and include five park areas.  Greenwood Avenue is a 30 foot wide street running
from east to west and Park Avenue runs from south to north.

In the southwest corner of the cemetery is an area 50' x 110' called Potters
Field and is used for graves not having sufficient funds to pay the small
charge for a lot and in more earlier times for unidentified bodies.

At a meeting held December 21, 1914, it was decided to let the Catholic Church
use the southeast portion of the cemetery.  A motion by Robert LeFebvre and
seconded by J. W. Jamieson that every lot be paid for when it is staked out and
(before) any graves are dug on it.  Officers in 1958 included Wm.  Christen,
president; Paul Elford, vice president; and Harvey Haug, secretary    and
treasurer.   Following     the resignation of Harvey Haug as secretary and
treasurer, Mr. Haug's Job was taken over by Elmer R. Mayer, a job which carries
with it the staking out of the graves as needed.

In the winter when the cemetery is filled with snow and the ground is frozen,
it is a very difficult thing to find the different spike markers of the lots,
so it was proposed to place four steel pipes or markers in each of the four
quarters, from which tapes can be pulled to find any of the spikes looked for,
these pipes being put in place in May of 1962.

Another much needed change was made also in May of 1962.  Previously the least
anyone could purchase, as to graves, was one-fourth lot or two and one-half
graves, and this has over the years resulted in numerous unused graves.  So it
was changed to where graves can be sold in any number as needed.  In 1963 a
perpetual upkeep program was set up whereby persons could pay up their
perpetual keep, thereby eliminating the trouble of annual dues, and also it was
adopted that when graves ore sold, the perpetual upkeep fee is also collected
at the some time.

The Roscoe Cemetery was incorporated January 3, 1888 with a capitol stock of
$5000.00. The law requirement is that each corporation with capitol stock is to
file annual reports each year, and involves a fee.

Therefore on the 29th day of April, 1969 the Roscoe Cemetery Corporation was
changed to a nonprofit corporation, as there are no paid employees, with the
exception of the hired labor to keep up the cemetery.

Another custom was adopted whereby there is an annual cleanup day, which is a
few days before Memorial Day of each year.  It is always hoped that all of the
people living in the area will come out this once a year and help to give the
cemetery a very good cleaning for Memorial Day.

It is hoped that we can keep the graves seeded to gross, so it can be kept
mowed and a neat appearance can be kept.  Present officers are President Harold
Sahli. board members Ervin Pitzer, Edwin Dabbert, Melvin Roesch, Herbert Geist
and Leo Himmerich, secretary and treasurer, Elmer R. Mayer.

        Melvin Roesch, Leo Himmerich, Harold Sahli, president and Elmer Mayer,
        secretary-treasurer. Front row: Edwin Dabbert, Erwin Spitzer and
        Herbert Geist.

PRISCILLA LIBRARY CLUB

The Priscilla Club was started about 1910. There were not as many coffee
parties in those years as there are now, but there were four young women who
liked to get together and sew, visit and then have coffee.  They organized a
sewing club.  The women were Mrs. George Wilson, Mrs. Lovell Johnson, Mrs. Ward
McCafferty and the Presbyterian pastor's wife, Mrs. Carl Kearns, and as time
went on more women were asked to join.  The name was changed to the Priscilla
Embroidery Club and the membership was set at 25 members.

        Roscoe Public Library

During the years, many members came and went, at times some members tried to
change the club to a study club, reading club, Home Extension club or something
with a purpose other than sewing and visiting but it didn't "take".  Finally it
was decided that if the members had to have a goal in life, a public library
would be something worthwhile and gave us a goal to work toward.  The library
was started by each member giving books, this brought in about 50 volumes.  A
book cupboard was made, which is still in use today.  The library was first
housed in a corner of the fire house.  It was advertised and become popular at
once.

In those first years, books were borrowed from the State Library at Pierre,
S.D., which were kept for a certain length of time free, plus the freight.  Many
things were done to raise money.  Business places were solicited and financial
help and encouragement was received.  The library grew out of the little
cupboard and it was necessary to find a bigger place.  Someone made the wild
suggestion that a library be built.  The idea was kicked around for months but
finally members got to work.  Some of the club records were lost and among
those are the minutes of the year the library was actually built.  Mrs. Ralph
Flick and Mrs. Paul Elford were secretary and president during the building.
The lots where the library stands were owned by five men.  Mrs. Ralph Flick
contacted these men and told them what the club was trying to do and every one
of them gave their interest in the lots, except one, and he was paid $5.

The members husbands did most of the building.  The women took trucks and
trailers and hauled rocks for the foundation and chimney.  In 1932 the books
were moved by the members and were cataloged, numbered and stocked in
alphabetical order on the new shelves. Many books have been given to the
Library by the Roscoe people.  There are new books purchased each year by the
book committee.  The Library is now open one day a week, with members taking
turns being librarian.

The emblem of the club, a silver thimble, used to be presented to each member
departing the club.  No longer is this done as they are no longer a sewing club
but a book club.  The present membership is eight members and meetings are held
every two weeks, dues are 25 cents.

The holding of offices is rotated every six months. Among the social highlights
of the club is going to Aberdeen or some "surprise" place twice a year for
dinner and theatre.  These original ideas are always fun.  Mystery sisters keep
each member in suspense and suspicious the year round.  They reveal their names
at Christmas.

This is a little glimpse of the history of the Priscilla Club and it is hoped
that this history will serve in a humble way to preserve the memories of the
club.

        Marilyn Bieber, Marvelyn Roesch, Mable Winkler, Hilda Schurr, Irene
        Roesch, Mary Bieber, Hertha Mayer and Inez Haerter.

ROSCOE COMMERCIAL CLUB

The Roscoe Commercial Club was organized in August 1937 with W. C. Peterson
being the organizing chairman. Official officers being Chairman, George Wilson;
V-Chairman, W. C. Peterson; Secretary-Treasurer, W. Lammle; Executive
Committee, Otto Noehre, Martin Adam, Christ Schumacher, Paul Elford and Clarence
Mills.

Water works was the topic of the first meeting, the buying of a water tank from
Eureka.  Chamber recommended five man city board, mileage voted was two cents
per mile for auto use.

1946-Petitions circulated to get approval for a city water and sewage system.

1948-Community fire truck being purchased. Committee Chairman, Paul Mardian,
Minor Larson, Dr. Schad; cost of truck less extras, $6010.  REA building coming
into the picture at that time.

1952-Curb and gutter work for the blacktopping of main street at this time was
petitioned.  Snow-bound operation took place east of Roscoe, rescuing
basketball players and travelers, George Hammond using bulldozer and Swanson
Bus Lines furnished a bus. First annual F.E.M. meeting being held in Roscoe.
Sponsored an award night honoring Mr. Adolph Finkbeiner and Walter Schipke on
their well kept shelterbelts.

1953-Planned an addition to the school, took a school tour as far east as
Milbank.  Boys being sent to the outstanding young farmers banquet in
cooperation with Peter Krokosh, ag. teacher; Mervin Hauck, Dennis Kotschevar,
Morris Ernst, Melvin Borkirchert, and Leonard Goetz.

1955-Sponsored Jr. and Jr. Jr. Baseball, swimming tours and softball in
cooperation with City, Legion and Fire Deportment, first annual Good Neighbor
Day celebration and the first annual F.E.M. Beauty Contest was held.  Welcoming
committee welcomed the first highway Post Office.  South telephone lines being
organized.  Park being made into a picnic area and signs being put up.

1956-New curtain was put in in City Auditorium, sewing being done by the
Chamber of Commerce ladies.

1957-The first planning began on Roscoe's 75th Anniversary Celebration.  Gave
financial assistance to A.S.C. boys that are working on soil conserving
practices.  Bleachers and new chairs being purchased for city auditorium, labor
and some financial help given, to give increased seating capacity.

1958-Appointed first committee for the 75th Anniversary Celebration.

1964-Local businesses participate in Bank night from April-October.  Bowling
team organized for local lanes.

1965-F.E.M. Annual Meeting in Roscoe.  Bill Lammle honored after 30 years as
weather observer.

1966-An appreciation supper was held for Indee Basketball team.

1967-Helped get easements for road south of town with financial support needed.

1968-A special committee involving the community to get vacant lots and
property cleaned up.

1969-Soil Conservation Banquet was held in Roscoe November 20, gave 3-12 lb.
turkeys as door prize.

1970-CC was involved in operating a cafe for a time and also had a teen age
recreation center at the City Hall.  Furnished refreshments for Edmunds County
4-H Recognition Night.  Helped get easements for road north of town with some
financial support.

1971-F.E.M. Annual Meeting held in Roscoe in June.

1972-Furnished name togs for School Alumni Reunion in June.

1973-Ladies were included in CC membership.  Voted to pay a percentage of
Christmas lights for the city with committee appointed.

1974-First Low Rental Housing Unit approved.

1975-Various standing committees were established. Supported FHA Regional
meeting at the school March 15 with various booths set up by local people
demonstrating old time skills such as quilting, wine making, rug weaving, etc.
Sponsored a carnival, teen and adult dance for Good Neighbor Days in conjunction
with the County 4-H celebration.  Irene Borkirchert was named Bi-Centennial
chairperson and she received recognition for efforts in making Roscoe "Horizon
City" for the Nation's Bi-Centennial.  This obligated the CC to preserve some
of their "post" for future generations. Activities included in this were
Pioneer Days June 18 and 19, restore old Legion Hall for museum, park
improvement, athletic field lights, bleachers for football field and cannon and
flog for Legion.  Funds were obtained for tennis court to be matched by the city
and the school.  Second four-unit Senior low rental housing apartments were
approved.

        Dale Roesch, Dale Hettick, Dorothy Ernst 1982 Officers

1976-F.E.M. Meeting in Roscoe June 2. Bi-Centennial Celebration and Pioneer
Days June 18-19.  White elephant, rummage and bake sale to raise funds.
Installed comfort stations at the city park and athletic field lights.
Restored Museum building.  Instrumental in installing flog pole and Howitzer at
Legion building. First four-unit Senior low rental housing apartments completed
and filled.

1977-Sponsored Fall Festival along with school homecoming.  Second four-unit
Senior low rental housing apartments completed and filled.

1978-Tennis courts were completed.  A futile attempt was made to get a nursing
home in Roscoe.  This was a long committee project over a couple of years.
Purchased material and mode a new queen's robe for school and Snow Queen use.

1979-interest was generated for the building of a new Cafe and Lounge with
ground breaking March 24, and opening in August.  This disbanded the City
Liquor Store. Began plans for Roscoe's Centennial celebration for July 1,2, & 3
of 1983.  The United Fund was organized for the city of Roscoe.

1980-Plans continue for Centennial with fund raisers and committees working.

1981-Centennial Cookbooks published and put on sale.  Beautification committee
disposed of diseased trees at park and other places around town. Watermelon
feed and prize drawings during homecoming activities.

1982-Various annual activities continue which include: welcome wagon
certificates to newcomers in the community, clean up projects for community
betterment, encouragement of new businesses or industries in the community,
sponsor local Snow Queen Contest annually, support the Summer Youth Program,
provide Circus tickets for grades 3-5-7 each year, help FHA with annual
Halloween party for children and share expense of Christmas movie and Santa Day
treats with the school and Fire Department.  Officers for '82 are Dale Hettick,
president; Dale Roesch, vice president and Dorothy Ernst, secretary-treasurer.

ROSCOE GOLF ASSOCIATION

        Bruce Sahli, secretary-treasurer; John Spitzer, president.

In January of 1960, the Roscoe American Legion bought about 40 acres of land
from Fred Sauer.  This land is located in the southeastern part of Roscoe.  The
Legion building sets on part of it and the rest is located to the south of the
building.  Martin Scherf donated 14 acres of land to the Legion to the south of
the Sauer acreage which included a tree lot and gross land to the east of the
tree lot.  This land was used to construct a nine hole golf course with sand
greens.

A creek winds through the course from the northwest corner toward the
southeast.  During construction of the course the creek was dug deeper and
widened with earthmovers.  A bridge was built across the creek by Leo Himmerich
so golfers could cross over to play holes eight and nine.

The Roscoe Golf Ass'n was formed June 2, 1960. Throughout the years since the
club's existence, membership has fluctuated from 10-30 members.

A used three gang mower was purchased from Leola which lasted one year.  A used
"rough" mower was purchased from Dakota Turf, Sioux Falls, which was later
traded in for a new three gang Toro that is in use today. Later a five gang
Worthington was purchased from Dakota Turf which is also in use at present.

For about five years, a tractor was borrowed to pull the gangs until a small
tractor was purchased.

The first Golf Tournament was held in the summer of 1962.  It was an 18 hole
tournament with Bob Koenig of Onida taking first in Championship flight and
Walter Lammle of Roscoe taking second.  A tournament has been held every summer
since then, except in 1979.

Maintenance of the course has always been volunteer help by the members.

The first officers were Joe Prisbe, president; Peter Krokosh, secretary; and
Harold Margeson, treasurer. The present officers are John Spitzer, president;
and Bruce Sahli, secretary-treasurer.

ROSCOE ROD AND GUN CLUB

        Bruce Sahli and Harold Margeson, trustees; Dale Hettick,
        secretary-treasurer and Gottfried Adam, president.

The Roscoe Rod and Gun Club was organized March 21, 1957 with 42 members.
Ervin Spitzer was the first president.

The club was affiliated with the South Dakota Wildlife Federation.

One of the earliest projects was construction of a trap house which was located
one mile west of Roscoe on Mrs. Valberg Scherf's land.

One of the club's projects was teaching the Hunters Safety Course to the Junior
Hunters to be.

The club incorporated December 18, 1958 and the Articles of Incorporation were
made and entered into on August 13, 1959.

In the year of 1961, the club moved to its present site on the east side of the
American Legion building where two trap houses were constructed.

Present officers are: Gottfried H. Adam, Jr., president; Robert Swalve,
vice-president; Dale Hettick, secretary- treasurer; and Ricky Houck, Harold
Margeson, Gary Adam and Bruce Sahli, trustees.

ROSCOE SENIOR CITIZENS

        Roscoe Senior Center

A decision to organize the Senior Citizens of Roscoe was made at an open
meeting.  All interested persons were invited.  Mrs. William Lamont,
chairperson of the Governor's Advisory Council on Aging was the speaker.
Following a discussion to start a senior club, the group voted to meet in the
evening of August 16, 1970.

The first meetings were held in the city hall which was centrally located with
kitchen facilities so it was ideally suited as a Senior Citizens Club.

Plans for the initial meeting were made by Mrs. Robert Schurr, Mrs. Helen
Downer, Mrs. Elmer Mayer and Mrs. Emma Nehlich.  Taking part in the
organizational meetings were Reuben Kirschenmann, Elmer Mayer, Bert Ham, Mr.
and Mrs. John Fischer, Mr. and Mrs. Jake Keller, Mrs. Katie Lehr, Mrs. Florence
Mahon, Mr. and Mrs. Al Johnson, Mr. and Mrs. Fred Guthmiller, Mrs. Enoch Roth
and Mrs. Helen Downer.

        Roscoe Senior Citizens

        Hilda Schurr, Adeline Krebs, Bernice Jeschke and Alvina Fischer 1982
        Officers

The Roscoe Senior Club was the first organized group in Edmunds County.  The
first officers were: President, Mrs. Robert Schurr; Vice President, Mrs.
Florence Mahon; Secretary and Treasurer, Mrs. Berton Ham. Over the years many
members served as officers at different times.

The organized name decided on was the Roscoe Community Center of Roscoe.  It
was decided a $1 membership fee would be charged each person for one year, and
to meet every two weeks for the time being. This was changed to once a month
and finally twice a week, which is still in effect at this time.

The first members donated dishes and many other things needed.  A program was
set up by the Federal Government to furnish a noon meal and transportation at a
reduced price, all seniors were contacted.

A meeting was held and it was decided to apply for grant money, which was
approved.  The money was used to buy equipment, such as a pool table, tables,
chairs, dishes, silverware, stove and a record player.

The county set up an advisory board to receive the grant money.  Each center
was to have three members. Appointed from the center were Reuben Kirschenmann,
Harold Ellwein and Al Johnson.

        Back row: Bertha Schipke, Edna Mille, Kato Beck, Mathilda Kirschenmann
        and Clara Uhrich. Front row: Lydia Schmitt, Mary Keller, Dorothy
        Christopherson and Olga Buechler.

The group was always looking for some building as a permanent meeting place.  A
committee was appointed to meet with Art Scherf in regard to purchasing the
addition to Scherf's Store to be used as a Senior Center. The committee
reported back and after much discussion it was decided to buy the building from
Art Scherf.  With the limited amount of money every able bodied person was
asked to help fix and clean up the place.  After much work and many laughs they
were ready to move into the present center.

        Belle Kilber, Ida Ernst, Betty Lammle, Pauline Geist, Marvin and
        Dorothy Christopherson; inset, Christina Fischer.

The Grand Opening was September 12, 1974. Invitations were sent to centers in
Edmunds and McPherson Counties.  Bake sale, card parties, crafts and dances
were held at this time to raise money.

Now that they were permanently situated, it was decided to have the monthly
business meeting the first Friday of each month and to have the blood pressures
taken at this time.  The center is lucky to have Mrs. Elroy Goetz to do this
volunteer work for them.  The center is open Tuesdays and Fridays of each week,
and the seniors come and enjoy themselves.

In June of 1976, Roscoe celebrated the Bicentennial. The Seniors took part in
this by dressing up in finery of the by-gone days.  A float was made for the
parade and the group had a chorus and a square dance routine that entertained
in the evening Grand Stand Performance.

        John and Irene Nechlich, Harold and Agnes Ellwein, Theophil and Emma
        Winkler, Oscar and Adeline Krebs and Fred and Irene Guthmiller.

John Scholfield, who lived in the Roscoe area many years ago, always remembered
his friends here and sent money at different times to the Senior Center.  Mr.
Scholfield passed away in the Philippines in 1979.

There have been many improvements made on the building over the years.  After
the big cleaning job, the walls were paneled and carpet was put down.  In 1980
the Wiedrich Construction renovated the north outside wall of the building and
in 1982 new sidewalks were put in.  The last coat of sealer was put on the north
outside wall.  After much hard work they have a good looking building and thank
everyone who took part in the work.  It adds a lot to the street corner.

New members are always welcome and they hope it will be a place that future
Senior Citizens will enjoy.

        Harold Ellwein, Theophil Winkler, Oscar Krebs and Fred Guthmiller.

ROSCOE SPORTSMAN'S CLUB

The Roscoe Sportsman's Club began in March of 1980. The club was organized to
help better the community as well as to provide entertainment by holding fund
raising events.  The 1982 officers are Mark Meier, president; LeRoy Voegele,
vice president, and Ron Rohrbach, secretary-treasurer.

In the past the club has sponsored a rabbit drive, a dance for Good Neighbor
Days and a Smoker.  Money was donated for such events as to the school for the
Halloween party, to the Snow Queen contest and to help obtain the film, "Focus
on the Family" which was open to the public.

        Ron Rohrbach, Mark Meier and LeRoy Voegele 1982 Officers

A Demolition Derby and a Smoker were held to raise funds for the restroom
facilities which were built by the club members at the Roscoe Sports Complex.

An adult recreation night is held at the school Thursday evenings during the
winter months.

A fishing derby was held and prizes awarded to help raise money for the
projects which are yet to come.

With the support of the Roscoe community, the club will be able to continue
various activities and events for all.

        Col.  Louis Shirley, wife Edna and daughter Judy

        Adam and Julia Schipke Hofer
