                ROSCOE MEMORIES

THE LAST INDIAN SCARE
as reported in the Roscoe Herald Nov. 13, 1890

The telegraphic columns of the daily papers have been filled for several days
with reports of a threatened uprising among the Indians on the reservations
west of the river.  The latest advices are strongly contradicting, but as
nearly as we can decide from reports, the ruling price for scalps ought to be
about par.

Nov. 20, 1890

Editor Tracy of the Democrat, mode a trip to Eureka on Tuesday's train,
presumably to ascertain from the most reliable sources how soon the Indians
could be expected to swoop down on the county seat.

G.W. Carpenter, Quartermaster, General for the state of South Dakota, was in
town over night Monday night.  He had with him a supply of arms and ammunition
which had been ordered to Bowdle and Eureka to allay the fears of the people.

If it isn't too late we would like to suggest to those terrorized citizens of
Ipswich that the court house would make an excellent block-house while the
massacre is in progress.  Those steel cells in the basement would afford ample
protection.

The Women's Relief Corps at this place was inspected Saturday in an official
manner by Mrs. Houser of Aberdeen, who came out for that purpose.  The
inspection of the Corps at this time was not due to the impending Indian war.

Among the many messages occasioned by the Indian disturbance within the last
few days was one that went over the wires dictated by a refugee who justified
his position by saying: "At any rate, there is more satisfaction in being a
live coward than a dead hero."

The family of R.T. Trunkey went east Tuesday night to visit with his friends
during the winter.  Mrs. Trunkey's aversion to being massacred is so great that
she believes a change of scene will be conducive to longevity.  Rankin T. will
cook and wash dishes up at the ranch.

Certain persons took advantage of nightfall and the Indian fright a few nights
ago to personate a party of redskins on the war-path.  Had their pretensions
been taken in good faith and a few charges of shot been fired into them there
wouldn't have been nearly so much fun in the affair as they anticipated.

Lieutenant-Governor-elect Hoffman was a passenger to Aberdeen Monday night,
returning Tuesday.  He stated that the stampede of people in the river counties
had been uncontrollable, notwithstanding the assurances of those who had made
an investigation and found that there were no Indians east of the river, and
therefore no immediate cause for alarm.

Nov. 20, 1890

When the Cruel War is Over

The Indian war that has been fought on paper and in the minds of the people for
the last two weeks is not yet at an end.  The governor has issued a
proclamation, it is true, telling the settlers that they are safer now than at
any time since their settlement on the frontier, for the reason that the
Indians are being closely watched, and troops are occupying every strategic
point.  Yet the reports from the reservations are contradictory, enough so to
keep a portion of the people in a state of suspense.

One report has it that the Indians are committing depredations and killing
stock.  Another is that the Indians are as badly frightened as are the white
people, fearing the loss of their rations.  One thing is noticeable. These
Indian stories are like the blizzard stories of three winters ago-the farther
away from home they get the more prodigious they become.

People between here and the river have been frightened half out of their senses
and fled, while the people living directly on the bank of the river, adjoining
the reservation and in close proximity to the Indians, saw no cause for flight
and remained at home.

One thing is certain-certain as taxation.  The people in this community have no
reason to lie awake nights clinging to their scalps.  With the exception of
Sitting Bull the Indians on the reservation west of us are peaceably disposed,
and everything is quiet.  If any trouble should occur it will be in the
vicinity of the Bad Lands in the southwestern part of the state.

Governor Mellette's advice can safely be followed: "Assurance is not given to
settlers without a full information of the facts, and they con be assured that
every precaution has been taken and will continue to be exercised for their
safety in case of trouble, which is no longer anticipated.  Settlers should pay
no attention to idle rumors and wild newspaper reports manufactured to order,
but preserve their reason and try to restore to reason those who seem trying to
lose it."

Nov. 27, 1890

The most concise and comprehensive portrayal that we have seen of the Indian
situation is a small comic illustration in the Minneapolis Journal of Tuesday,
entitled "The Situation on the Frontier." It represents a wild Indian on the
plains with tomahawk at his side, clad only in war-point and feathers, and
dancing wildly with all the fervor of a fanatic.  Uncle Sam sitting close by
with a stock of guns at his side, eyes the excited savage intently, and is
represented as saying: "Go ahead, old Snakes-in-his-Boots, and dance yourself to
death if you want to.  But if you make any breaks at me, I'm ready for you."

If you con find nothing else to be thankful for this Thanksgiving, you can be
thankful that you have not been scalped or massacred.

Dec. 11, 1890

Nearly all classes of people have "kicked" on the results of the recent census.
Even the Indians are no exception.  One of the alleged reasons for the Indian
trouble is that the census returns shows a decrease in the number of Indians,
and the rations were therefore correspondingly decreased.  The Indians found
that 1200 of them would have to live all winter on $2000. The enumerator
complained that the Indians refused to be counted and in some instances he had
to do some guessing.

1890 INDEPENDENCE DAY

CELEBRATION AFTER SOUTH DAKOTA' STATEHOOD
as reported in the Roscoe Herald June 27, 1890

The Glorious Fourth

Soon will recur the annual occasion wherein the great American eagle is wont to
soar to dizzy heights, when the spread-eagle oratory from the lips of a thousand
silver-tongued declaimers makes the air resonant with patriotic symphonies,
when patriotism shall be stirred to its greatest depths in sixty-five million
breasts (or whatever number the census returns may show), when the happy swain
basks in the benign society and effulgent smiles of his best girl, and when the
irrepressible small boy revels in an immunity from censure  as he spreads
confusion, terror and firecrackers without let or hindrance--the Glorious
Fourth.

Since the year of Roscoe's greatest growth-1886- the denizens of this burg have
chosen to husband their patriotism and spare cash or to lend them to further the
festivities of   neighboring     communities      in    the celebration of the
nation's birthday; but in this year of grace eighteen hundred and ninety and of
our independence the one hundred and fourteenth Roscoe proposes to celebrate in
a becoming manner. Movements are on foot to corral and press into service
whatever of talent and ability may be necessary to make the enterprise a
success.  Two classes of workers will be needed-the one to make a noise-with
oratory, music and gunpowder, the other to quietly saw wood and raise the steam
which shall propel the machinery of the celebration in a satisfactory manner.

A meeting was held Monday evening, at which initiatory steps were taken and
committees appointed, to be followed by another meeting next Monday night. The
committees are these: subscriptions, A. B. Morris and J. W. Jamieson;
arrangements, M. T. Lightner, Geo. W. Akins, W. G. Bickelhaupt, F. M. Hopkins,
S. V. Arnold, F. J. Poole, Mrs. Gardiner, Mrs. Morris, Mrs. Akins, Mrs. E.
Lewis, and Mrs. Proctor; printing, Mr. Lightner and Mr. Gardiner; music, Chris.
Warden, Will Morris, Joel Proctor, Mrs. Cram and Miss Lou Wilson; fireworks,
Geo.  Akins, Al.  Morris, Geo.  Wilson, Capt. Arnold and Elmer Lewis.

July 3, 1890

The Celebration

Preparations for the Fourth of July celebration in Roscoe are going steadily
onward and a pleasant and profitable time can confidently be assured to those
who attend.  In the way of oratory Rev. A. S. Gardiner may be depended upon to
deliver the most patriotic and stirring address ever delivered in Edmunds
county.  It is also expected that Sheriff Faris of Ipswich will take part in
the exercises.  The other attendant features of the celebration, such as music,
games, etc., are being looked after by the proper committees.  A very
creditable program has been arranged and every part will be carried out in
detail.  Roscoe extends an invitation to all her friends to come and celebrate
with her citizens the first anniversary of American independence ever held in
the state of South Dakota.

July 10, 1890

Independence Anniversary

The one hundred and fourteenth anniversary of the birth of American
independence was celebrated in Roscoe in accord with previous announcements.
The weather was on the whole favorable, and the attendance was all that had
been anticipated.  Many gathered from the surrounding country, and some came in
on the train from the east.

The order of exercises was made to conform as nearly as possible to the
advertised program.  The opening exercises consisted of music by the orchestra,
and scripture reading and prayer.  Then a group of thirteen young ladies,
dressed to represent the thirteen original states, ascended the platform and
rendered that patriotic song, "The Red, White and Blue," and just as the last
verse was finished a smaller girl joined their number dressed to represent
South Dakota and bearing a banner on which was inscribed, "South Dakota-For
Prohibition and Equal Suffrage." On the reverse side were fastened samples of
growing grain.  The bearer of the banner, to the tune of "Red, White and Blue,"
song the following, the thirteen joining in the chorus:

We wave one more flag for Dakota,
The last, not the least, of the train.
Here Freedom has gained her chief triumph,
Prohibition from mountain to plain.

Chorus, "Then three cheers for the Red, White and
Blue," etc.

Equal suffrage we hope to establish.
When November rolls 'round once again;-
then three cheers for the pride of the prairie.
South Dakota, land of hay, corn and grain.

Chorus.

The Declaration of Independence was next read by J. W. Parmley of Ipswich,
after which followed a song by the school children, "The Discovery of American
by Christopher Columbus," and music by the orchestra. The orator of the day,
Rev. A. S. Gardiner, then delivered a most excellent address, a summary of
which we give to our readers this week.

In the afternoon, when the calls of hunger had been appeased, games and races
claimed the attention of the crowd till nightfall, and in the evening a
pyrotechnic display and a dance completed the day's entertainment.

GOLDEN JUBILEE

The year 1933 saw the citizens of the Roscoe community gathering on June 17 to
commemorate the first 50 years.  The July 3, 1933 city records state that a bill
was paid to Scherf Bros. for $2.68 for 100 lbs. flour--the prize given to the
largest family attending the Roscoe Golden Jubilee!  Following is an account of
the activities taken from the June 23, 1933 issue of the Roscoe Independent:

ANNIVERSARY IS BIG SUCCESS AS CITY CELEBRATES

Parade With Old Time Costumes and Floats Best Ever Seen In City

Crowds Throng Roscoe In Day of Activities

Many Come Long Distance To Enjoy Program, Shows, Concert and Dance

A celebration which attracted a gathering unexcelled in number at any time in
recent years marked the passing of Roscoe's fiftieth anniversary here last
Saturday.  People from all parts of the county attended and took part in the
affair which was successful in every way.  Weather during the day was ideal.

An opening band concert fittingly proceeded the opening of the day's program,
which began with a parade, very well planned and arranged and cleverly
depicting scenes of pioneer days of S.D. along with several other types of very
well prepared floats.  The parade was nearly a half mile in length.  The
interest demonstrated on the parts of townsmen as well as farmers of the
vicinity in community affairs may very favorably set forth in this event.

Occupying the remainder of the morning several speakers, prominent men in state
and county affairs, delivered interesting talks on topics chiefly depicting
early life in the Dakotas and the struggle and efforts set forth by the
pioneers in beginning the upbuilding of this country.  Speakers were Thos.
Ronayne, state director of inspections, Judge Bottum, circuit judge of this
district, J. W. Parmley, prominent S.D. man and one of the early settlers here;
A. W. Watwood of Aberdeen, candidate for representative at the last primaries.
Both young and old participated in and enjoyed a very complete arrangement of
various races.  The extremely warm weather during that part of the day dampened
the little ardor of the enthusiastic contestants.

A baseball game, Loyalton vs. Roscoe, on the local field, attended by everyone
completed the afternoon festivities.  The score was 14-5 in favor of Loyalton.
Dancing and other forms of amusements occupied the evening.

Seemingly reluctant of deporting from the scene of such ardent and patriotic
celebrating, the crowds departed to meditate on the significance of an event so
evidently well planned and diligently worked for by citizens who are interested
in maintaining the spirit that was necessary and unwanting in earlier times
during the building and development of our fair city.

        Taking part in the Parade were: Ernest Williams, Viola Thielen
        Tschirley, Inez Christen Haerter, Hayden Williams, Wilbur Williams,
        Lucille Tschirley Silverman and Esther Christen Bock at the organ.

THREE FIRST SETTLERS ARE AT ROSCOE FOR ANNIVERSARY FETE

At the Roscoe semi-centennial celebration last Saturday, as far as we have been
able to ascertain, there were only three of the 1883 settlers present.  They
were J. W. Parmley, a settler in April 1883, now of Ipswich; Julia Schipke
Hofer and Elias W. Hinsdale, both of May or June 1883.  Mrs. Hofer was the
daughter of Charles Schipke, and was eight years old when her parents located a
mile east of Roscoe.  Elias Hinsdale, usually called "Shorty" Hinsdale now
lives at Eureka. His claim was south of Roscoe a mile.  J. W. Parmley's
pre-emption was port of the townsite of Roscoe, that south of the Yellowstone
Trail, and east of main street. At the time settlement was made the land was not
surveyed except into squares of twenty four miles, one side of which was what
is now Roscoe's main street.

        Part of the Golden Jubilee Parade.

ROSCOE IS OLDEST TOWN NOW IN EXISTENCE IN COUNTY

At the homecoming at Roscoe last Saturday the question naturally came up as to
which was the oldest town--Roscoe or Ipswich.  The oldest towns of the county
are no more.  They were LaBelle at the Snake Creek crossing, which gave way to
Mina, a mile south: Georgetown, Freeport and Edmunds, forming a triangle to the
center of which the railroad extended from Aberdeen and founded Ipswich,
October 1883.  Roscoe was founded in April 1883 by Charles P. Morgan and J. W.
Parmley and named after Roscoe Conkling, former senator from New York, a
relative of Mr. Morgans'.

DIAMOND JUBILEE

        Street dancing on Booster trip

The next major festival in Roscoes' history occurred June 16-17, 1958, when
Roscoe celebrated their Diamond Jubilee.  Seventy-five years of progress--from
its humble beginning in a "tent hotel", to which the first settlers came for
food and shelter, through those early years which saw many businesses came into
existence and flourish, the wooden walkways, homes being built (some of which
still stand today) and with the coming of the railroad, the addition of a
school and several churches, Roscoe become a community of prominence and
importance to the then growing population of the area.  According to the
January 11, 1900 Edmunds County Democrat, a newspaper of that era; "Quite a few
new Russian settlers just over from Russia are settling on the cancelled
entries south and west of Roscoe.  The immigration into the county during 1900
will be the largest since the early eighties." The years following to 1958 saw
many major changes.  The coming of electricity, hot and cold running water, made
it possible for us to have the many modern conveniences that now existed
throughout the world!  The automobile had almost completely replaced the horse
and wagon.  So, with the passing of the Livery Stables, the hitching posts, the
little one room shack behind every home, the modernizing of the business places
and homes, the wooden walkways replaced by cement sidewalks, the "face" of
Roscoe had indeed changed since 1883!  Yes, we had much to be thankful for and
much cause to celebrate in 1958.  The entire community united their efforts
toward that goal and celebrate, we did! ! !

        Roscoe Dutch Band entertaining on Booster trip.  Dennis Stockert,
        Delbert Sonnenfeld, Randy Huber and Gottfried Adam.

        Beauty Shop Six singing on Booster trip.  Norma Binfet, Gloria Ellis,
        Sheila Schneider, Carol Trefz and Donna Kilber. (Adelida Peterson
        accompanist, not pictured.)

        Leading the parade are John Roesch, oldest veteran and Mayor George
        Hammond followed by Roscoe Legion Colorguard.


        Chet P. Schneider, Jubilee General Chairman.

The following excerpts from reports on the festivities appeared in the June 25,
1958, Roscoe Independent:

The biggest crowd that this city ever saw was here for the Diamond Jubilee
celebration Monday and Tuesday. An estimated 4,000 were present for the
activities. Many of the visitors were from out of state who came here to renew
old acquaintances and to visit their old home town.  Registration of pioneers
began Monday morning.  The sod house located south of the Gamble Store was the
registration headquarters and the very appropriate idea of John Spitzer,
chairman of this committee.  The sod house which was connected to a fishing
shack was crowded most of the two days of the Jubilee with pioneers who came to
register and to view the construction of the shanty.  The total registration for
the two days was 1,007 persons with special ribbons given to all those who
qualified, having lived here any time beginning with 1883 through 1928.

        Royalty, Miss Roscoe, Gloria Laib and Miss South Dakota, Pat Miller of
        Mobridge.

        Horsedrawn gas wagon, P. A. Schneider.

"The best parade in the history of Roscoe" and like comments could be and still
are heard pertaining to the parades June 16 and 17.  The hour and a half long
parade was witnessed by thousands of people lined along the procession's route.
Not only were there serious, solemn and humorous entries but there were
historical, animal, kiddies, beautiful girls, commercial floats and three
bands--Hosmer, Hoven and Roscoe. There were 193 entries.  During both days the
parade was fortunate to have wonderful weather, although some of the jubilee
activities were visited by intermittent showers.  After the parade Monday,
everyone went to the City Park for a free barbecue.

        Oxen drawn covered wagon Ed Roesch and Ross Hales

        The Hamak Steamer

        Crowds lining Roscoe street

        Ivy Circle Garden Club    Jane Job and Peggy Margeson

        Roscoe Auto Company  Lydia, Henry and Jerome Trefz and Emil Borkirchert

        Priscilla Club    Carlo Meier and Vicki Schneider

Over 3,000 people were served a menu of pork and beans, pickles, buns and
barbecued beef.

Clark Pahlas, assistant to Col.  William G. Robinson, the State Historian, who
was unable to attend, gave the principal address.  He spoke about the progress
we have made in 75 years.  He paid tribute to the pioneers and also gave the
history of Roscoe and explained how it originated with a tent.

The Roscoe High School graduates, classes from 1922-1958, held a reunion in the
City Hall.  This was the first reunion of all classes since the first class
graduated in 1922.  Raymond Decker, Aberdeen, was chairman and introduced all
persons having had some duty in arranging this reunion.  He also introduced two
persons from the graduating class of 1923 who were present, namely, Ledo Cole
Miller of Leola, S.D. and Edmund McCafferty of Mobridge.  Marion Haug led the
group in singing school songs and Carol Mayer led the group in singing the new
version of the school song.

        Ann's Apparel

        Roscoe Grain and Feed     Sandy, Bonnie and Ginger Himmerich

        Crowds lining streets during parade

        Presbyterian Church

        "The old prospector" Edwin Bauer and his burros.

        Free barbecue at the City Park.

The special stage show headliners included the Eddie and Ruth Jester Show,
featuring Ed Cotany and Moira Martell with their magic, juggling and musical
acts.

        Sod house built by Mr. and Mrs. August Adam and Mr. and Mrs. John
        Spitzer.

        Large crowd in attendance for stage shows.

The Ashley Quartette brought many favorable comments from the oldsters in the
crowd.  Their German songs brought back many pleasant memories.  This was
followed by the crowning of the Junior King and Queen who were Donnie Ellwein
and Adele Meier of Roscoe and the Pioneer King and Queen, who were Orrin
Stanley, 85, of Portland, Ore. and Katherine Stern, 82, of Roscoe.

The home talent show consisted of old time fiddling by Charles Christen,
accompanied on the piano by Ed Schumacher, songs by the Beauty Shop Six, Melody
Peterson and Curtiss Jeschke, and entertainment by the Roscoe Dutch Band.  Miss
South Dakota, Pat Miller of Mobridge pleased the audience with two numbers,
accompanied by Mrs. Harold Peterson.  Lyle Groves of Mobridge emceed all stage
shows.  George B. German of Yankton made recorded interviews of the old
pioneers which were heard over radio station WNAX in the ensuing days.  Making
a big hit with the audience were the Don Nolte Trio of Aberdeen.  The youngsters
gave their own arrangements of popular music.  The dance committee reports a
fine turn-out for both the old and new time dances, which rounded up the days'
activities.

Thus ended two history making days in the life of the City of Roscoe!

        Acquaintances renewed    Val Scherf, Harold Sherf and Viola Thielen
        Tschirley.

        Mayor and Mrs. George Hammond

MOTHER NATURE BLASTS ROSCOE

        Roscoe's main street after a big blizzard in the winter of 1936.

Prominent among the hardships faced by the pioneers on the virgin prairies of
Dakota, were the long, bitter cold winters accompanied many times by sudden
fierce snowstorms, leaving all life forms at the mercy of Mother Nature.

        Keeping roads open to travel in Dakota winters has always proven to be
        an enormous task, as shown in these pictures taken February 18, 1932.
        Shown are horses with walking plows used to break up the snow on
        Highway 12 east of Roscoe.  Many men followed with hand shovels to
        clear it off the road. After many hours of hard labor, traffic was able
        to move.

        Ben Mulder, Christ Nehlich and Art Bauer shown on one of their trips to
        town to get supplies in the winter of 1950-51.  The base horse and sled
        was often their only means of "getting out."

        Clearing the roads in 1932.

        1952 marked another of the "really big" storms when the railroad was
        blocked and had to be dug out by manpower and the RR snow plow.  During
        the storm before the track was closed, the train was sent out east of
        Roscoe to rescue a school bus load of children who were stranded on
        their way home from a basketball game in Ipswich.

        The three day snow storm which occurred March 3-4-5, 1966 was one of
        the unusual storms in that it moved into the area slowly.  Also
        throughout the storm the temperature never dropped below 0 degrees.  The
        electricity was disrupted from Wednesday night to Saturday evening,
        leaving many homes without heat.  Neighbors with other sources of heat
        opened their homes to those without and many weathered the storm
        keeping warm with whatever means they could in their homes.  Residents
        of two homes had to be literally rescued by digging the snow away from
        their doors so they could got out.

        The home of Mr. and Mrs. Matt Forkel, Sr. was covered so that only the
        roof showed.  They were dug out by the "neighborhood rescue team" on
        Sunday morning.  March 1966.

        The tunnel was large enough to allow cars the size of Harold Sahli's
        1971 Dodge Monaco to pass through easily in 1978

        Some of the men who took part in this project were: Earl Hettick,
        Darwin Rohrbach, Adorn Schumacher, Philip Aman, Leo Himmerich, Mike
        Goetz, Elmer Mayer, Fred Himmerich and Alvin Schmitt. 1978

        The home of Supt. Henry Kosters and family also needed assistance in
        digging out after the storm. (Note: Snowbank to the right of the house
        completely covers the attached garage.) March 1966

        A scene after the storm in the Emil Borkirchert farmyard.

        The cattle in this picture next to Rudolph Schumacher's house in Roscoe
        belong to Dale Roesch, who lives five and one-half miles north of
        Roscoe. During the '78 blinding blizzard, when the cattle's eyes and
        nostrils froze shut, a B-52 Bomber (whose Radar Base Station was
        temporarily between Roscoe and Ipswich) flew its course over the Roesch
        farm and other area farms, frightening the cattle, causing them to
        stampede. The fence lines, being buried in the snow, allowed them the
        freedom to run off in various directions.

        The winter of 1977-78 proved to be unusually severe with many days of
        extreme cold temperatures and constant blowing snow.  This caused
        country roads to be closed much of the time and a record number of 26
        school days were missed.  One of the beauties of the aftermath of the
        1978 blizzard was created by several of Roscoe's citizens who had the
        spirited ambition to conquer this mountain of snow by tunneling through
        it, working with shovels, payloader and tractor scoop.  Thus, the
        connection was made between the northeast end of town and the rest of
        Roscoe.

CALVES GO TO COLLEGE

        Identical twin Hereford Heifer calves were born on the Edward Werre
        farm May 5, 1969.  In answer to an item in the Farm Journal, placed by
        the University of Wisconsin, seeking identical twins, Mr. Werre sold
        the calves for nutritional research on their experimental farm.

FROM STABLE TO STOVE

Before the days when the luxuries of gas heat, fuel oil, electric heat or the
"miracle" of solar panels become a possible reality, the Dakota pioneer had to
rely on the raw materials available to them to supply heat for their homes.
Several forms of this "raw material" were put into practical use.  Following is
a step-by-step account of one generally accepted procedure: The first step,
after the livestock had so generously made their contribution, was to clean it
out of the barns, load it onto some sort of conveyance such as the stone boat in
picture No. 2 and spread it on a level spot to a depth of about eight inches.
It was packed down by keeping cattle on it, by pulling a heavy roller over it,
or by walking horses on it as illustrated in picture No. 3. This was generally
done in the Spring.  After it was packed, it was cut into pieces with a
"cutting tool" such as a spade as shown in picture No. 4, and set on its' side
to dry as in picture No. 5. After it had dried sufficiently, it was stacked as
shown in picture No. 6. Picture No. 7 shows that it has been loaded and is
being transported to be put into neat stocks.  This stacking was usually the
responsibility of one with stacking experience as it had to be done carefully
with a plan toward keeping the stack intact after the "chips" were being
removed for use.  Note in picture No. 8 that the thickness of the pieces has
shrunk to two or three inches and become stove size.  The stocks were usually
kept close to the house for convenience.

These pictures are of Philipp and Marie Lammle as they perform this
painstakingly slow but very necessary task.

CLOSEST NEIGHBORS

Beebe sprung up between Ipswich and Roscoe in the early 1900s.  It acquired its
name from Marcus P. Beebe, longtime banker from Ipswich, owning the land of the
site of Beebe.  Beebe first originated one-fourth mile east of its present
site, moved back the one-fourth mile to its present site.  Beebe consisted of
10 business places at one time.  A Model T dealer, Phil Schneider, selling new
Model T's at $349.00 a piece, shipped into Beebe by railroad, car load lots of
four cars, two T's scheduled for Schneiders' Garage and two T's for Sam Hagen
in Ipswich, pulled by a team of horses from the railroad site to the garage
with no mileage on the new units.  Next was the Modern Woodmen of American
Lodge and Dance Hall, which hosted Lawrence Welk. Then the Lumber Yard owned
and operated by Ruddy Rassmuson, providing supplies for many of the farm homes,
plus the general merchandise store built in 1917, built, owned and operated by
Frank E. Clinton of Aberdeen.  This store was later purchased by Julius F. and
Earl T. Hardt of Chicago.  The Hardt's resided on and built the farm now
operated by Art and Oscar Zambo. One of the highlights of Beebe was when the
store was robbed by the Mackey Bros. of Ipswich with a shoot out involved in
the robbery, wounding J. F. Hardt by Mr. Hardt's hand gun failing, and Earl
Hardt being wounded and saved by the protection of the cash register.  The
store in its later years was torn down by Clarence Zempel.  John Schneider had
a public open air slaughter house, doing custom butchering.

        Beebe, F. E Clinton's Store 1914

The population at one time was 43 people, namely Phil Schneider, Jr. and his
family of four, Henry Allbee, family of 13, J.F. Hardt, family of three, John,
Pete and Philip Schneider, Sr., Schultz family of three, Rassmunson family of
four, Hammond family of five, Tony Sitter and Kimmit family of two.

Beebe experienced a bad fire September 5, 1928 burning out the entire Phil
Schneider, Jr. corner consisting of three big buildings, a garage with three new
Model T's, cream station, two story grocery and hardware store.  The
replacement of this corner was the building of a new garage and purchasing of
the dance hall, pulled by one horse with a capison owned by Larc Bell of Leola,
S.D. for three blocks.  The dance hall was converted into a grocery and bar
business.  Some of the best furs were shipped out of Beebe to New York Markets
by Pete Schneider, a blacksmith and trapper. The livery born housed and
serviced many of the salesmen traveling by horse and buggy owned and operated
by Philip Schneider, Sr.

        Gretna

In 1942, Beebe started to fade away when P. A. Schneider uprooted and moved his
three buildings to Roscoe; then the death of Pete Schneider closed out the
blacksmith and trapper business; the Elevator was moved to Ipswich and last but
not the least the Hardt Store was torn down.

Beebe served as a protection to many in the winter blizzards for people
traveling the Yellowstone Trail.  A new home was built by Robert Hieb long
after there was no more Beebe, only the bare prairie.  This home is called the
Little House on the Prairie.

        Beebe, Schneider's Corner 1930

ROSCOE HERALD Feb. 5, 1891
John Grass and Mad Bear, two noted Sioux Indians from Standing Rock agency and
Louis Primeau, a half breed who is chief of Indian police, the latter
accompanied by his wife, composed a party who changed cars here Saturday
enroute to Washington to consult with powers that be.  These persons with the
exception of Mrs. Primeau were members of a party of seventeen who passed
through here in the fall of 1888 on a mission similar to the present one.
Sitting Bull was a member of the first party.

        Plowing for LeFebvre Land Co.,1910. Henry LeFebvre behind the wheel in
        car, Robert LeFebvre standing on plow on the right.

        Combining wheat on John Beyer's farm

ROSCOE HERALD March 12, 1891
Rumors of another Indian uprising in the spring have begun to be circulated in
the daily papers.  Senator Pettigrew and Representative Gamble have been
interviewed and state that reports coming to the war dept. from the different
agencies give reason for such reports.

ROSCOE HERALD April 16, 1891
Rutherford Proctor shot a white crane Monday.  It measured six feet from tip to
tip of wings.

If anybody wishes to engage the services of a person properly known as a
veterinarian, but commonly called "horse doctor" one of progressive ideas who
belongs to a new school of medicine, we respectfully refer such a one to J.F.
Wake, the hardware dealer.  Mr. Wake sold some gasoline a few days ago to a
person who said it was to be used in killing lice on stock.  Now, Mr. Wake is
the owner of a lousy little Indian pony that is as lazy as its' former
aboriginal owner could possibly be.  Mr. Wake made a practical application of
this new vermin exterminator.  It resulted in awakening more life in the pony
than anyone would have imagined his hide could possibly have held..We haven't
learned what effect the application had on the lice, but that is immaterial.
The value of the experiment has been demonstrated.  With oats selling at fifty
cents a bushel and gasoline for twenty cents a gallon, a little knowledge of
the principles of arithmetic will show that an external application of the one
is cheaper than internal application of the other, and the effect is also more
marked.

November 26, 1891
One of O. B. Cobb's valuable (?) mules died a few days ago.
